SQL and PostgreSQL

32 chapters · hands-on labs

1. The relational model: tables, keys, relations

Covers: relations, primary/candidate/surrogate/natural keys, foreign keys and ON DELETE actions, the missing index on referencing columns, one-to-one / one-to-many / many-to-many, 1NF to 3NF, denormalization. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Constraints" (§5.5, incl. §5.5.4 Primary Keys, §5.5.5 Foreign Keys), "Identity Columns" (§5.3), "CREATE SEQUENCE", "ORDER BY" (§7.5); PostgreSQL source src/backend/utils/adt/ri_triggers.c (REL_18_STABLE). Normal forms follow E. F. Codd's general theory, not a PostgreSQL feature. Checked 2026-09-30.

A relational database keeps data in tables. A table is a set of rows that all have the same columns. A key is a column, or a group of columns, whose values identify one row. A relation between tables is one table that stores the key of a row in another table.

What a table is, and what it is not

In the theory, a table is called a relation. A relation is a set of rows. A set has no order and no duplicates. SQL keeps the first rule and relaxes the second. A table has no built-in row order. If you do not write ORDER BY, PostgreSQL returns the rows in an order that "depends on the scan and join plan types and the order on disk" (docs §7.5). That order can change after an UPDATE, a VACUUM or a new plan. Code that depends on it works in tests and fails later.

SQL does allow duplicate rows in a table with no key. Two identical rows then cannot be told apart. You cannot update or delete one of them by its values alone. This is the first reason for keys.

Keys

A candidate key is a minimal set of columns whose values are unique for every row and never NULL. In customers, both id and email are candidate keys. The primary key is the one candidate key you pick as the main address of the row. Other tables point to it. PostgreSQL makes a primary key column NOT NULL and creates a unique B-tree index on it (§5.5.4). A table can have only one primary key. Other candidate keys get a UNIQUE constraint, like email text NOT NULL UNIQUE.

A natural key is a value that exists in the real world, such as an email or a country code. A surrogate key is a value the database makes up, such as the bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY column in every acmeshop table. An identity column takes its values from a sequence (§5.3).

Surrogate key (id bigint)Natural key (email)
StabilityNever changes. Nothing in the business can make it change.Can change. A customer changes their email. Every foreign key that holds the old value must change too.
Size in other tables and indexes8 bytes, fixed.The length of the string, in every referencing row and in every index on those columns.
Protects against duplicatesNo. Two rows with the same email get two different ids. You still need UNIQUE (email).Yes, by definition.
MeaningNone. Sequence values have gaps. A rolled-back insert does not give its number back ("CREATE SEQUENCE" notes).Readable in logs and joins.

Most production schemas use a surrogate primary key plus a UNIQUE constraint on the natural key. A junction table like order_items often uses a composite natural key instead, PRIMARY KEY (order_id, product_id). That key also states a business rule. One order has at most one row per product, and qty holds the count.

Relations between tables

A foreign key is a column in one table (the referencing table) whose value must match a key of a row in another table (the referenced table). orders.customer_id REFERENCES customers(id) says that every order belongs to a customer that exists. The build-up shows how the acmeshop schema grows, one table at a time.

customersPK id · UNIQUE email
←1 to many
ordersPK id · FK customer_id
↑1 to many
productsPK id · CHECK price >= 0
←1 to many
order_itemsPK (order_id, product_id)
FK order_id · FK product_id
customer_settings (example)PK customer_id, also FK to customers

customers stands alone. It has a surrogate primary key id and a second candidate key email, enforced by UNIQUE.

orders stores customer_id. One customer can have many orders. Each order has exactly one customer. This is one-to-many. The foreign key always lives on the "many" side.

products also stands alone. An order can hold many products and a product can be in many orders. That is many-to-many, and no single foreign key column can hold it.

The junction table order_items turns one many-to-many relation into two one-to-many relations. Each row is one product in one order, with facts that belong to that pair, qty and the price paid.

One-to-one is a foreign key that is also unique. If customer_settings.customer_id is both the primary key and a foreign key to customers, each customer has at most one settings row. Teams split a table this way to keep rarely read or large columns out of the main table.

What a foreign key does at write time

A foreign key is not only documentation. PostgreSQL implements it with internal triggers that run checks on every write (ri_triggers.c).

Insert or update on the referencing side. When you insert an order, PostgreSQL runs a query shaped like SELECT 1 FROM ONLY customers x WHERE id = $1 FOR KEY SHARE OF x. If no row comes back, the statement fails. FOR KEY SHARE locks the customer row so no other transaction can delete it before yours commits (locks are chapter 28). The referenced column always has an index, because a foreign key must point to a primary key, a unique constraint or a non-partial unique index (§5.5.5). So this check is always fast.

Delete or key update on the referenced side. When you delete a customer, PostgreSQL must find orders that still point to it. It runs SELECT 1 FROM ONLY orders x WHERE $1 = customer_id FOR KEY SHARE OF x once for each deleted row. What happens next depends on the ON DELETE action:

ActionWhat happens to referencing rowsUse when
NO ACTION (default)The delete fails if referencing rows still exist when the check runs. The check runs at the end of the statement, or at commit if the constraint is DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED. So other commands in the transaction can fix the problem first.Independent objects. The app must delete children on purpose.
RESTRICTThe delete fails. The check cannot be deferred.You want a hard stop, even inside deferred transactions.
CASCADEReferencing rows are deleted too. Their own foreign keys then apply, so a cascade can travel down several tables, or fail on a table further down.The child is a part of the parent and cannot exist alone, like the order_items of an order.
SET NULLThe referencing columns become NULL. Other constraints still apply. If the column is NOT NULL, the delete fails.The link is optional, for example a product's manager.
SET DEFAULTThe referencing columns get their default value. If that value does not match a row in the referenced table, the delete fails.A "deleted user" placeholder row exists.

Since PostgreSQL 15, SET NULL and SET DEFAULT on delete can take a column list, for example ON DELETE SET NULL (author_id) for a multicolumn key. A NULL in a referencing column skips the check entirely, unless the key is declared MATCH FULL. So declare foreign key columns NOT NULL when the link is required, as acmeshop does.

The index PostgreSQL does not create

A primary key or a unique constraint creates an index automatically. A foreign key does not create an index on the referencing column. The docs give the reason. Not every schema needs it, and there are many ways to index (§5.5.5). The cost is easy to miss. In acmeshop, orders.customer_id has no index unless you add one. With 1,000,000 orders (an example count), each DELETE FROM customers row runs the check query above as a Seq Scan over the whole orders heap. Deleting 1,000 inactive customers means 1,000 full scans. The same missing index makes the common join "all orders of customer 42" read every order.

CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY orders_customer_id_idx ON orders (customer_id);
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY order_items_product_id_idx ON order_items (product_id);
-- order_items(order_id) needs no extra index: it is the leading column of the primary key

A multicolumn index can serve a query on its leading column (chapter 19). That is why order_items.order_id is already covered and product_id is not.

Lab: foreign keys at write time

Pick the ON DELETE action of each foreign key. Then run a statement. Each statement is atomic. If any step fails, nothing changes. Try CASCADE on orders with NO ACTION on order_items. Try SET NULL on the NOT NULL column. The row counts in the cost line are example numbers.

SET DEFAULT in this lab assumes customer_id bigint NOT NULL DEFAULT 0, and no customer 0 exists. The delete then fails on the foreign key check, as the docs say it must.

Normalization in practice

Normalization means you store each fact in one place. Each normal form removes one kind of anomaly. An anomaly is a write that leaves the data in disagreement with itself.

FormRuleBad design on acmeshopAnomaly it removes
1NFEach column holds one value. No lists packed into one column, no repeated column groups like product1, product2.orders.products = 'Keyboard, Mouse'You cannot put a foreign key or an index on one product in the list. "Which orders contain a Mouse?" becomes a string search. Renaming a product means editing strings in many rows.
2NF1NF, and no column depends on only part of a composite key.order_items(order_id, product_id, qty, product_name)product_name depends on product_id alone. A rename must update thousands of rows. If one update misses rows, the same product has two names.
3NF2NF, and no column depends on another non-key column.orders(id, customer_id, customer_email)customer_email depends on customer_id, not on the order. When the customer changes their email, old orders keep the old one, and a query picks whichever row it finds first.

PostgreSQL arrays and jsonb break strict 1NF. That is fine when the app always reads and writes the value as a whole, for example a list of tags. It is a problem when you need a foreign key on each element or a constraint across elements. Then use a child table (chapter 2 covers the types).

Not every copy is a normalization error. order_items.price looks like a copy of products.price, but it is a different fact. It is the price at the time of the order. If you removed it, a later price change would rewrite old invoices.

When teams denormalize

Teams store a derived or copied value on purpose when a read is very frequent and the join or aggregate behind it is expensive. orders.total is an example. It equals the sum of the order's items, so it is derived data. It saves an aggregate over order_items on every order list page. The price is that every write must keep it right. Update the items and the total in the same transaction, or use a trigger. If a code path forgets, the stored total and the real sum differ, and nothing in the database reports it. Other common forms are counters (customers.order_count), a materialized view for reports, and a copy of a few columns into a search table. Each one trades write complexity and a risk of drift for fewer joins at read time. Denormalize after you measure a slow read, not before.

2. Data types, NULL and constraints

Covers: key types (integer, bigint, identity, uuid), numeric vs float, text vs varchar(n), timestamptz vs timestamp, boolean, jsonb vs json, arrays, enum vs text + CHECK; NULL and three-valued logic, IS DISTINCT FROM, COALESCE, NULL in aggregates, UNIQUE, ORDER BY and NOT IN; NOT NULL, CHECK, UNIQUE, PRIMARY KEY, FOREIGN KEY, EXCLUDE, deferrable constraints. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Numeric Types" (§8.1), "Character Types" (§8.3), "Date/Time Types" (§8.5), "Enumerated Types" (§8.7), "UUID Type" (§8.12), "JSON Types" (§8.14), "Identity Columns" (§5.3), "Constraints" (§5.5), "CREATE TABLE", "Comparison Functions and Operators" (§9.2), "Logical Operators" (§9.1), "Conditional Expressions" (§9.18), "UUID Functions" (§9.14), "Aggregate Functions" (§9.21), "Subquery Expressions" (§9.24), "Sorting Rows" (§7.5), "ALTER TYPE", release notes 15 and 18. Checked 2026-09-30.

A data type says what values a column can hold and what the operators on them mean. NULL is a marker for "no value here". A constraint is a rule the database checks on every write, so bad data cannot get in, whatever app or script writes it.

The types production apps use

ChoiceWhat to knowDefault for a new app
integer vs bigint keysinteger is 4 bytes, max 2,147,483,647. bigint is 8 bytes, max about 9.2 × 1018 (§8.1). A busy table can use up 2.1 billion ids. Sequence values are also used up by rolled-back inserts and by INSERT ... ON CONFLICT, so the count of ids used is higher than the row count. Changing the type of a large key column later rewrites the whole table, and the referencing columns in other tables usually need the same change.bigint. The 4 extra bytes per row are cheap.
serial vs identityBoth take values from a sequence. GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY is the SQL standard form. It rejects an explicit value in INSERT unless you write OVERRIDING SYSTEM VALUE (§5.3). BY DEFAULT accepts explicit values, which is useful for data imports. An identity column is NOT NULL but does not guarantee uniqueness by itself. You still need PRIMARY KEY.bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY
uuid keys16 bytes. Any system can make one without asking the database, so ids can be created in clients or across shards. gen_random_uuid() (same as uuidv4()) is fully random. So each new key goes to a random place in the primary key B-tree and touches a random index page (chapter 16). uuidv7(), new in PostgreSQL 18, starts with a millisecond timestamp, so new keys are close together in the index like a sequence. A v7 UUID shows its creation time to anyone who sees it.Use when ids must be created outside the database. Prefer uuidv7().
numeric vs float for moneyreal and double precision are IEEE 754 binary floats. They are "inexact". 0.1 has no exact binary form, so 0.1::float8 + 0.2::float8 = 0.3 is false. numeric stores decimal digits and is exact for add, subtract and multiply. The docs recommend it for money. It is much slower than integer and float math (§8.1).numeric(12,2) for amounts, or bigint cents.
text vs varchar(n)Same storage and speed. varchar(n) only adds a length check (§8.3). char(n) pads with spaces and is usually the slowest. A length limit is a business rule, and changing it later needs an ALTER. A CHECK (length(x) <= n) on text gives the same rule.text, with a CHECK where a limit is real.
timestamptz vs timestampBoth are 8 bytes with 1 microsecond resolution. timestamptz converts the input to UTC using the offset in the string, or the session's TimeZone setting if there is none. It stores UTC, and converts back to the session TimeZone on output. The original zone is not stored (§8.5). timestamp (without time zone) silently ignores any zone in the input. It stores the wall-clock fields as given. Two servers in different zones then write values that cannot be compared.timestamptz for moments in time. Store the user's zone name in a separate column if you need it.
boolean1 byte, with three states (true, false and NULL). Add NOT NULL unless "unknown" is a real state.boolean NOT NULL DEFAULT false
jsonb vs jsonjson stores the exact input text. It keeps whitespace, key order and duplicate keys, and must be parsed again on each use. jsonb stores a parsed binary form. It drops whitespace and key order, keeps only the last duplicate key, and supports GIN indexes (§8.14, chapter 20).jsonb. Use json only to keep the text exactly as received.
arraystext[], bigint[]. Good for small lists read as a whole, such as tags. No foreign key on the elements. Changing one element writes the whole value.A child table when elements need keys or their own rows.
enum vs text + CHECKAn enum value is 4 bytes and sorts in declared order. You can add and rename labels, but you cannot remove one or change the order without dropping the type (§8.7). A value added with ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE inside a transaction cannot be used until that transaction commits. text with CHECK (status IN ('new','paid','shipped','cancelled')) can change in both directions with one constraint swap.Either. text + CHECK is easier to change, as orders.status does.

NULL and three-valued logic

NULL means the value is unknown or missing. It is not zero and not the empty string. Any ordinary comparison with a NULL input returns NULL, which in a boolean context means UNKNOWN. 7 = NULL is UNKNOWN. So is NULL = NULL, because two unknown values might or might not be equal (§9.2). SQL logic therefore has three truth values, TRUE, FALSE and UNKNOWN (§9.1).

aba AND ba OR bNOT a
TRUEUNKNOWNUNKNOWNTRUEFALSE
FALSEUNKNOWNFALSEUNKNOWNTRUE
UNKNOWNUNKNOWNUNKNOWNUNKNOWNUNKNOWN

The rule behind the table is simple. If the answer would be the same for TRUE and for FALSE in place of the UNKNOWN, you get that answer. FALSE AND x is FALSE for any x. Otherwise the answer is UNKNOWN.

What matters is where each clause draws the line. WHERE, ON and HAVING keep a row only when the condition is TRUE. UNKNOWN drops the row, the same as FALSE. CHECK constraints do the opposite. They reject a row only when the condition is FALSE. So CHECK (price >= 0) accepts a NULL price. This is why a CHECK needs a NOT NULL next to it when NULL is not allowed.

Lab: three-valued logic evaluator

Pick values for a row, including NULL. Build a condition. See the value of each part, and what WHERE, CHECK and CASE do with the result. The truth table cell that decides the result is outlined.


 

 




The tools for NULL


x IS NULL and x IS NOT NULL always return TRUE or FALSE. Never write x = NULL. It is UNKNOWN for every row, so WHERE x = NULL returns nothing. (A setting transform_null_equals exists to rewrite it for old apps. Do not depend on it.) a IS DISTINCT FROM b is <> that treats two NULLs as equal and NULL vs a value as different. It is the right test for "did this column change?" in a trigger or an UPDATE ... WHERE. COALESCE(a, b, ...) returns the first argument that is not NULL, for example COALESCE(nickname, name).


NULL in aggregates


count(*) counts rows. count(col) counts rows where col is not NULL. sum, avg, min and max skip NULL inputs. On zero input rows, every aggregate except count returns NULL, not 0 (§9.21). So SELECT sum(total) FROM orders WHERE customer_id = 4 returns NULL for Dara, who has no orders. Write COALESCE(sum(total), 0) when the app expects a number. avg(discount) over a column with NULLs is the average of the known values, not of all rows. Decide which one the report needs.


NULL in UNIQUE


By default a UNIQUE constraint treats NULLs as not equal to each other. So a UNIQUE (phone) column accepts any number of rows with a NULL phone. Usually that is what you want, because many customers have no phone. Since PostgreSQL 15 you can write UNIQUE NULLS NOT DISTINCT (phone). Then NULL counts as one value and a second NULL is a duplicate. For a multicolumn key such as UNIQUE (customer_id, coupon_code), one NULL in any column lets the row through, which can let duplicates in when you did not expect it.



 
Lab: NULL in a UNIQUE column

 
The table has phone text UNIQUE. Insert values and see which ones the constraint rejects.

 

  

 

 




NULL in ORDER BY


PostgreSQL sorts NULL as if it were larger than any value. So the default is NULLS LAST for ASC and NULLS FIRST for DESC (§7.5). A "most recent first" list with ORDER BY shipped_at DESC puts every unshipped order at the top. Write ORDER BY shipped_at DESC NULLS LAST. An index can serve that sort only if it was built with the same NULLS order, or its exact reverse (chapter 19).


The NOT IN trap


x NOT IN (list) means x <> each element, all joined with AND. If the list has a NULL, one of those tests is UNKNOWN. So the result can never be TRUE. It is FALSE when x matches, and UNKNOWN otherwise (§9.24). The query below should return customers with no orders. If any row of the subquery has a NULL customer_id, it returns no rows at all, with no error.


SELECT name FROM customers
WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT customer_id FROM orders);        -- breaks if any customer_id IS NULL

SELECT name FROM customers c
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id);  -- always correct


In acmeshop orders.customer_id is NOT NULL, so the first form works today. It breaks the day someone relaxes that constraint. Use NOT EXISTS (chapter 4 shows it as an anti-join).



Constraints


The app checks input too, but only the database sees every writer. Writers include two app versions during a deploy, a migration script, a person in psql, and two requests at the same moment. A constraint holds for all of them.




ConstraintHow it is enforcedNotes


NOT NULLChecked on each row written.Most columns should have it (§5.5.2). Cheaper than the equal CHECK (x IS NOT NULL).


CHECK (expr)Evaluated on the new or updated row only. Fails only on FALSE.Must use only the row being written. A CHECK that calls a function reading other tables may seem to work, but can break later and can make a dump fail to restore (§5.5.1).


UNIQUEA unique B-tree index is created. The index insert finds duplicates.For "unique among some rows", use a unique partial index instead (chapter 19).


PRIMARY KEYUNIQUE + NOT NULL, with a unique B-tree index.One per table.


FOREIGN KEYInternal triggers run check queries (chapter 1).No automatic index on the referencing columns.


EXCLUDEAn index (usually GiST) finds rows where all listed operators return TRUE against the new row.Generalizes UNIQUE to other operators, for example "no two bookings of the same room overlap".




CREATE EXTENSION btree_gist;   -- lets GiST handle = on bigint
CREATE TABLE room_bookings (
  room_id  bigint NOT NULL,
  during   tstzrange NOT NULL,
  EXCLUDE USING gist (room_id WITH =, during WITH &&)
);


PostgreSQL 18 adds the same rule as key syntax, PRIMARY KEY (room_id, during WITHOUT OVERLAPS), enforced with GiST. It also adds NOT ENFORCED for CHECK and foreign key constraints. Such a constraint is not checked. It only documents a rule that the app keeps.


Deferrable constraints


By default each constraint is checked right after each row or statement. UNIQUE, PRIMARY KEY, EXCLUDE and foreign keys can be declared DEFERRABLE. NOT NULL and CHECK cannot. A DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED constraint is checked at COMMIT. SET CONSTRAINTS ... DEFERRED does the same for one transaction. Use it to insert two rows that reference each other, or to swap two positions under a unique (list_id, position). There is one more detail. A non-deferrable UNIQUE is checked row by row, so UPDATE items SET position = position + 1 can fail halfway on a temporary duplicate. The SQL standard checks at the end of the statement. You get that with DEFERRABLE INITIALLY IMMEDIATE, which the docs warn can be much slower. A deferrable constraint also cannot be the arbiter of INSERT ... ON CONFLICT.









3. How a SELECT is evaluated: the logical order


Covers: the logical order FROM/JOIN, WHERE, GROUP BY, HAVING, window functions, SELECT list, DISTINCT, UNION/INTERSECT/EXCEPT, ORDER BY, LIMIT/OFFSET; aliases in WHERE, GROUP BY and ORDER BY; why WHERE cannot hold aggregates or window functions; logical vs physical order; undefined row order. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "SELECT" (Description and clause sections), "Table Expressions" (§7.2, incl. §7.2.5 Window Function Processing), "Sorting Rows" (§7.5), "LIMIT and OFFSET" (§7.6), synchronize_seqscans (§19.13.1); PostgreSQL source src/backend/parser/parse_agg.c for error texts. Checked 2026-09-30.


A SELECT statement is written in one order and defined in another. The logical order is the order in which the clauses are applied to get the result. First comes FROM, then WHERE, then GROUP BY, and so on, with SELECT near the end. Each clause takes the rows made by the clause before it and gives rows to the next one.



The problem this order solves


SQL says what result you want, not how to get it. So the standard must define the result without talking about indexes or loops. It does this with a simple model. Each clause is a step that turns one set of rows into another set of rows. The written order (SELECT ... FROM ... WHERE) reads like English. The logical order is the real definition. When you know it, most "why is this an error?" questions answer themselves. A name or a value can only be used in a clause that runs after the clause that makes it.




 

  
1. FROM / JOINbuild one row set from all tables

  
↓

  
2. WHEREkeep rows where the condition is TRUE

  
↓

  
3. GROUP BY + aggregatesone row per group; count, sum... computed

  
↓

  
4. HAVINGkeep groups where the condition is TRUE

  
↓

  
5. Window functionsrank(), sum() OVER (...) on the rows left

  
↓

  
6. SELECT list, then DISTINCToutput columns and aliases are born here

  
↓

  
7. UNION / INTERSECT / EXCEPTcombine with other SELECTs

  
↓

  
8. ORDER BYthe only clause that sets row order

  
↓

  
9. LIMIT / OFFSETcut the sorted result

 

 

  
FROM computes every item in the list. Several items are cross-joined. JOIN ... ON combines two items and keeps the pairs that match (chapter 4). A WITH query is computed before this, like a temporary table.

  
WHERE tests each row by itself. A row stays only if the condition is TRUE. FALSE and UNKNOWN both drop it (chapter 2). No groups exist yet, so no aggregate can be used here.

  
GROUP BY folds all rows with the same grouping values into one row. The aggregates are computed here. From now on only grouped columns and aggregates exist. A plain column that is not grouped has many values per group, so the SELECT list cannot use it.

  
HAVING is WHERE for groups. It can test aggregates, because they now exist.

  
Window functions run after grouping, aggregation and HAVING (§7.2.5). They see the rows that are left. If the query groups, they see group rows, so rank() OVER (ORDER BY sum(total) DESC) is valid.

  
The SELECT list computes the output columns and gives them their aliases. Then DISTINCT removes duplicate output rows, or DISTINCT ON keeps the first row of each set.

  
Set operations combine whole results. UNION, INTERSECT and EXCEPT remove duplicates unless you write ALL. UNION ALL does not need that extra duplicate check, so use it when duplicates cannot occur or do not matter.

  
ORDER BY sorts. It runs after SELECT, so it can use output aliases and column numbers. Rows that are equal on every sort key come back in an order the implementation chooses.

  
LIMIT and OFFSET take a slice of the sorted rows. Without ORDER BY, "the first 10 rows" is not defined. OFFSET 10000 must still produce and throw away 10,000 rows.

 

 





What the order explains


A SELECT alias cannot be used in WHERE. The alias is made in step 6. WHERE runs in step 2, before the alias exists. PostgreSQL says so directly:


SELECT id, total * 1.19 AS gross FROM orders WHERE gross > 100;
ERROR:  column "gross" does not exist

SELECT id, total * 1.19 AS gross FROM orders WHERE total * 1.19 > 100;   -- repeat the expression
SELECT id, total * 1.19 AS gross FROM orders ORDER BY gross;              -- fine: ORDER BY runs after SELECT


ORDER BY accepts an output column name or number, but only as a plain name. ORDER BY gross + 1 fails, because inside an expression, ORDER BY reads input columns. If a name is both an input column and an output alias, ORDER BY takes the output column.


PostgreSQL's GROUP BY extension. The docs say that output columns are "nominally computed" after grouping, but PostgreSQL still lets GROUP BY name an output column by alias or by number, as in GROUP BY 1 or GROUP BY country. This is not in the SQL standard. There is one catch. When a name matches both an input column and an output alias, GROUP BY takes the input column. That is the opposite of ORDER BY, and the docs say the difference is kept for standard compatibility.


WHERE cannot use aggregates. WHERE decides which rows go into the groups. An aggregate needs the finished group. The value it would test does not exist yet. The fix is HAVING, or a filter inside the aggregate:


SELECT customer_id FROM orders WHERE count(*) > 5 GROUP BY customer_id;
ERROR:  aggregate functions are not allowed in WHERE

SELECT customer_id FROM orders GROUP BY customer_id HAVING count(*) > 5;
SELECT customer_id, count(*) FILTER (WHERE status = 'paid') FROM orders GROUP BY customer_id;


WHERE cannot use window functions. Window functions run in step 5, after WHERE and HAVING. "Latest order per customer" with WHERE row_number() OVER (...) = 1 fails with window functions are not allowed in WHERE. Compute the window in a subquery or CTE, then filter the outer query (chapters 6 and 8).


SELECT * FROM (
  SELECT o.*, row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY created_at DESC) AS rn
  FROM orders o
) t
WHERE rn = 1;



Logical order is not execution order


The logical order defines what the result is. It does not say how the server gets it. The planner may run a different physical order when the result is the same (chapters 10 and 21). Some common examples:


    

  • A WHERE condition on one table is applied while that table is read, before the join, often through an index. The logical model joins first and filters later, but the result is the same.
    • 

  • With ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 10 and an index on created_at, the server reads the index backwards and stops after 10 rows. It never sorts the whole table.
    • 

  • PostgreSQL evaluates SELECT list expressions after the sort and the LIMIT, when they are not used by DISTINCT, ORDER BY or GROUP BY. So an expensive function in the SELECT list runs only for rows that are returned. This behaviour is guaranteed since PostgreSQL 9.6.
    • 



So never reason about speed from the logical order. Reason about the result from it, and about speed from EXPLAIN (chapter 25).



Row order is not defined without ORDER BY


If there is no ORDER BY, "the rows are returned in whatever order the system finds fastest to produce". The actual order depends on the plan and the order on disk, "but it must not be relied on" (§7.5). A plan change after ANALYZE, a parallel scan, or a synchronized scan that starts mid-table can change it without warning. Two cases catch production code:


    

  • Pagination with LIMIT 20 OFFSET 40 and no ORDER BY, or an ORDER BY on a column that is not unique. Rows can repeat across pages or be skipped. Always add a unique tie-breaker, as in ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC.
    • 

  • A GROUP BY or a window function happens to return sorted rows today, because the plan sorted for it. The docs warn against relying on that. A plan that uses a hash table for grouping returns the groups in no useful order.
    • 





 
Lab: step through one query, clause by clause

 
The sample has 6 customers and 9 orders (example rows). Turn clauses on and off. Then step through the pipeline and watch the row set after each clause. Rows a clause removes are struck through. When GROUP BY is off, the query switches to a per-order version and HAVING is not allowed.

 

  
  
  
  
  
  
 

 

 

 

 

 











4. Joins in SQL


Covers: join as a filtered cross product; INNER, LEFT, RIGHT, FULL, CROSS, self join; ON vs USING vs NATURAL; semi-join (EXISTS, IN) and anti-join (NOT EXISTS, NOT IN with NULL); ON vs WHERE in outer joins; row multiplication with two one-to-many children; joining more than two tables. The join algorithms are chapter 23. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Table Expressions" (§7.2.1.1 Joined Tables, §7.2.2 The WHERE Clause), "SELECT" (FROM Clause), "Subquery Expressions" (§9.24), "Query Planning" (§19.7.4, from_collapse_limit, join_collapse_limit); PostgreSQL source src/backend/commands/explain.c for join node labels, src/backend/optimizer/prep/prepjointree.c (reduce_outer_joins). Checked 2026-09-30.


A join combines rows from two tables into one row when a condition says they belong together. Logically, a join builds every pair of rows (the cross product) and keeps the pairs where the join condition is TRUE. Outer joins then add back the rows that found no partner, with NULL in the other table's columns.



A join is a filtered cross product


customers CROSS JOIN orders pairs each of the 4 sample customers with each of the 5 sample orders. That makes 20 pairs. customers JOIN orders ON o.customer_id = c.id is the same 20 pairs, filtered by the ON condition. The docs say that the JOIN forms "do nothing you couldn't do with plain FROM and WHERE" for inner joins. This is the logical meaning only. No real server builds 1,000,000 × 100,000 pairs for acmeshop. The planner picks a Nested Loop, Hash Join or Merge Join that gives the same rows (chapter 23).


The ON condition follows the three-valued logic from chapter 2. A pair is a match only when ON is TRUE. If o.customer_id is NULL, o.customer_id = c.id is UNKNOWN for every customer. So that order never matches anything.




 

  
CROSS JOINall pairs: 4 × 5 = 20

  
↓ keep pairs where ON is TRUE

  
INNER JOINmatched pairs only

  

   
LEFT JOIN+ unmatched left rows, NULL right

   
RIGHT JOIN+ unmatched right rows, NULL left

  

  
FULL JOIN+ unmatched rows from both sides

  
Semi-join and anti-joinEXISTS / NOT EXISTS: filter left rows, add no columns

 

 

  
Start from every pair. CROSS JOIN returns them all. It is rarely what you want on two real tables. It is useful with a small generated set, such as generate_series of dates crossed with product categories, to build a report grid.

  
JOIN (same as INNER JOIN) keeps only matched pairs. Dara, who has no orders, is not in the result. An order whose customer does not exist is not in it either.

  
LEFT JOIN keeps every left row. A left row with no match appears once, with NULL in all right-side columns. RIGHT JOIN is the mirror. a RIGHT JOIN b gives the same rows as b LEFT JOIN a, and most teams write only LEFT.

  
FULL JOIN keeps unmatched rows from both sides. It is used to compare two sets, for example what is in the warehouse table but not in the orders table, and the other way round.

  
A semi-join asks "does a match exist?" and returns each left row at most once. An anti-join asks "does no match exist?". Neither adds columns from the right side. EXPLAIN shows them as join nodes with Semi or Anti in the name, for example Hash Semi Join and Hash Anti Join.

 

 






 
Lab: join explorer

 
The sample orders here is a staging copy with no foreign key, so it can hold order 105 for customer 9, who does not exist, and a row with a NULL customer. The grid is the cross product. ✓ means ON is TRUE and ? means UNKNOWN. Green result rows are matched pairs. Yellow rows are padded with NULL.

 

  
  
  
  
 

 

 

 

  
Cross product: customers × orders

  
Result

 

 





ON, USING and NATURAL


ON takes any boolean expression. USING (col) is shorthand for equality on columns with the same name in both tables, and it returns that column once instead of twice. NATURAL is USING on every column name the two tables share. The docs call NATURAL "considerably more risky", because a column added later to either table silently joins too. In acmeshop the risk is already there. customers and orders both have id and created_at. So customers NATURAL JOIN orders means ON c.id = o.id AND c.created_at = o.created_at. That returns nonsense or nothing, with no error. Use ON in application code. Use USING only where the names were designed to match.



Self join


A self join is a table joined to itself under two aliases. It answers questions about pairs of rows in one table. "Customers who share a country":


SELECT a.name, b.name, a.country
FROM customers a
JOIN customers b ON b.country = a.country AND a.id < b.id;


a.id < b.id removes each row paired with itself and keeps only one of (Ana, Chen) and (Chen, Ana). Tree data, such as a categories.parent_id column, uses a self join for one level and a recursive CTE for all levels (chapter 8).



Semi-join and anti-join


"Customers who ordered something" looks like a join, but a join returns Ana twice, once per order. A semi-join returns her once and stops at the first match:


SELECT c.* FROM customers c WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id);
SELECT c.* FROM customers c WHERE c.id IN (SELECT customer_id FROM orders);           -- same result


Fixing the join with SELECT DISTINCT gives the same rows, but it first makes all the duplicates and then removes them. It can also merge rows that were really different.


The anti-join, "customers with no orders", has three spellings. They are not the same:




FormWhen the subquery has a NULL customer_id


WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id)Correct. The NULL row never matches, so it does not matter.


LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id WHERE o.id IS NULLCorrect. Test a column that is NOT NULL in the table, such as the primary key, so a NULL can only mean "no match".


WHERE c.id NOT IN (SELECT customer_id FROM orders)Returns no rows. 4 NOT IN (1, 2, NULL) is UNKNOWN, not TRUE (§9.24, chapter 2).





ON vs WHERE in outer joins


For an inner join, a condition in ON and the same condition in WHERE give the same result. For an outer join they do not. The docs explain why. "A restriction placed in the ON clause is processed before the join, while a restriction placed in the WHERE clause is processed after the join."


-- every customer, with their big orders if they have any
SELECT c.name, o.id FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id AND o.total > 100;

-- only customers who have a big order: the LEFT JOIN acts as an inner join
SELECT c.name, o.id FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.total > 100;


In the second query, Dara's padded row has o.total = NULL. NULL > 100 is UNKNOWN, so WHERE drops it. The LEFT JOIN added the row and the WHERE took it out again. Any WHERE test on an outer-side column that is not TRUE for NULL has this effect. The planner sees it too and turns such a LEFT JOIN into an inner join. Try both forms in the lab. If you want the WHERE form but still keep the padded rows, the test must allow NULL, for example WHERE o.id IS NULL OR o.total > 100. That still has a different meaning from the ON version, because a customer whose orders are all small then disappears.



Row multiplication: two one-to-many children


Join a parent to one child table and each parent row repeats once per child row. That is correct. Join it to two child tables through the same parent and each child row is paired with every row of the other child. Take order 101 with 2 rows in order_items and 2 rows in a payments(order_id, amount) table (an example table for this lab). The join gives 2 × 2 = 4 rows. sum(oi.qty * oi.price) counts each item twice. sum(p.amount) counts each payment twice. count(*) says 4. No error appears. The report is simply wrong, and it gets worse as the children grow.



 
Lab: the row multiplication trap

 
Set how many item rows and payment rows order 101 has. Compare the three queries. The item line totals and payment amounts are example values. Two items and two payments have the same amount on purpose.

 

  
  
  
 

 

 

 




The fix is to aggregate each child to one row per parent first, then join the results. Use subqueries, CTEs or LATERAL (chapter 7). sum(DISTINCT ...) is not a fix. It removes equal amounts that are really different rows, such as two items that each cost 25.00.


SELECT o.id, i.items_total, p.paid
FROM orders o
LEFT JOIN (SELECT order_id, sum(qty * price) AS items_total FROM order_items GROUP BY order_id) i ON i.order_id = o.id
LEFT JOIN (SELECT order_id, sum(amount) AS paid FROM payments GROUP BY order_id) p ON p.order_id = o.id
WHERE o.id = 101;



Joining more than two tables


Without parentheses, JOINs nest from left to right. Each JOIN takes the result so far as its left input. For inner joins the order does not change the result. The planner is free to reorder them and search for the cheapest order, up to join_collapse_limit items at a time (default 8, the same as from_collapse_limit). Chapters 21 and 23 cover how it chooses. Outer joins restrict the reordering, because moving them changes the result.


A common bug mixes the two. An inner join after a left join, on the left join's nullable side, removes the padded rows again:


SELECT c.name, o.id, oi.product_id
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o       ON o.customer_id = c.id
JOIN order_items oi      ON oi.order_id = o.id;      -- Dara disappears: o.id is NULL, so ON is UNKNOWN

-- keep her: make the second join LEFT as well
LEFT JOIN order_items oi ON oi.order_id = o.id;


Every table you add is a new chance for row multiplication. Before you aggregate over a multi-table join, check which tables are on the "many" side of the parent. If more than one is, aggregate them first.









5. GROUP BY and aggregates


Covers: grouping, the GROUP BY rule and functional dependency, WHERE vs HAVING, aggregates and NULL, DISTINCT, FILTER and ORDER BY inside aggregates, GROUPING SETS, ROLLUP, CUBE. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "The GROUP BY and HAVING Clauses" (§7.2.3), "GROUPING SETS, CUBE, and ROLLUP" (§7.2.4), "Aggregate Expressions" (§4.2.7), "Aggregate Functions" (§9.21), SELECT reference page ("GROUP BY Clause", "HAVING Clause", "Compatibility: Functional Dependencies"), parser source parse_agg.c for the error text. Checked 2026-09-30.


GROUP BY puts the rows that have equal values in the grouping columns into one group. The query then returns one row per group. An aggregate function such as count, sum or max takes all the rows of one group and gives back one value.



Without grouping, a query returns one output row for each input row. Many questions need a different shape: "how much did each country spend", "which customers have more than two orders". Grouping changes the shape of the result. Many input rows go in, one row per group comes out. This chapter is about what the result means. How PostgreSQL computes it (HashAggregate, GroupAggregate) is chapter 24.



The steps, in logical order


Chapter 3 gave the logical order of a SELECT. Grouping sits in the middle of it. Here is the part that matters for this chapter, with the query we use as the example.


SELECT c.country, count(*) AS orders, sum(o.total) AS spent
FROM customers c JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status <> 'cancelled'
GROUP BY c.country
HAVING sum(o.total) > 100
ORDER BY spent DESC;



 

  
FROM + JOINone row per (customer, order) pair

  
↓

  
WHEREdrops single rows. No aggregates allowed here

  
↓

  
group DE
group US
group FR

  
↓

  
aggregatescount(*), sum(o.total) once per group

  
↓

  
HAVINGdrops whole groups

  
↓

  
SELECT list, then ORDER BYone output row per group that is left

 

 

  
FROM and the join build the input. Each row is one order with the columns of its customer.

  
WHERE looks at one row at a time. It keeps a row only when the condition is true. False and NULL both drop the row. WHERE runs before any group exists, so it cannot use sum() or count().

  
GROUP BY sorts the rows that are left into groups. Two rows go into the same group when they have equal values in every GROUP BY column. The order of the columns in GROUP BY does not change the groups.

  
Each aggregate runs over the rows of one group. After this step the separate rows are gone. Only the group rows are left, each with its grouping values and its aggregate results.

  
HAVING looks at one group row at a time. It can use aggregates, because they exist now. A group that fails the condition is dropped.

  
The SELECT list is computed once per group row. ORDER BY can sort by an aggregate or by its output name (spent).

 

 





Why every selected column must be grouped or aggregated


After grouping, one output row stands for many input rows. A grouping column has one value in the whole group, so the database can show it. A column that is not grouped can have a different value in each row of the group. The database has no rule to pick one, so PostgreSQL refuses the query.


SELECT c.country, o.status, count(*)
FROM customers c JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.country;
ERROR:  column "o.status" must appear in the GROUP BY clause or be used in an aggregate function


There are two correct fixes, and they mean different things. Add o.status to GROUP BY to get one row per (country, status) pair. Or put it in an aggregate, for example string_agg(DISTINCT o.status, ','), to keep one row per country and list the statuses.


There is one exception. If the GROUP BY list contains the primary key of a table, every other column of that table has only one value per group. The primary key decides them. This is called a functional dependency, and PostgreSQL accepts it (since PostgreSQL 9.1):


SELECT c.id, c.name, c.email, count(o.id) AS orders
FROM customers c LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id;          -- c.name and c.email depend on the primary key c.id


PostgreSQL only uses the primary key for this. A UNIQUE NOT NULL column such as email does not count, even though the SQL standard allows more cases. So GROUP BY c.email with c.name in the SELECT list is still an error. MySQL without ONLY_FULL_GROUP_BY accepts any column and returns a value from some row of the group. PostgreSQL never does that.



WHERE vs HAVING


WHERE filters rows before grouping. HAVING filters groups after the aggregates. Put a condition in WHERE when it does not need an aggregate. The result is the same, but WHERE removes the rows early, so fewer rows go into the grouping step, and WHERE can use an index. A condition on a grouping column, for example HAVING c.country = 'DE', is legal. It is better written as WHERE c.country = 'DE'.


The two filters can also give different results. WHERE o.total > 50 ... GROUP BY c.country counts only the large orders of each country. HAVING max(o.total) > 50 keeps whole countries that have at least one large order, and counts all their orders.



Aggregates and NULL


All built-in aggregates skip NULL inputs, with one exception. count(*) counts rows, not values, so it counts every row. This gives the most common NULL surprises:




ExpressionResultWhy


count(*)number of rowscounts rows, NULL or not


count(o.id)number of non-NULL o.idafter a LEFT JOIN, a customer with no orders has one row with o.id NULL. count(*) gives 1, count(o.id) gives 0


count(DISTINCT o.status)number of different non-NULL valuesDISTINCT removes equal values before the count


sum(x) over no rows, or over only NULLsNULL, not 0every aggregate except count returns NULL when it gets no input. Write coalesce(sum(x), 0)


avg(x)sum of non-NULL values / count of non-NULL valuesNULLs do not count as 0. If NULL means 0 in your data, avg(coalesce(x, 0)) gives a different answer


array_agg(x) over no rowsNULL, not {}same rule as sum




In GROUP BY, all NULL values of a column go into one group. Here NULL behaves like a value, not like "unknown". So GROUP BY o.status after a LEFT JOIN gives one group with status NULL for all customers without orders.



One row even when there are no rows


A query with an aggregate but no GROUP BY treats the whole input as one group. It always returns exactly one row, even when the input is empty:


SELECT count(*), sum(total) FROM orders WHERE status = 'lost';
 count | sum
-------+------
     0 | NULL          -- one row, not zero rows

SELECT status, count(*) FROM orders WHERE status = 'lost' GROUP BY status;
(0 rows)             -- no rows means no groups, so no output


This difference matters in application code. The first form always returns a row, so code can read it without a check. The second form can return nothing. A HAVING that fails removes even the single row of the first form.



DISTINCT, FILTER and ORDER BY inside an aggregate


The full aggregate call is name([DISTINCT] args [ORDER BY ...]) [FILTER (WHERE ...)].


    

  • DISTINCT sends each different value to the aggregate once. count(DISTINCT customer_id) counts buyers, not orders. It needs a sort or hash of the values in each group, so it costs more than a plain count.
    • 

  • FILTER (WHERE ...) sends only some rows of the group to this one aggregate. The other aggregates in the same query still see all rows. This replaces the older sum(CASE WHEN ... THEN total END) pattern and gives several counts in one pass:

    SELECT c.country,
       count(*)                                     AS all_orders,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE o.status = 'paid')    AS paid,
       sum(o.total) FILTER (WHERE o.status = 'paid') AS paid_total
FROM customers c JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.country;
    • 

  • ORDER BY inside the call sets the order in which rows reach the aggregate. sum and max do not care. string_agg, array_agg and jsonb_agg do. Without it the order is not defined, and it can change when the plan changes. The ORDER BY goes after all arguments: string_agg(status, ',' ORDER BY created_at). The form string_agg(status ORDER BY created_at, ',') is also valid syntax but means something else. It is a call with one argument and two sort keys. No string_agg takes one argument, so it fails.
    • 

  • WITHIN GROUP is for ordered-set aggregates, where the order is part of the math. percentile_cont(0.5) WITHIN GROUP (ORDER BY total) is the median order total.
    • 




GROUPING SETS, ROLLUP and CUBE


A report often needs totals at several levels: per country and status, per country, and a grand total. You could write three queries joined with UNION ALL. GROUPING SETS does it in one query, and PostgreSQL reads the input only once for it. Each set in the list groups the same input separately. A column that is not in the current set is NULL in the output rows of that set.




WrittenSame as GROUPING SETS


ROLLUP (country, status)((country, status), (country), ()). Totals down a hierarchy


CUBE (country, status)((country, status), (country), (status), ()). Every combination, 2n sets


GROUPING SETS ((country), ())exactly the sets you list. () is the grand total




The added NULLs cause a problem. If the data itself has NULLs, a NULL in the output can mean "this is a subtotal" or "the value was NULL". The GROUPING(country, status) function tells them apart. It returns a bit mask, with a 1 bit for each argument that is not part of the current set. GROUPING(country, status) = 3 marks the grand total row.




 
Lab: watch rows go into groups

 
The input is customers c LEFT JOIN orders o on a small sample: 9 rows, and Dara has no orders, so her order columns are NULL. Pick the grouping, the aggregate and the filters, then step through. Try count(*) against count(o.id) with GROUP BY c.id. Try the WHERE that matches no row, with and without GROUP BY. Try ROLLUP and look for two kinds of NULL.

 

  
  
 

 

  
  
 

 

 

 

 





Common mistakes


    

  • COUNT after a LEFT JOIN. count(*) gives 1 for a customer with no orders. Count a column of the right table: count(o.id).
    • 

  • Sums inflated by a join. Join orders to order_items and then sum(o.total): each order total is added once per item line. Aggregate each table in its own subquery first, then join the results (chapter 7), or sum the items instead.
    • 

  • Aggregate in WHERE. WHERE count(*) > 2 fails with "aggregate functions are not allowed in WHERE". Use HAVING.
    • 

  • Expecting 0 from sum. Use coalesce(sum(x), 0) when the application needs a number.
    • 

  • Relying on order. GROUP BY does not sort the output. A plan with HashAggregate returns groups in hash order. Add ORDER BY.
    • 

  • "Latest order per customer" with GROUP BY. max(created_at) gives the latest date, but not the other columns of that order. Use DISTINCT ON, a window function (chapter 6) or LATERAL (chapter 7).
    • 










6. Window functions


Covers: window vs GROUP BY, PARTITION BY, ORDER BY in OVER, frames (ROWS, RANGE, GROUPS, the default frame), ranking functions, lag/lead, running totals, moving averages, top-N per group, place in the logical order, WindowAgg. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Window Functions" tutorial (§3.5), "Moving-Aggregate Mode" (§36.12.1), "Window Function Calls" (§4.2.8), "Window Functions" (§9.22, Table 9.67), "Window Function Processing" (§7.2.5), SELECT reference page ("WINDOW Clause", "DISTINCT Clause"), release notes 15.0 and 18.0, source explain.c ("Run Condition"). Checked 2026-09-30.


A window function computes a value for each row from a set of related rows, called the row's window. Unlike GROUP BY, it does not merge rows. Every input row stays in the output and gets one extra column.



Chapter 5 showed that GROUP BY gives one row per group. Often you need the detail row and a value about its group in the same output row. Examples are each order with the customer's running total, each order with its rank inside the customer, and each day with the change from the day before. Before window functions this needed a self-join or a correlated subquery (chapter 7), which is slow and hard to read. A window function does it in one pass over sorted rows.


SELECT id, customer_id, created_at, total,
       sum(total) OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY created_at) AS running_total
FROM orders;



The three parts of OVER



 

  
input rowsafter FROM, WHERE, GROUP BY, HAVING

  
↓ PARTITION BY customer_id

  
partition Ana
partition Ben
partition Chen

  
↓ ORDER BY created_at, inside each partition

  
sorted partitionrows with equal ORDER BY values are peers

  
↓ frame for the current row

  
framea slice of the partition that moves with the current row

  
↓

  
same row + one new column

 

 

  
Window functions see the rows that are left after grouping and HAVING. If the query has GROUP BY, the window sees group rows, not table rows.

  
PARTITION BY splits the rows into partitions, like GROUP BY but without merging them. The function never looks across a partition border. Without PARTITION BY the whole result is one partition.

  
ORDER BY inside OVER sets the order of rows in each partition. It is not the order of the output. Rows that sort as equal are called peers. Without ORDER BY, all rows of the partition are peers and their order is not defined.

  
The frame is the set of rows that an aggregate such as sum uses for the current row. It is given in rows, ranges of values, or peer groups. Ranking functions and lag/lead ignore the frame and use the whole partition.

  
The function writes one value into the current row. Then the next row becomes the current row and its frame moves.

 

 





Kinds of window functions




FunctionValue for the current rowUses the frame?


row_number()1, 2, 3 ... inside the partition. Peers get different numbers in an order that is not definedno


rank()row_number of the first peer. Peers share a rank and leave a gap: 1, 1, 3no


dense_rank()number of the peer group: 1, 1, 2. No gapsno


lag(x, n, default) / lead(x, n, default)x from the row n rows before / after, in the partition. NULL (or default) past the edgeno


first_value(x), last_value(x), nth_value(x, n)x from the first / last / nth row of the frameyes


any aggregate: sum, avg, count, max...the aggregate over the rows of the frameyes




With ORDER BY total DESC, two orders of 60.00 are peers. row_number gives them 1 and 2 in an order you cannot predict. rank gives both 1 and the next row 3. dense_rank gives both 1 and the next row 2. For "top 3 orders" this decides whether ties can give you 4 rows (rank, dense_rank) or exactly 3 (row_number). If you use row_number and need a stable result, add a column that makes the order unique: ORDER BY total DESC, id.



The frame, and the default that surprises people


A frame has a mode and two ends: {ROWS | RANGE | GROUPS} BETWEEN start AND end.


    

  • ROWS counts physical rows. ROWS BETWEEN 2 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW is always at most 3 rows.
    • 

  • RANGE works on values of the ORDER BY column. CURRENT ROW means "the current row and all its peers". With an offset, RANGE BETWEEN '7 days' PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW means all rows whose created_at is at most 7 days before. An offset needs exactly one ORDER BY column.
    • 

  • GROUPS counts peer groups. GROUPS BETWEEN 1 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW is the previous peer group plus the current one. It needs an ORDER BY.
    • 



If you write no frame, the default is RANGE BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW. With ORDER BY this is "from the start of the partition to the last peer of the current row". Without ORDER BY every row is a peer, so the frame is the whole partition. This default causes two common bugs:


    

  • last_value returns the current row. The frame ends at the current row (or its last peer), so the last row of the frame is the current row. To get the last row of the partition, write ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND UNBOUNDED FOLLOWING, or use first_value with the opposite ORDER BY.
    • 

  • Running totals jump on ties. sum(total) OVER (ORDER BY created_at) gives two orders with the same created_at the same running total, and that total already includes both. Because RANGE includes all peers. If you want the total to grow one row at a time, write ROWS BETWEEN UNBOUNDED PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW and make the order unique.
    • 



Rows near the edge of a partition have a smaller frame. A 3-row moving average has only 1 row for the first row of a partition. That is the correct math for the frame, but a chart can show a false spike at the start. There is also EXCLUDE CURRENT ROW, EXCLUDE GROUP and EXCLUDE TIES to remove rows from the frame, for example to compare a row with the average of the others.




 
Lab: partitions, order and frames

 
Eight sample orders. Click a row to make it the current row. Its partition is shown, the rows of its frame are green, and the current row is blue. Try last_value with the default frame. Try sum ordered by created_at without a partition and look at the two orders of 2026-09-03. Try rank against dense_rank ordered by total DESC.

 

  
  
 

 

  
  
 

 

 

 





Common patterns


-- change from the previous order of the same customer
SELECT id, customer_id, total,
       total - lag(total) OVER w AS diff
FROM orders
WINDOW w AS (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY created_at, id);

-- 7-row moving average per product
SELECT order_id, product_id, price,
       avg(price) OVER (PARTITION BY product_id ORDER BY order_id
                        ROWS BETWEEN 6 PRECEDING AND CURRENT ROW) AS avg7
FROM order_items;

-- share of the customer's total
SELECT id, total, total / sum(total) OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id) AS share
FROM orders;


The WINDOW clause names a window so several functions can share it. Functions with the same PARTITION BY and ORDER BY are guaranteed to see the rows in the same order, even when the order has ties, and they share one sort.



Where windows run, and why you need a subquery to filter on them


In the logical order (chapter 3), window functions run after FROM, WHERE, GROUP BY and HAVING, and before DISTINCT, ORDER BY and LIMIT. So WHERE cannot see a window value. It does not exist yet. WHERE rank() OVER (...) <= 3 fails with "window functions are not allowed in WHERE". Compute the value in a subquery or CTE, then filter it outside:


-- top 3 orders per customer
SELECT *
FROM (SELECT o.*, row_number() OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id
                                     ORDER BY total DESC, id) AS rn
      FROM orders o) t
WHERE rn <= 3;


The same order has useful effects. A window can use an aggregate, because grouping is already done: rank() OVER (ORDER BY sum(total) DESC) with GROUP BY customer_id ranks customers by spend. And DISTINCT runs after windows, so SELECT DISTINCT does not reduce the rows a window function sees.


The subquery form reads and sorts every row of orders even when you want only 3 per customer. Two other forms can be faster:


    

  • DISTINCT ON for top 1: SELECT DISTINCT ON (customer_id) * FROM orders ORDER BY customer_id, total DESC. One sort, keep the first row of each customer. It is PostgreSQL syntax, not standard SQL.
    • 

  • LATERAL (chapter 7) for top N when there are few groups and an index on (customer_id, total DESC): for each customer, read only the first N index entries. This beats a full sort when each group has many rows.
    • 




How PostgreSQL runs it: the WindowAgg node


The plan node is WindowAgg. It needs its input sorted by the PARTITION BY columns and then the ORDER BY columns. The planner puts a Sort under it, or uses an index that gives that order. A query with two different window definitions gets two WindowAgg nodes with a Sort between them.


EXPLAIN SELECT id, sum(total) OVER (PARTITION BY customer_id ORDER BY created_at)
FROM orders;
 WindowAgg
   ->  Sort
         Sort Key: customer_id, created_at
         ->  Seq Scan on orders


WindowAgg keeps the rows of the current partition in a temporary store, in memory up to work_mem and then in a temporary file. Since PostgreSQL 18, EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows which one was used, and how much, on a Storage: line of the WindowAgg node. For a frame that grows with the current row, it adds each new row to the running aggregate state. For a frame whose start moves, it can remove the rows that leave the frame from the state, if the aggregate has an inverse transition function (the docs call this moving-aggregate mode). count and sum over integer or numeric have one. sum over float8 does not, because subtraction would not give back the exact old value. Without it, PostgreSQL recomputes the aggregate over the whole frame each time the frame start moves. The cost is then rows × frame length instead of rows.


Since PostgreSQL 15 the planner can stop a WindowAgg early. When the outer query filters with rn <= 3 on row_number(), rank(), dense_rank() or count(), EXPLAIN shows a Run Condition on the WindowAgg. Once the condition turns false in a partition, the node stops computing the window functions for the rest of that partition and skips its rows. Without PARTITION BY it stops at once. The Sort below it still reads every row. Because WindowAgg needs sorted input, the output often comes out in that order. The docs say not to rely on it. Add an outer ORDER BY.









7. Subqueries: scalar, correlated, LATERAL


Covers: scalar, row and table subqueries, IN, EXISTS, ANY/SOME, ALL, NOT IN and NULL, correlated vs uncorrelated, InitPlan, SubPlan, hashed SubPlan, pull-up to Semi Join and Anti Join, flattening of FROM subqueries and what blocks it, scalar subquery vs JOIN + GROUP BY, LATERAL. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Scalar Subqueries" (§4.2.11), "Subqueries" and "LATERAL Subqueries" (§7.2.1.3, §7.2.1.5), "Subquery Expressions" (§9.24), "Using EXPLAIN" (§14.1), "Constraints" (§5.5.5, foreign keys and indexes), from_collapse_limit (§19.7.4); release notes 17.0; planner source src/backend/optimizer/README, plan/subselect.c (make_subplan, build_subplan, convert_ANY_sublink_to_join, convert_EXISTS_sublink_to_join, simplify_EXISTS_query), prep/prepjointree.c (pull_up_sublinks, is_simple_subquery), path/allpaths.c (subquery_is_pushdown_safe), executor/nodeSubplan.c. Checked 2026-09-30.


A subquery is a SELECT written inside another statement, in parentheses. It is correlated when it uses a column of the outer query. Then its meaning is "run it again for each outer row". The planner often finds a faster way to get the same result.



Kinds of subquery, by shape




KindReturnsExampleRules


scalarone row, one columnSELECT name, (SELECT max(total) FROM orders) FROM customersMore than one row is an error at run time: "more than one row returned by a subquery used as an expression". Zero rows gives NULL, not an error.


rowone row, several columnsWHERE (customer_id, status) = (SELECT id, 'paid' FROM ...)Same one-row rule. Compared column by column.


table (derived table)any rows and columnsFROM (SELECT customer_id, sum(total) s FROM orders GROUP BY 1) tUsed in FROM like a table. PostgreSQL 16 and later do not need an alias. Earlier versions required one.




The zero-row rule hides bugs. A scalar subquery that finds nothing gives NULL, and NULL can quietly remove a row in a later WHERE. The more-than-one-row rule is checked only when it happens. A query can run for months and then fail on the day a second matching row appears.



Kinds of subquery, by use




FormTrue whenFalse whenNULL when


EXISTS (sub)sub returns at least one rowsub returns no rowsnever


x IN (sub) = x = ANY (sub)some row equals xno row equals x and no row is NULL (or sub is empty)no row equals x and some row is NULL, or x is NULL


x op ANY (sub), SOMEx op row is true for some rowit is false for every row (or sub is empty)no true result and some NULL result


x op ALL (sub)true for every row (or sub is empty)false for some rowno false result and some NULL result


x NOT IN (sub) = x <> ALL (sub)no row equals x and no row is NULLsome row equals xno row equals x and some row is NULL




EXISTS only asks "is there a row?", so the select list inside it does not matter. The usual style is EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM ...). PostgreSQL stops the subquery as soon as it finds one row.



The NOT IN trap


Look at the last row of the table. If the subquery returns even one NULL, x NOT IN (sub) can never be true. 3 NOT IN (1, 2, NULL) means 3 <> 1 AND 3 <> 2 AND 3 <> NULL, which is true AND true AND NULL, which is NULL. WHERE drops the row. So the query returns zero rows, and there is no error. Take "customers who were never referred", with a nullable referred_by column:


SELECT id FROM customers
WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT referred_by FROM customers);        -- 0 rows if any referred_by is NULL

SELECT id FROM customers c
WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM customers r WHERE r.referred_by = c.id);   -- correct


NOT EXISTS has no NULL problem, because a NULL in r.referred_by = c.id is just "no match". It is also faster. The planner turns NOT EXISTS into an Anti Join, as the next sections show. It never does that for NOT IN.



Uncorrelated and correlated




Uncorrelated. It does not use the outer query. Its result is the same for every outer row.

SELECT * FROM orders
WHERE total > (SELECT avg(total) FROM orders);


Correlated. It uses c.id from the outer query. The result changes per outer row.

SELECT c.name,
  (SELECT count(*) FROM orders o
   WHERE o.customer_id = c.id) AS n
FROM customers c;




The SQL meaning of a correlated subquery is "for each outer row, put its values in, run the subquery, use the result". That is the meaning, not the plan. PostgreSQL has four ways to run a subquery, and it tries them in a fixed order.



How PostgreSQL really runs a subquery



 

  
parsersubquery in an expression becomes a SubLink node

  
↓ planner step 1: pull_up_sublinks

  
IN / EXISTS → Semi Join · NOT EXISTS → Anti Joinsubquery becomes a normal join input

  
↓ planner step 2: pull_up_subqueries

  
simple FROM subqueries are flattenedtheir tables join the parent's FROM list

  
↓ what is left: SS_process_sublinks → make_subplan

  
InitPlanuncorrelated, one value, runs once

  
SubPlancorrelated, runs once per outer row

  
hashed SubPlanuncorrelated IN / NOT IN, runs once, then hash probes

 

 

  
The parser keeps each subquery used in an expression (IN, EXISTS, a scalar subquery) as a SubLink. The planner decides how to run each one.

  
If the SubLink is an IN, = ANY or EXISTS at the top level of WHERE (joined by AND, not under OR), the planner turns it into a semi join: "keep the outer row if at least one inner row matches, output it once". NOT EXISTS becomes an anti join: "keep the outer row if no inner row matches". Now the join planner (chapter 23) can pick Hash, Merge or Nested Loop and the join order. This is called pull-up.

  
A simple subquery in FROM is merged into the parent. Its tables and WHERE conditions become part of the parent query, so outer conditions can use its indexes. The next table lists what stops this.

  
A SubLink that is not pulled up becomes a separate plan. If it does not use outer columns and gives one value (a scalar subquery, or an EXISTS without outer columns), it becomes an InitPlan. It runs once, the first time its value is needed. The value is saved and reused for every row.

  
A correlated SubLink that was not pulled up becomes a SubPlan. The executor runs it again for each outer row, with the new outer values. EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows this as loops= equal to the number of outer rows.

  
An uncorrelated IN or NOT IN that stays a SubPlan can be hashed. The executor runs the subquery once, puts its rows in a hash table in memory, and checks each outer row against it. This needs a hashable = operator, and the planner must expect the table to fit in hash_mem (work_mem times hash_mem_multiplier). If not, it is a plain SubPlan that reads the saved subquery result again for each outer row.

 

 





Here is what each looks like in EXPLAIN. Costs and times are removed. Since PostgreSQL 17, EXPLAIN writes the output of a sub-plan as (InitPlan 1).col1 or (SubPlan 1).col1. Older versions wrote $0, $1. The examples assume an index orders_customer_id_idx on orders(customer_id). A foreign key does not create that index for you.




InitPlan (uncorrelated scalar)

SELECT * FROM orders
WHERE total > (SELECT avg(total) FROM orders);

 Seq Scan on orders
   Filter: (total > (InitPlan 1).col1)
   InitPlan 1
     ->  Aggregate
           ->  Seq Scan on orders orders_1


SubPlan (correlated scalar), with EXPLAIN ANALYZE

SELECT c.name, (SELECT count(*) FROM orders o
                WHERE o.customer_id = c.id)
FROM customers c;

 Seq Scan on customers c (rows=100000.00 loops=1)
   SubPlan 1
     ->  Aggregate (rows=1.00 loops=100000)
           ->  Index Only Scan using orders_customer_id_idx
                 on orders o (rows=10.00 loops=100000)
                 Index Cond: (customer_id = c.id)


Semi Join (IN or EXISTS pulled up)

SELECT * FROM customers c
WHERE EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o
              WHERE o.customer_id = c.id);

 Hash Semi Join
   Hash Cond: (c.id = o.customer_id)
   ->  Seq Scan on customers c
   ->  Hash
         ->  Seq Scan on orders o


hashed SubPlan (NOT IN)

SELECT * FROM customers c
WHERE c.id NOT IN (SELECT customer_id FROM orders);

 Seq Scan on customers c
   Filter: (NOT (ANY (id = (hashed SubPlan 1).col1)))
   SubPlan 1
     ->  Seq Scan on orders




The Semi Join plan is one possible plan. The planner picks the join method, and which side to hash, by cost. It can also turn a semi join into a normal join over unique inner values. NOT EXISTS gives the same shapes with Anti Join. The number to read in EXPLAIN ANALYZE is loops. The SubPlan above runs 100,000 times (example row counts). Each run is cheap, but 100,000 index lookups add up. Without the index, each run is a full Seq Scan on orders, so 100,000 × 1,000,000 rows are read. That is the classic slow correlated subquery.



When pull-up and flattening fail


For IN and EXISTS the planner needs to know that turning the subquery into a join does not change the result. The source code lists the cases it refuses:




CaseResultWhy


IN / EXISTS under OR, or inside a larger expressionSubPlanonly a SubLink at the top level of an AND list can become a join


NOT IN(hashed) SubPlan, never Anti JoinNULL makes NOT IN differ from an anti join, as shown above


EXISTS without outer columnsInitPlanit is not a join, it is one true/false value


EXISTS whose subquery has aggregates, GROUP BY, HAVING, window functions, set operations, OFFSET, or LIMIT other than a positive constantSubPlanEXISTS (... LIMIT 1) is accepted, because a positive LIMIT cannot change "is there a row"


correlated INSemi Join since PostgreSQL 17, SubPlan beforesince 17 the pulled-up subquery is treated as LATERAL


volatile function in the IN test or the EXISTS WHERESubPlana join could call it a different number of times




For a subquery in FROM, flattening (the planner calls it pull-up too) needs a "simple" subquery. It is blocked by: aggregates, GROUP BY, HAVING, grouping sets, window functions, DISTINCT, ORDER BY, LIMIT, OFFSET, set-returning functions in the select list, set operations (except a simple UNION ALL, which has its own path), FOR UPDATE, a WITH clause inside it, volatile functions in its select list, and security barrier views. Flattening also stops when the merged FROM list would have more than from_collapse_limit items (default 8).


A subquery that is not flattened is planned on its own and read through a Subquery Scan. The planner can still push outer WHERE conditions into it in many cases. For example, a condition on a GROUP BY column goes into the subquery. LIMIT and OFFSET stop this, because a filter inside a LIMIT changes which rows the LIMIT keeps. That is why OFFSET 0 is the classic hand-written optimization fence. It has no effect on the result, but the planner will neither flatten the subquery nor push conditions into it. People use it to force a plan shape. Chapter 8 shows the modern way, WITH ... AS MATERIALIZED.



Scalar subquery in SELECT vs LEFT JOIN + GROUP BY




-- A: correlated scalar subquery
SELECT c.id, c.name,
  (SELECT count(*) FROM orders o
   WHERE o.customer_id = c.id) AS n
FROM customers c
ORDER BY c.id LIMIT 20;


-- B: join, then group
SELECT c.id, c.name, count(o.id) AS n
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
GROUP BY c.id
ORDER BY c.id LIMIT 20;




Both give the same rows. PostgreSQL does not turn a scalar subquery in the select list into a join, so A is always a SubPlan. Which form is faster depends on how many outer rows you need:


    

  • Few outer rows (a page of 20, one customer): A wins when orders(customer_id) has an index. The select list is computed after LIMIT (see the SELECT reference page), so A does 20 small index lookups. B may have to join and group many orders before the LIMIT can act.
    • 

  • All outer rows (a report): B wins. It reads orders once and groups with a hash (chapter 24). A does one index lookup per customer, which is slower, and without the index it reads orders once per customer.
    • 

  • With several counts (orders, items, reviews) B joins all of them at once. The joined rows multiply, and the counts come out wrong (chapter 5). Use one scalar subquery or one LATERAL per count, or group each table in its own derived table and then join.
    • 




LATERAL


A normal subquery in FROM cannot see the other FROM items. Each one is computed on its own. With LATERAL in front, a FROM subquery can use columns of the FROM items to its left. For each row of the left side, PostgreSQL runs the subquery with that row's values and joins its result rows to that row. It is a correlated subquery that can return many rows and many columns. A scalar subquery can do neither.


-- last 3 orders of each German customer
SELECT c.name, o.id, o.total, o.created_at
FROM customers c
LEFT JOIN LATERAL (
    SELECT id, total, created_at
    FROM orders
    WHERE customer_id = c.id
    ORDER BY created_at DESC
    LIMIT 3
) o ON true
WHERE c.country = 'DE';


LEFT JOIN LATERAL ... ON true keeps customers with no orders. CROSS JOIN LATERAL (or a comma) drops them. The subquery has ORDER BY and LIMIT, so it cannot be flattened. The plan is a Nested Loop with the subquery on the inner side and loops= equal to the number of customers. With an index on orders(customer_id, created_at DESC), each loop reads only 3 index entries. That is the fast top-N-per-group plan when the groups are large. A window function with row_number() (chapter 6) reads and sorts every order. It wins when you need most of the rows anyway, or when there is no such index.




 
Lab: how many times does the inner query run?

 
Pick a query form. Every form answers a question about customers and their orders. Step through the 4 sample customers (Ana, Ben and Chen have orders, Dara has none) to see what the executor does for each outer row. Then change the number of customers to see the totals at a larger size. Orders are 8 rows in the sample and 1,000,000 at the larger sizes, with about 10 per customer (example numbers). Plans are the typical shape. The planner picks by cost.

 

  
  
 

 

  
  
 

 

 

 

 











8. CTEs and recursive queries


Covers: WITH as a named subquery, materialization and inlining (before and since PostgreSQL 12), MATERIALIZED / NOT MATERIALIZED, CTE Scan, recursive CTEs (working table, intermediate table, UNION vs UNION ALL), SEARCH and CYCLE, infinite recursion and LIMIT, data-modifying CTEs and their snapshot. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "WITH Queries (Common Table Expressions)" (§7.8.1 to §7.8.4, including "Recursive Query Evaluation", "Search Order", "Cycle Detection", "Common Table Expression Materialization", "Data-Modifying Statements in WITH"), SELECT reference page ("WITH Clause", "Compatibility"), release notes 12.0 and 14.0, planner source plan/subselect.c (SS_process_ctes), parser source parse_cte.c for the error texts. Checked 2026-09-30.


A CTE (common table expression) is a subquery with a name, written in a WITH clause before the main statement. The main statement can use the name like a table. A recursive CTE can use its own name, so it can follow links in a tree or graph to any depth.



WITH customer_spend AS (
    SELECT customer_id, sum(total) AS spent
    FROM orders
    WHERE status <> 'cancelled'
    GROUP BY customer_id
)
SELECT c.name, s.spent
FROM customer_spend s JOIN customers c ON c.id = s.customer_id
WHERE s.spent > 1000;


This is the same as a derived table in FROM (chapter 7). The gain is that the steps read from top to bottom, and the main query can use one CTE many times. A later CTE can use an earlier one in the same WITH. The name exists only for this one statement.



Materialized or inlined


There are two ways to run a CTE. Materialized. Run the CTE once, keep its rows in a temporary store (in memory up to work_mem, then in a temporary file), and let every reference read that store. Inlined. Paste the CTE into the main query as if it were a subquery in FROM, then plan the whole thing together (chapter 7 flattening).


Up to PostgreSQL 11, every CTE was materialized. The planner planned it on its own and could not push the main query's WHERE into it. People used CTEs on purpose as an optimization fence, and also got slow queries by accident. Since PostgreSQL 12 the rule is:




CTEDefault since PostgreSQL 12


non-recursive, plain SELECT without volatile functions, referenced onceinlined


same, but referenced two or more timesmaterialized (computed once, read by each reference)


recursive, or INSERT / UPDATE / DELETE / MERGE, or contains a volatile function such as random()always materialized. NOT MATERIALIZED is ignored




You can override the default: WITH x AS MATERIALIZED (...) forces a separate computation, WITH x AS NOT MATERIALIZED (...) forces inlining. Both are PostgreSQL extensions to the SQL standard.



 

  
WITH paid AS (SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'paid')
SELECT * FROM paid WHERE customer_id = 2

  

   
inlinedone scan of orders with both conditions
↓
Index Scan on orderscustomer_id = 2, filter status = 'paid'

   
materializedCTE computed first, without the outer filter
↓
all paid orders in a temporary store
↓
CTE Scan on paidFilter: customer_id = 2

  

 

 

  
The CTE selects all paid orders. The main query wants the paid orders of one customer.

  
Inlined is the default here since PostgreSQL 12. The planner merges the two levels. Both conditions reach the scan of orders, so an index on customer_id can find the few rows at once. This is called predicate pushdown.
