Materialized is what PostgreSQL 11 does, or AS MATERIALIZED. The CTE runs as written and stores every paid order. The customer_id = 2 filter is applied later, row by row, in the CTE Scan node. The store has no index.

 

 




Materializing wins when the CTE is expensive and used several times, or when it calls an expensive function. The docs give this case:


WITH w AS (
    SELECT key, very_expensive_function(val) AS f FROM some_table
)
SELECT * FROM w AS w1 JOIN w AS w2 ON w1.f = w2.f;


Materialized, the function runs once per row. Inlined, it runs once per row for each of the two references. Inlining wins when each reference needs only a few rows of a large CTE, so the filters and indexes of the main query can reach the base table. The opposite case also exists: AS MATERIALIZED is the modern, readable way to build a fence when the planner picks a bad plan for the merged query, in place of the old OFFSET 0 trick.


In EXPLAIN, a materialized CTE shows as a CTE name sub-plan under the top node and a CTE Scan on name node where it is read. Since PostgreSQL 18, EXPLAIN ANALYZE also shows the memory or disk the CTE's store used. An inlined CTE leaves no trace. The plan is the same as if you had written the subquery by hand.


A materialized SELECT CTE is also read lazily. PostgreSQL computes its rows only as far as the main query asks for them. If the main query stops early, for example because of a LIMIT, the rest of the CTE never runs.




 
Lab: where does the filter run?

 
The sample has 8 orders, 4 of them paid. Change the keyword, the version and the number of references. Watch where customer_id = 2 is applied, and how many rows the CTE produces before the filter. Assume an index on orders(customer_id).

 

  
  
  
 

 

 

 





Recursive CTEs


SQL has no loop. A recursive CTE adds one, for data where the depth is not known ahead: a category tree, an employee and manager chain, a graph of links. To show it we add a small table to the shop:


CREATE TABLE categories (
  id         int PRIMARY KEY,
  parent_id  int REFERENCES categories(id),   -- NULL for a top-level category
  name       text NOT NULL
);
INSERT INTO categories VALUES
  (1, NULL, 'Electronics'), (2, 1, 'Computers'), (3, 2, 'Laptops'), (4, 2, 'Desktops'),
  (5, 1, 'Phones'), (6, 5, 'Cases'), (7, NULL, 'Books'), (8, 7, 'Novels');


To find Electronics and everything under it, write a non-recursive term, then UNION ALL (or UNION), then a recursive term that joins the table to the CTE's own name:


WITH RECURSIVE sub(id, name, depth) AS (
    SELECT id, name, 0 FROM categories WHERE id = 1          -- non-recursive term
  UNION ALL
    SELECT c.id, c.name, s.depth + 1                          -- recursive term
    FROM categories c JOIN sub s ON c.parent_id = s.id
)
SELECT * FROM sub;


The word "recursive" is misleading. PostgreSQL runs it as a loop, with two temporary tables. This is the algorithm from the docs:



 

  
evaluate the non-recursive termfor UNION, remove duplicates

  
↓ rows go to the result and to the working table

  
result
working table

  
↓ recursive term, with the self-reference reading only the working table

  
intermediate tablefor UNION, drop rows already in the result

  
↓ append to the result, then working := intermediate, clear intermediate

  
working table empty? stop

 

 

  
Run the non-recursive term once. Here it gives the row for Electronics, depth 0.

  
Put these rows in the result and in the working table. The working table holds only the rows found in the last round.

  
Run the recursive term. The name sub inside it means the working table, not the whole result so far. So each round only looks for children of the rows found in the round before. The new rows go into the intermediate table. With UNION, rows equal to a row already in the result (or in this round) are dropped here.

  
Append the intermediate rows to the result. Replace the working table with the intermediate table. Empty the intermediate table.

  
Repeat from the recursive term while the working table has rows. When a round finds nothing new, the loop stops. If every round finds something, it never stops.

 

 




Because each round reads only the rows of the last round, the result is built level by level: first depth 0, then all depth 1, then all depth 2. That is breadth-first order. The docs call this an implementation detail. If you need an order, compute it and ORDER BY it. SEARCH DEPTH FIRST BY id SET ord or SEARCH BREADTH FIRST BY id SET ord (since PostgreSQL 14) adds a column for that. The recursive term has limits. The self-reference may appear only once, it must be in the recursive term, and ORDER BY inside the recursive query gives "ORDER BY in a recursive query is not implemented".



Cycles and queries that never end


If the data has a loop, for example Electronics is set as a child of Cases, the recursive term always finds a "new" row. With UNION ALL the working table never becomes empty. The query runs until you cancel it or statement_timeout stops it. Four defences, from weakest to strongest:


    

  • UNION instead of UNION ALL. It drops rows equal to a row already in the result. This only helps if the repeated row is really equal. Here the depth column is different on each lap, so UNION does not stop it. It also keeps every result row in a hash table to check for duplicates.
    • 

  • A depth limit: WHERE s.depth < 20 in the recursive term. It always stops, but it returns wrong rows (each lap of the loop again) until the limit is reached.
    • 

  • A path array. Carry ARRAY[id] along, add each new id, and stop when the new id is already in the path: WHERE NOT c.id = ANY(s.path).
    • 

  • The CYCLE clause (since PostgreSQL 14) writes the path array for you:

    WITH RECURSIVE sub(id, name, depth) AS ( ... same as above ... )
  CYCLE id SET is_cycle USING path
SELECT * FROM sub WHERE NOT is_cycle;
    
It adds two columns. path is the list of ids so far. is_cycle is true for a row whose id is already in its path. Such a row is output once, marked, and not followed further.
    • 



For testing, a LIMIT in the main query also stops a runaway CTE: SELECT * FROM sub LIMIT 100. It works because PostgreSQL computes CTE rows only as far as they are fetched. The docs advise against it in production. Other databases differ, and it fails as soon as the main query sorts or joins the CTE, because then it reads all of it.




 
Lab: run a recursive CTE round by round

 
Step through the query above. Watch the working table (the rows the next round starts from), the intermediate table (the rows this round found) and the result. Then add the cycle and try each fix. The lab stops a runaway query after 12 rounds. PostgreSQL would not stop.

 

  
 

 

  
  
 

 

 

 

  
working table

  
intermediate table

  
result so far

 





Data-modifying CTEs


A CTE can be an INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE or MERGE. With RETURNING, its changed rows become a table that the rest of the statement can read. The classic use is to move rows in one statement:


WITH moved AS (
    DELETE FROM orders
    WHERE status = 'cancelled' AND created_at < now() - interval '2 years'
    RETURNING *
)
INSERT INTO orders_archive
SELECT * FROM moved;


(This assumes the cancelled orders have no order_items rows. Otherwise the foreign key stops the DELETE.) One statement means one transaction step. Either both the DELETE and the INSERT happen, or neither does. No other session can see the rows in both tables or in neither. Rules to know:


    

  • One snapshot. All sub-statements and the main statement run with the same snapshot (chapter 12). They cannot see each other's changes to the tables. WITH t AS (UPDATE products SET price = price * 1.05 RETURNING *) SELECT * FROM products returns the old prices. Read t to see the new ones. RETURNING is the only way to pass changed rows between the parts.
    • 

  • Always run, exactly once, to the end, even if nothing reads their output. That differs from a SELECT CTE, which runs only as far as it is read.
    • 

  • No order between the parts. The sub-statements run at the same time as each other and as the main statement. Do not change the same row in two parts. Only one change takes effect, and you cannot reliably predict which.
    • 

  • Top level only. A data-modifying CTE must be attached to the top-level statement, not to a subquery ("WITH clause containing a data-modifying statement must be at the top level"). It cannot be recursive, but it can use the result of a recursive SELECT CTE, for example to delete a whole subtree of categories.
    • 



For large moves, one statement also means one long transaction. It holds its locks until it ends, and while it runs, VACUUM (chapter 13) cannot remove the old versions of rows that other sessions update or delete in the meantime. Move millions of rows in batches, for example with WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM orders WHERE ... LIMIT 10000) in the DELETE, and run it until it moves nothing.








9. Inside the server: processes and memory


Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "How Connections Are Established" (§51.2), "Resource Consumption" (§19.4), "Connections and Authentication" (§19.3), "Write Ahead Log" (§19.5), "Vacuuming" (§19.10), "Managing Kernel Resources" (§18.4), "Glossary" (Appendix M), "pg_stat_activity" (§27.2), PostgreSQL 18 release notes (asynchronous I/O). Checked 2026-09-30.


A running PostgreSQL server is a group of operating system processes that share one area of memory. One supervisor process, the postmaster, starts all the others. Each client connection gets its own server process, called a backend. Background processes do the work that no single client owns, such as writing pages to disk and cleaning up dead tuples.


The docs call this the "process per user" model. It answers three questions. How do many clients run queries at the same time? The operating system runs their backends in parallel on all CPU cores. How do they see the same data? All processes map the same shared memory, which holds the page cache and the lock table. How does one bad query not break the others? Each backend has its own private memory, so a big sort in one session does not overwrite anything in another session.



The parts, one at a time



 

  
psql / appclient process
→TCP 5432

  
postmasterlistens, forks, supervises

  
↓ fork()

  

   
backendserves client 1

   
backendserves client 2

   
backendserves client N

  

  
private memory per backendwork_mem per sort/hash node, temp_buffers, catalog cache, plan cache

  
shared memory (one copy for the whole instance)
   

    
shared bufferspage cache, default 128MB

    
WAL buffersdefault 1/32 of shared buffers

    
lock tableheavyweight locks

    
commit log cachepg_xact status of each XID

   

  

  

   
background writer
checkpointer
WAL writer

  

  

   
autovacuum launcher
→
autovacuum workersmax 3 at once

  

  

   
WAL archiverif archive_mode
WAL summarizerif summarize_wal
logical replication launcher

  

  
I/O workersio_method = worker, 3 by default

  
↓ read() / write() / fsync()

  
OS page cache
→
data files and WAL files on disk

 

 

  
The postmaster is the first process of an instance. It reads the configuration, creates the shared memory, starts the background processes and listens on the TCP port (5432 by default). It does not run queries itself.

  
A client connects. The postmaster accepts the socket and creates a child process for it (on Linux and other Unix systems with fork()). The child authenticates the client and then serves only that one connection until it closes. This child is the backend.

  
Every new connection gets a new backend. 100 connections means 100 backend processes. max_connections (default 100) is the limit, and 3 of those slots are kept for superusers (superuser_reserved_connections).

  
Each backend has private memory that no other process can see. Sorts and hash tables use it, up to work_mem per node. It also holds caches of catalog rows and of prepared plans. These caches fill as the session touches more tables, so a long-lived backend is larger than a new one.

  
All processes attach to one shared memory area. Shared buffers are the page cache (chapter 14). WAL buffers hold WAL records before they are written (chapter 15). The lock table holds table-level locks for all sessions (chapter 28). The commit log cache holds the committed or aborted status of recent transactions, which every visibility check needs (chapter 12).

  
Three processes move data from memory to disk. The background writer writes some dirty pages ahead of time, so backends seldom have to write a page themselves before they can reuse a buffer. The checkpointer runs checkpoints (every 5 minutes by default, or earlier when WAL grows). The WAL writer flushes WAL buffers to disk every 200 ms by default.

  
The autovacuum launcher visits each database about once per autovacuum_naptime (default 1 minute) and starts autovacuum workers there. The workers run VACUUM and ANALYZE on tables that changed enough (chapters 13 and 21). At most autovacuum_max_workers (default 3) run at once.

  
Optional processes. The WAL archiver copies finished WAL files to backup storage (chapter 30). The WAL summarizer, added in PostgreSQL 17, records which pages each stretch of WAL changed, so an incremental backup can copy only those. The logical replication launcher starts workers for logical replication subscriptions.

  
PostgreSQL 18 adds asynchronous I/O. A backend can queue several reads and continue, instead of waiting for each read. With the default io_method = worker, a pool of I/O worker processes (io_workers, default 3) does the reads. On Linux builds with liburing, io_method = io_uring uses the kernel's io_uring interface instead. io_method = sync gives the old behaviour.

  
Below PostgreSQL is the operating system. PostgreSQL reads and writes files through the OS, so a page can be in shared buffers, in the OS page cache, in both, or only on disk. This is why the docs advise about 25% of RAM for shared buffers and not most of it.

 

 





You can see these processes on a live server. ps shows names such as postgres: checkpointer and postgres: app acmeshop 192.0.2.10(51234) idle. Inside SQL, pg_stat_activity has one row per process with a backend_type column, for example client backend, autovacuum worker, checkpointer, walwriter, parallel worker and so on.


SELECT pid, backend_type, state, query
FROM pg_stat_activity
ORDER BY backend_type;



The background processes




ProcessJobWhen it runsMain settings


postmasterStarts the instance, forks backends and background processes, restarts them after a crashAlwaysport, max_connections


background writerWrites dirty pages from shared buffers in small rounds, so free buffers are readyAlways, wakes every bgwriter_delaybgwriter_delay, bgwriter_lru_maxpages


checkpointerWrites all dirty pages and records a checkpoint, so crash recovery can start from thereEvery checkpoint_timeout (5min) or when WAL reaches max_wal_size (1GB)checkpoint_timeout, max_wal_size


WAL writerWrites and flushes WAL buffers to the WAL filesEvery wal_writer_delay (200ms)wal_writer_delay


autovacuum launcher and workersFind tables that need VACUUM or ANALYZE and run itLauncher always (unless autovacuum is off), workers on demandautovacuum_naptime (1min), autovacuum_max_workers (3)

startup process, WAL receiver, WAL senderReplay WAL, receive it, send itDuring crash recovery, on a standby, for replicationchapter 15 and 30




If one backend crashes (for example, it is killed by the Linux out-of-memory killer), the postmaster cannot know if that process left shared memory in a broken state. So it ends all other backends, resets shared memory and runs crash recovery from the WAL. This is the default (restart_after_crash = on). Every client sees its connection drop, not only the one that crashed. This is the main cost of shared memory between processes, and it is a reason to keep memory use per backend under control.



Shared memory and private memory


The most important difference is who pays. Shared memory is allocated once at server start and has a fixed size. Private memory is allocated by each backend when it needs it, and the total grows with the number of busy connections.




AreaWhereDefaultNotes


shared_buffersshared128MB (initdb may pick less)The docs suggest about 25% of RAM on a dedicated server with 1GB or more. More than 40% is unlikely to help, because the OS cache also holds data pages. Server start only.


wal_buffersshared-1 = 1/32 of shared_buffers, at least 64kB, at most one WAL segment (16MB)With the default 128MB shared buffers this is 4MB. Written out at every commit, so large values seldom help.


lock tablesharedroom for max_locks_per_transaction (64) × (max_connections + max_prepared_transactions) objectsCounts locked objects such as tables, not rows. Row locks are stored in the tuples (chapter 28).


commit log cache (transaction_buffers)shared0 = shared_buffers/512, between 16 and 1024 pagesCaches pg_xact, 2 bits of status per XID.


work_memprivate, per sort or hash node4MBHash nodes may use work_mem × hash_mem_multiplier (2.0), so 8MB. Above the limit, the node writes temporary files.


maintenance_work_memprivate, per operation64MBVACUUM, CREATE INDEX, ALTER TABLE ADD FOREIGN KEY. Each autovacuum worker can use this much (or autovacuum_work_mem).


temp_buffersprivate, per session8MBPage cache for temporary tables only. Only used if the session touches temporary tables.


catalog cache, relation cache, plan cacheprivateno fixed limitCopies of catalog rows, table descriptions and prepared plans. They grow with the number of tables and statements the session has used.





Why work_mem is a trap


The name suggests "memory per query" or "memory per connection". It is neither. The docs say that work_mem is the limit for one sort or hash operation. A single query can have several such nodes at once. A Hash Join, a HashAggregate and a Sort in one plan is common (chapter 22). Each node can use its full limit. Each parallel worker is a separate process with its own limit. And every busy connection can do the same at the same time.


So the worst case is roughly shared memory + connections × nodes per query × work_mem × (1 + parallel workers), plus maintenance memory. Raising work_mem from 4MB to 256MB "because one report is slow" can push this over the RAM of the machine. The Linux out-of-memory killer then kills a backend, and as described above, all connections drop. The safe pattern is a moderate global value and a larger value only where it is needed, for example SET work_mem = '256MB' in the report session or ALTER ROLE reporting SET work_mem = '256MB'.




 
Lab: memory budget for acmeshop

 
Change the settings and watch the worst-case total. Compare the "per connection" guess with the real rule (per node, per process). The server size is an example. The defaults are PostgreSQL 18 defaults.

 

  

   

   

   

   

  

  

   

   

   

   

  

 

 

 

 




The lab shows a worst case. Real usage is lower. Most connections are idle most of the time, and a node only takes memory up to what it needs. But the worst case is what happens during a traffic spike, and it is what the out-of-memory killer sees. The lab also leaves out the fixed cost of each backend (its code, stack and caches), which is the subject of the next point.



Why a process per connection is costly


A new connection is not free. The postmaster must fork a process, the backend must authenticate the client (with TLS, a key exchange too) and then load catalog data into its caches on first use. That is fine for a connection that lives for hours. It is slow for an app that opens a connection per HTTP request.


Many open connections also cost when they are idle. Each backend holds a slot in max_connections, private memory for its caches, and an entry in the shared arrays that every snapshot must scan (chapter 12). The docs note that PostgreSQL sizes some shared memory directly from max_connections. More backends than CPU cores also means more context switches and more contention on shared locks. The usual answer is a connection pooler such as PgBouncer in front of PostgreSQL, so that thousands of client connections share a few dozen backends (chapter 29).


Setting max_connections to 2000 to fix "too many clients" errors treats the symptom. Each of those 2000 backends can use work_mem for each node of its query. Fix the number of connections with a pool, not with a bigger limit.








10. The path of a query: parse, rewrite, plan, execute


Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Overview of PostgreSQL Internals" (§51.1 The Path of a Query, §51.3 The Parser Stage, §51.4 The Rule System, §51.5 Planner/Optimizer, §51.6 Executor), "The Query Tree" (§39.1), "Views and the Rule System" (§39.2), "Frontend/Backend Protocol" (§54.1, §54.2 Message Flow), "PREPARE", "Query Planning" (§19.7, plan_cache_mode); source src/backend/tcop/postgres.c, src/backend/utils/cache/plancache.c, src/backend/rewrite/rewriteHandler.c (REL_18_STABLE). Checked 2026-09-30.


A query arrives at its backend as plain text. The backend turns it into rows in five stages. The parser checks the syntax, the analyzer finds the tables and columns, the rewriter expands views, the planner picks a way to run it, and the executor runs it. Each stage takes a tree and gives the next stage a new tree.


Why five stages and not one? Each stage needs different information and fails in a different way. The parser needs only the SQL grammar. The analyzer needs the system catalogs, so it needs a transaction. The planner needs statistics. The executor needs the data. Keeping them apart also lets PostgreSQL save the output of the early stages and reuse it, which is what a prepared statement does.



This chapter follows one query on the acmeshop database through every stage:


SELECT name, email
FROM customers
WHERE country = 'DE'
LIMIT 10;




 

  
applicationsends SQL text over the connection

  
↓ Query message (or Parse / Bind / Execute)

  
parserlexer + grammar
→raw parse tree

  
analyzerparse analysis
⇄
system catalogspg_class, pg_attribute, pg_type, pg_proc, pg_operator

  
↓ Query tree

  
rewriterrules, views, row security
⇄
pg_rewrite, pg_policy

  
↓ Query tree (rewritten)

  
plannerpaths, costs, cheapest
⇄
pg_statistic, pg_class

  
↓ plan tree

  
executorpulls tuples through the plan
⇄
shared buffers, heap, indexes

  
↓ RowDescription, DataRow × n, CommandComplete, ReadyForQuery

  
application gets the rows

 

 

  
The client sends the SQL text in a protocol message. There is no parsing on the client side. The backend that owns this connection (chapter 9) does all the work below.

  
The parser checks the text against the SQL grammar and builds a raw parse tree. It knows only syntax. It does not know if a table called customers exists.

  
The analyzer looks up every name in the system catalogs. It finds the table, the column numbers and types, and the operator that = means for two text values. The result is a Query tree.

  
The rewriter applies rules stored in the catalogs. A reference to a view is replaced by the view's defining query. Row-level security policies are added here as extra conditions. The output is still a Query tree.

  
The planner builds the possible ways to run the query, estimates a cost for each, and keeps the cheapest. It turns that into a plan tree of plan nodes (chapter 21).

  
The executor calls the top plan node again and again. Each call returns one tuple, which the node gets by calling its own children (chapter 22).

  
Each result row goes to the client as a DataRow message, as soon as it is produced. CommandComplete says the statement ended. ReadyForQuery says the backend is idle again.

 

 





Step 0: the connection and the protocol


The client and backend talk with the PostgreSQL frontend/backend protocol. There are two ways to send a query.


The simple query protocol sends one Query message with the whole SQL text. The text can hold several statements separated by semicolons. The backend runs all five stages for each statement and sends back the results. Values are part of the text, so they are always sent as text.


The extended query protocol splits the work into messages. This is what most drivers use (JDBC, the Go and Rust drivers, and libpq's PQexecParams):


Parse    name="" query="SELECT name, email FROM customers WHERE country = $1 LIMIT 10"
         backend: parser + analyzer + rewriter run now. Result kept as a prepared statement.
Bind     statement="" portal="" params=['DE']
         backend: the planner runs now (or reuses a cached plan). Result kept as a portal.
Describe portal=""       backend: RowDescription (column names and types)
Execute  portal="" max_rows=0   backend: the executor runs. DataRow, DataRow, ... CommandComplete
Sync                    backend: commits the implicit transaction, sends ReadyForQuery


The mapping of stages to messages comes from the backend source (exec_parse_message, exec_bind_message in postgres.c). It has three results that matter in practice.


    

  • The parameter 'DE' never goes through the parser. It arrives as a separate value in Bind. So a value cannot change the structure of the SQL, which is why parameters stop SQL injection.
    • 

  • A Parse message can hold only one statement. More than one gives a syntax error.
    • 

  • A named prepared statement lives until the session ends. Its parse and analysis are done once and reused for each Bind.
    • 




Stage 1: the parser


The parser has two parts, built with the classic Unix tools. The lexer (scan.l, built with flex) cuts the text into tokens, such as keywords, identifiers, constants and operators. For our query that is SELECT, name, ,, email, FROM, customers, WHERE, country, =, 'DE', LIMIT, 10. Unquoted identifiers are folded to lower case here, which is why Customers and customers are the same table but "Customers" is not. The grammar (gram.y, built with bison) matches the tokens against the rules of SQL and builds a raw parse tree:


SelectStmt
  targetList:  ResTarget(ColumnRef "name"), ResTarget(ColumnRef "email")
  fromClause:  RangeVar "customers"
  whereClause: A_Expr "=" (ColumnRef "country", A_Const 'DE')
  limitCount:  A_Const 10


Every name in this tree is only a string. The parser does no catalog lookups on purpose. The docs give the reason. Catalog lookups can only be done inside a transaction, and the backend does not want to start a transaction for every string it receives. The raw parse tree is enough to see that a statement is BEGIN, COMMIT or ROLLBACK, and to run those without more analysis. Only when the statement is a real query does the backend start a transaction and go on.


A typo in a keyword stops the query here, but the error does not always point at the typo. SELECT name FORM customers gives ERROR: syntax error at or near "customers" (SQLSTATE 42601). FORM is not a keyword, so the grammar reads it as a column alias, the same as name AS form. The next token, customers, is the first one that no grammar rule accepts. With the simple query protocol, the whole text is parsed before any statement in it runs, so a syntax error in the third statement stops the first two as well.



Stage 2: the analyzer


The docs call this stage the "transformation process". It walks the raw parse tree and asks the catalogs what each name means.


    

  • customers: search each schema in search_path in pg_class. Found as a table. The analyzer also takes an AccessShareLock on it, so no one can drop it while the query runs.
    • 

  • name, email, country: look up in pg_attribute for that table. They are columns 3, 2 and 4, all of type text.
    • 

  • = between text and an untyped literal: the literal is given the type text, and the operator text = text is found in pg_operator.
    • 

  • A FuncCall such as sum(total) becomes an Aggref (aggregate) or a FuncExpr (normal function), because only the catalog knows which one sum is.
    • 



The result is a Query tree. Names are replaced by numbers. There is a range table entry for the table, and there are Var nodes that say "range table entry 1, column 4".


Query  commandType=SELECT
  rtable:     [1] RTE_RELATION customers
  jointree:   FromExpr from=[1]
              quals: OpExpr text = text (Var 1.4 country, Const 'DE'::text)
  targetList: [1] Var 1.3 name  text
              [2] Var 1.2 email text
  limitCount: Const 10::bigint


Errors from this stage mean "the syntax is fine but something does not exist or does not fit". Examples are relation "custmers" does not exist (42P01), column "nme" does not exist (42703), operator does not exist: text = integer (42883) if you write country = 1, and invalid input syntax for type numeric (22P02) for a literal that cannot be converted.



Stage 3: the rewriter


The rewriter applies the rule system. Its input and output are both Query trees. The docs describe it as a kind of macro expansion. Its main job today is views. In PostgreSQL, a view is stored as an empty relation plus an ON SELECT DO INSTEAD rule in pg_rewrite that holds the view's query.


CREATE VIEW paid_orders AS
  SELECT id, customer_id, total FROM orders WHERE status = 'paid';

SELECT customer_id, total FROM paid_orders WHERE total > 100;


After analysis the range table has one entry, paid_orders. The rewriter finds the rule and replaces that entry with a subquery that holds the view's Query tree:


SELECT customer_id, total
FROM (SELECT id, customer_id, total FROM orders WHERE status = 'paid') paid_orders
WHERE total > 100;


The planner then usually "pulls up" this simple subquery into the outer query, so the final plan is one scan of orders with both conditions. A view costs nothing extra at run time in this case. A view with security_barrier, or one with aggregates or LIMIT, cannot always be flattened like this.


Row-level security is also added in the rewriter. For each table with RLS turned on, the rewriter reads the policies from pg_policy and attaches their conditions to that table's range table entry as security conditions. So a policy such as USING (customer_id = current_setting('app.customer_id')::bigint) becomes part of the query before the planner sees it, and no plan can skip it.



Stage 4: the planner


The planner takes the rewritten Query tree and asks which of all the ways to get this result is the cheapest. For each table it builds paths. A sequential scan is always possible, and each index whose column matches a condition or the ORDER BY gives another. For joins it tries join orders and join methods. It estimates a cost for each path from statistics, keeps the cheapest, and turns it into a full plan tree. Chapter 21 explains the estimates. For our query on the acmeshop example data, with no index on country, the plan is:


EXPLAIN SELECT name, email FROM customers WHERE country = 'DE' LIMIT 10;

 Limit
   ->  Seq Scan on customers
         Filter: (country = 'DE'::text)


(Cost numbers are left out here. Chapter 25 shows how to read them.) The planner also does some work that looks like execution. It folds constant expressions. For example, WHERE total > 10 * 10 becomes total > 100 once, at plan time. So SELECT 1/0 fails with division by zero in the planner, before any row is read.



Stage 5: the executor


The executor builds a state for each plan node and then calls the top node until it returns nothing. Here, Limit asks Seq Scan for a tuple. Seq Scan reads the next tuple from the heap, checks the filter, and returns it if it passes. After ten tuples, Limit stops asking, and the scan stops too, even if the table has 100,000 rows. Chapter 22 shows this pull model in detail.


Errors that depend on the data happen here, in the middle of the run. Examples are division by zero (22012) when a row with a zero divisor is reached, a failed cast of a stored value, or a unique violation in an INSERT. Some rows may already have been sent to the client. The statement still fails as a whole, and the transaction is aborted (chapter 27).



Which stage gave the error?




StageExampleErrorSQLSTATE


parserSELECT name FORM customerssyntax error at or near "customers"42601


analyzerSELECT nme FROM customerscolumn "nme" does not exist42703


analyzerSELECT * FROM custmersrelation "custmers" does not exist42P01


analyzerWHERE country = 1operator does not exist: text = integer42883


plannerSELECT 1/0 FROM customersdivision by zero (constant folding)22012


executorSELECT 100 / price FROM products with a free productdivision by zero, when that row is reached22012





Utility statements skip the planner


CREATE TABLE, ALTER TABLE, VACUUM, SET, BEGIN and other DDL and control statements are utility statements. They are parsed, but there is nothing to plan, because there is only one way to create a table. The backend wraps them in a plan with no plan nodes (the source comment says "Utility commands require no planning") and runs them directly in ProcessUtility. Some utility statements contain a real query, for example CREATE TABLE ... AS SELECT and EXPLAIN. That inner query goes through all the stages.




 
Lab: step a query through the stages

 
Pick a query or edit the text, then press Next stage. The lab has a small lexer, a small grammar (SELECT list FROM table [WHERE ... AND ...] [LIMIT n]), a catalog with the acmeshop tables and the paid_orders view, and a few sample rows. Watch which stage stops the bad queries. Try your own typos and SELECT 1/0 FROM customers.

 

 

 

 

 

 





Prepared statements and the plan cache


A prepared statement saves the output of the parser, analyzer and rewriter, so the next executions skip those stages. In SQL it looks like this (the protocol Parse message does the same):


PREPARE by_status(text) AS
  SELECT id, total FROM orders WHERE status = $1;

EXECUTE by_status('new');


Planning is the interesting part. PostgreSQL can use two kinds of plan:


    

  • A custom plan is made for each execution, with the real parameter values. The planner knows that status = 'new' matches few rows and can pick an index.
    • 

  • A generic plan is made once, with $1 unknown. It saves planning time, but the planner must guess the selectivity of status = $1 without the value. It uses an average based on the number of distinct values in the column.
    • 



With the default plan_cache_mode = auto, the rule in the docs and in plancache.c is as follows. The first five executions use custom plans, and PostgreSQL records their average estimated cost (including an estimate of the planning cost). Then it builds the generic plan once. From then on it uses the generic plan if the generic plan's estimated cost is lower than that average. If not, it keeps making custom plans.


The failure mode is skewed data. Say (example numbers) that in acmeshop 85% of orders are shipped and 0.5% are new, and there is an index on status. A job calls by_status('shipped') five times. Each custom plan is a Seq Scan. The generic plan (for an average value, about a quarter of the table) is also a Seq Scan with a similar cost, so PostgreSQL switches to it. Now a user request calls by_status('new'). It gets the cached Seq Scan over 1,000,000 rows, not the index scan it would get with a custom plan. EXPLAIN EXECUTE by_status('new') shows it. A generic plan contains $1 where a custom plan shows 'new'.


There are two fixes. Set plan_cache_mode = force_custom_plan for the session, role or function that sees skewed values, or split the query so rare and common values use different statements. force_generic_plan is the opposite lever, for a very hot query whose plan does not depend on the values.


A cached plan is not kept forever. PostgreSQL invalidates it and plans again when a table in it changes (DDL), when its statistics are updated by ANALYZE, or when search_path changes. Prepared statements belong to one session, so they are lost when the connection closes. This matters with a transaction-mode connection pooler (chapter 29).









11. Heap storage: files, pages, tuples, TOAST


Covers: files and forks, the 8 kB page, line pointers, the tuple header, TID and ctid, fillfactor and the free space map, how a read finds a row, TOAST. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Database File Layout" (§66.1), "TOAST" (§66.2), "Free Space Map" (§66.3), "Visibility Map" (§66.4), "Database Page Layout" (§66.6), CREATE TABLE (fillfactor, toast_tuple_target), CLUSTER, "Client Connection Defaults" (default_toast_compression); source src/include/access/heaptoast.h (REL_18_STABLE). Checked 2026-09-30.


The heap is the main storage of a table. It is a file of 8 kB pages. Each page holds a few tuples. A tuple is one stored version of a row. When a row changes, PostgreSQL writes a new tuple, so one row can have several tuples at the same time (chapter 12). Values that are too large for a page go to a separate TOAST table.



Files on disk


All data of one server lives in one directory, the data directory (often called PGDATA). Each database has a subdirectory base/<database oid>. Inside it, each table and each index is a separate file. The file name is the filenode number from pg_class.relfilenode. The filenode is often equal to the table's OID, but commands that rewrite the table (TRUNCATE, CLUSTER, VACUUM FULL, some ALTER TABLE forms) give it a new filenode. So do not assume that the two numbers are the same.


acmeshop=# SELECT pg_relation_filepath('orders');
 pg_relation_filepath
----------------------
 base/16384/16402            -- example numbers


A file never grows past 1 GB. When the table is larger, PostgreSQL adds segments: 16402, 16402.1, 16402.2 and so on. (1 GB is the default. You can change it only when you compile PostgreSQL.) Next to the main file, each table has two more files, called forks:




FileForkWhat it holds


16402, 16402.1, ...mainThe pages with the tuples.


16402_fsmfree space mapOne byte per heap page that tells roughly how much free space the page has. The bytes are kept in a small tree, so a search for "a page with at least N bytes free" is fast. Indexes have one too (except hash indexes).


16402_vmvisibility mapTwo bits per heap page: all-visible (no tuple on the page needs vacuum) and all-frozen. Only tables have it. VACUUM sets the bits. Any change to the page clears them. Chapter 13 shows what it gives.


16402_initinitOnly for unlogged tables and indexes.




The orders table in this book has 1,000,000 rows (an example number). Its heap is one file with some thousands of pages. Its primary key index orders_pkey is a different file, with its own pages.



The 8 kB page


The page is the unit of storage and of I/O. PostgreSQL never reads or writes less than one page. The same layout is used by tables, indexes, sequences and TOAST tables. A page has five parts:



 

  

   
PageHeaderData24 bytes

   
line pointers4 bytes each, grow →

   
free spacepd_lower to pd_upper

   
tuples← grow from the end

   
special spaceindexes only

  

  
INSERT: one new line pointer at pd_lower, one new tuple below pd_upper, free space shrinks from both sides

 

 

  
The page starts with a 24-byte header. pd_lsn (8 bytes) is the WAL position of the last change to the page. The page must not reach disk before the WAL up to that position (chapter 15). pd_checksum holds a checksum. Since PostgreSQL 18, initdb turns data checksums on by default. pd_lower, pd_upper and pd_special are 2-byte offsets from the page start. pd_prune_xid is a hint that the page may have tuples that pruning can remove.

  
Line pointers come after the header. Each line pointer is 4 bytes. It holds the offset of a tuple in the page, its length and a state (normal, dead, unused, redirect). The tuples themselves are put at the end of the page and grow toward the front. pd_lower points to the end of the line pointer array. pd_upper points to the start of the last tuple added.

  
The free space is the gap between pd_lower and pd_upper. The page is full when a new line pointer and a new tuple do not both fit in this gap.

  
Each INSERT takes 4 bytes from the front of the gap (a line pointer) and the tuple size, rounded up to 8 bytes, from the back. The two ends meet in the middle. This is why the page does not need to know the tuple count in advance.

  
The special space is at the very end. A table page does not use it (pd_special = 8192). A B-tree page stores links to its left and right sibling pages there (chapter 16).

 

 





The tuple


Each tuple starts with a fixed header, HeapTupleHeaderData. It is 23 bytes on most machines:




FieldSizeWhat it is


t_xmin4The XID of the transaction that inserted this tuple.


t_xmax4The XID of the transaction that deleted or updated it. 0 if none. Chapter 12 is about these two fields.


t_cid4The command number inside the transaction. It lets a statement not see its own new tuples.


t_ctid6The TID of this tuple, or of the newer tuple of the same row after an UPDATE.


t_infomask22The number of columns, plus flag bits (for example "heap-only tuple").


t_infomask2Flag bits: "has NULLs", "has variable-width columns", and the hint bits that cache the commit status of t_xmin and t_xmax.


t_hoff1The offset where the column data starts.




After the header comes the null bitmap, but only if at least one column is NULL. It has one bit per column. A NULL value takes no space in the data area, only its bit. Then comes padding, because t_hoff must be a multiple of 8 on a 64-bit machine. With no NULLs, the 23-byte header is padded to 24. With NULLs in a table of up to 8 columns, the bitmap is 1 byte and 23 + 1 = 24, so the padding goes away. Then comes the column data, one column after the other, in the order of the table definition.


Each column value is aligned for its type. A bigint, a timestamptz and a double precision start on an 8-byte boundary. An int starts on a 4-byte boundary. A boolean needs no alignment. So the column order changes the row size:


-- (a boolean, b bigint, c boolean, d bigint)
-- a: 1 + 7 padding, b: 8, c: 1 + 7 padding, d: 8   = 32 bytes of data
-- (b bigint, d bigint, a boolean, c boolean)
-- b: 8, d: 8, a: 1, c: 1                          = 18 bytes of data


With the 24-byte header and the rounding of the whole tuple to 8 bytes, that is 56 against 48 bytes per tuple. On a table with hundreds of millions of rows, the difference is gigabytes. The usual rule is to put the fixed-width 8-byte columns first, then 4-byte, then 2-byte, then the variable-width columns such as text. Short text values (up to 126 bytes) have a 1-byte length header and no alignment, so they pack well.



The TID and one level of indirection


A TID is the address of a tuple, as the pair (page number, line pointer number). You can see it in the hidden column ctid:


acmeshop=# SELECT ctid, id, name FROM customers ORDER BY id LIMIT 3;
 ctid  | id | name
-------+----+------
 (0,1) |  1 | Ana
 (0,2) |  2 | Ben
 (0,3) |  3 | Chen


An index entry holds a TID. It does not hold the byte offset of the tuple. The reason is the line pointer. Inside a page, PostgreSQL can move tuples to join small free gaps into one large gap (this is called defragmentation). When it moves a tuple, it changes only the offset in the line pointer. The line pointer number stays the same, so every TID in every index is still correct. The line pointer is freed only when no index can point to it any more (chapter 13). The cost of this design is 4 bytes per tuple and one extra step on each lookup.


A ctid is not a stable row ID. An UPDATE writes a new tuple with a new TID, and VACUUM FULL or CLUSTER gives every tuple a new TID. Do not store it in the application.



How an INSERT finds a page


    

  1. The backend first tries the last page it inserted into for this table (it keeps this in memory).
    2. 

  2. If that page has no room, it asks the free space map for a page with enough free space.
    3. 

  3. If no page has room, it adds a new empty page at the end of the file ("extends" the relation).
    4. 

  4. It writes the tuple, adds a line pointer, moves pd_lower and pd_upper, and writes a WAL record (chapter 15).
    5. 



The free space map is not exact and not always current. VACUUM updates it (chapter 13). So a table where rows are only deleted can have empty space that INSERTs do not find until VACUUM runs.


fillfactor is a table setting from 10 to 100. The default for tables is 100, so INSERT fills pages completely. With fillfactor = 70, INSERT stops using a page when it is 70% full. The other 30% stays free for UPDATEs of rows on that page. An UPDATE that finds room on the same page can be a HOT update, which does not need new index entries (chapter 12). So a lower fillfactor is good for tables with many UPDATEs, and 100 is best for tables that are only inserted into.


ALTER TABLE orders SET (fillfactor = 80);   -- applies to pages filled from now on



How a read finds a row


The heap has no order. A new tuple goes to whichever page has room. So there are two ways to find a row:


    

  • Seq Scan. Read every page of the file in order. For each line pointer, check if the tuple is visible to the snapshot (chapter 12) and if it matches the WHERE clause. The reads are sequential, so they are fast per page, but the cost grows with the table size.
    • 

  • Index scan. Search the index (chapter 16). It gives TIDs. For each TID, read that heap page, follow the line pointer, check visibility. Each TID can be a different page, so this is random I/O. It is fast when few rows match.
    • 



Chapter 18 compares the scan types. CLUSTER orders USING orders_pkey rewrites the table in index order. This is a one-time change. New and updated rows do not keep the order, and the command takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock that blocks reads and writes for the whole run.



TOAST: values larger than a page


A tuple cannot span two pages. The largest tuple is 8160 bytes (8192 minus the header and one line pointer). But a text or jsonb value can be up to 1 GB. TOAST (The Oversized-Attribute Storage Technique) closes this gap. Only variable-width types (text, bytea, jsonb, arrays, numeric and so on) can be TOASTed. A table with such columns has a TOAST table, linked by pg_class.reltoastrelid.


Say we add a long description to products:


ALTER TABLE products ADD COLUMN description text;


When a new tuple is wider than TOAST_TUPLE_THRESHOLD, PostgreSQL makes it smaller before it stores it. The threshold is chosen so that 4 tuples fit on a page. The macro in heaptoast.h gives 2032 bytes for 8 kB pages. (The CREATE TABLE page gives 2040 bytes for the related toast_tuple_target. Both are "about 2 kB".) The steps are:


    

  1. Compress the largest values that allow compression. The method is pglz by default (default_toast_compression), or lz4 if the server was built with it. You can set it per column: ALTER TABLE products ALTER COLUMN description SET COMPRESSION lz4;
    2. 

  2. If the tuple is still wider than the target (also about 2 kB), move values out of line. The value is cut into chunks of at most 1996 bytes. Each chunk is one row in the TOAST table, with columns chunk_id, chunk_seq and chunk_data. A unique index on (chunk_id, chunk_seq) finds them.
    3. 

  3. In the main tuple, the value is replaced by an 18-byte TOAST pointer.
    4. 



Each column has a storage strategy (ALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMN ... SET STORAGE):




StrategyCompress?Move out of line?Use


PLAINnonoFixed-width types such as int, bigint. If a row is too big, the INSERT fails.


EXTENDEDyes, firstyes, if still too bigThe default for text, jsonb, bytea and most variable-width types.


EXTERNALnoyesLarge values that do not compress, or when you read parts with substring(). PostgreSQL can then fetch only the chunks it needs.


MAINyesonly as a last resortKeep the value in the main tuple where possible.




TOAST has a clear effect on queries. The main table stays small, so more of it fits in shared buffers (chapter 14), and a Seq Scan that filters on small columns reads fewer pages. A TOASTed value is fetched and decompressed only when the query needs it. So SELECT * on a table with large TOASTed columns costs more than SELECT id, name. Each large value means extra index lookups and page reads in the TOAST table, plus decompression. An UPDATE that does not change a TOASTed value keeps the old pointer and does not copy the value. An UPDATE that changes it writes the whole value again, even for a one-character change.




 
Lab: one 8 kB heap page

 
Insert tuples into one page and watch pd_lower and pd_upper move. The numbers follow the real layout, with a 24-byte page header, 4-byte line pointers, 23-byte tuple header padded to 24, every tuple rounded up to 8 bytes. The other columns of the row take 40 bytes in this lab (an example). Delete a tuple, then prune the page, then VACUUM, and watch the line pointer. Make the description larger than about 2 kB to see TOAST.

 

  
  
  
 

 

  
  
  
  
  
  
  
 

 

 

 

  
lpctidstateoffsetlengthnote

  

 




In the lab, DELETE does not free any space. It only sets t_xmax. Pruning removes the tuple bytes and joins the gaps, but the line pointer stays as "dead", because an index can still hold its TID. Only VACUUM, after it has removed the index entries, marks the line pointer "unused", and then a new INSERT can reuse it. The tuples that stay have the same TID before and after pruning, even though their offsets change.









12. MVCC: tuples, XIDs and snapshots


Covers: the reader/writer problem, xmin and xmax, UPDATE and DELETE as new tuples, snapshots and the visibility rule, pg_xact and hint bits, rollback, HOT updates, the xmin horizon, Read Committed against Repeatable Read, the undo-log design. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Concurrency Control, Introduction" (§13.1), "Transaction Isolation" (§13.2), "Transactions and Identifiers" (§67.1), "System Information Functions" (pg_current_snapshot, Table 9.85), "Database Page Layout" (§66.6), "Heap-Only Tuples (HOT)" (§66.7), CREATE TABLE (fillfactor); source src/backend/access/heap/README.HOT, src/backend/storage/buffer/README (hint bits). Checked 2026-09-30.


MVCC (multiversion concurrency control) means that PostgreSQL keeps several tuples of the same row at the same time. A write never changes a tuple in place. It adds a new tuple and marks the old one as ended. Each statement reads with a snapshot, a list of which transactions had committed when it started, and it sees only the tuples that were current for that snapshot.



The problem


Say session A runs a long report over orders and session B changes the status of one order at the same time. With one copy of each row, there are two bad options. A can lock the rows it reads, and then B must wait for the report. Or B can change the row in place, and then A sees half-old and half-new data. MVCC gives a third option. B writes a new tuple. A keeps reading the old tuple, because A's snapshot says that B had not committed when A started. The docs state it as "reading never blocks writing and writing never blocks reading". Two writers of the same row still wait for each other (chapter 28).



The mechanism: xmin and xmax


Every transaction that writes gets a XID, a 32-bit number from one counter for the whole server. PostgreSQL gives the XID at the first write, not at BEGIN. A read-only transaction never gets one. It has only a "virtual" ID, which costs nothing. Each tuple header (chapter 11) holds two XIDs: t_xmin, the XID that inserted the tuple, and t_xmax, the XID that deleted or updated it (0 if none). You can read them as hidden columns:


acmeshop=# SELECT ctid, xmin, xmax, id, country FROM customers WHERE id = 1;
 ctid  | xmin | xmax | id | country
-------+------+------+----+---------
 (0,1) |   90 |    0 |  1 | DE          -- example XIDs



 

  
XID 100: INSERT Ana, DE
→
(0,1) xmin 100, xmax 0Ana, DE

  
XID 105: UPDATE country = 'US'
→
(0,1) xmin 100, xmax 105Ana, DE, t_ctid → (0,2)
(0,2) xmin 105, xmax 0Ana, US

  
XID 110: DELETE
→
(0,2) xmin 105, xmax 110Ana, US

  
(0,1) dead after 105 commits and old snapshots end
(0,2) dead after 110 commits and old snapshots end

 

 

  
INSERT writes one tuple. xmin is the XID of the inserting transaction. Until XID 100 commits, no other session sees the row.

  
UPDATE is "mark the old tuple, insert a new one". It sets xmax = 105 on the old tuple and sets the old tuple's t_ctid to the TID of the new tuple. The new tuple has xmin = 105. It is a full copy of the row with the new value, not only the changed column. A snapshot from before 105 committed still sees DE. A snapshot from after sees US.

  
DELETE writes no new tuple. It only sets xmax = 110. The bytes stay on the page.

  
A tuple becomes a dead tuple when its xmax has committed and no snapshot that could still see it is open. Only then can pruning or VACUUM remove it (chapter 13).

 

 





Snapshots and the visibility rule


A snapshot has three parts. PostgreSQL prints it as xmin:xmax:xip_list:


acmeshop=# SELECT pg_current_snapshot();
 pg_current_snapshot
---------------------
 100:104:100,102        -- example


    

  • xmin (100): the lowest XID that was still running. Every XID below it has either committed or rolled back.
    • 

  • xmax (104): one more than the highest XID that had finished. Every XID from here up had not finished, so its work is not visible.
    • 

  • xip (100, 102): the XIDs between the two that were still running.
    • 



To decide if a tuple is visible to a snapshot, PostgreSQL asks these questions in order:


    

  1. Did my own transaction insert it (xmin is my XID)? Then I see it, unless I also deleted it.
    2. 

  2. Did the inserting XID roll back? Then no one sees it.
    3. 

  3. Was the inserting XID still running for my snapshot (it is in xip, or it is at or above the snapshot's xmax)? Then I do not see it, even if it has committed since.
    4. 

  4. So the insert is visible. Now look at xmax. Is it 0, or did that XID roll back? Then I see the tuple.
    5. 

  5. Did my own transaction delete it? Then I do not see it.
    6. 

  6. Was the deleting XID still running for my snapshot? Then the delete does not count for me, and I see the tuple.
    7. 

  7. Else the delete committed before my snapshot. I do not see the tuple.
    8. 



Question 2 needs the commit status of an XID. That is stored in the commit log, the pg_xact directory. To avoid a lookup for every tuple, the first backend that finds the status writes it into the tuple as hint bits in t_infomask ("xmin committed", "xmax invalid" and so on). The next reader does not need pg_xact. Setting hint bits changes the page, so the first SELECT after a large load can cause writes to disk.



What a rollback costs


Nothing is undone. ROLLBACK marks the XID as aborted in pg_xact, and that is all. The tuples it inserted have an aborted xmin, so rule 2 hides them. The tuples it "deleted" have an aborted xmax, so rule 4 shows them again. So a rollback of a transaction that changed a million rows is as fast as a commit. The price is paid later. The million new tuples are dead, and VACUUM must remove them.



The consequences


    

  • Dead tuples build up. Every UPDATE and DELETE leaves one. Something must remove them and give the space back. That is VACUUM (chapter 13).
    • 

  • An UPDATE writes a whole new tuple. Changing one boolean in a 300-byte row writes about 300 bytes plus WAL. (Large TOASTed values that do not change are not copied, chapter 11.)
    • 

  • Each index can need a new entry. The new tuple has a new TID. An index entry points to a TID, so every index on the table gets a new entry, even if the indexed value did not change. A table with 8 indexes pays 8 index inserts per UPDATE. HOT removes this cost in a common case.
    • 




HOT: heap-only tuple updates


An UPDATE is a HOT update when two conditions are true:


    

  1. No column that any index uses is changed. "Uses" includes columns in a partial-index WHERE clause and in expression indexes. Summarizing indexes (BRIN is the only one in core) do not count; this is since PostgreSQL 16.
    2. 

  2. The new tuple fits on the same page as the old one.
    3. 



Then no index gets a new entry. The old tuple is marked "HOT updated". The new tuple is marked "heap-only". An index scan still finds the row. It follows the index to the old line pointer, then follows t_ctid along the HOT chain on the same page. Later, when the old tuple is dead, pruning removes its bytes and turns its line pointer into a redirect to the newest tuple. The index still points at the first line pointer, and the redirect sends it on. This pruning can happen during a normal SELECT or UPDATE on the page, without VACUUM. It is tried when the page has less free space than the larger of 10% and the fillfactor reserve, or when an UPDATE did not find room.


In acmeshop, UPDATE orders SET status = 'paid' WHERE id = 42 can be HOT, because status has no index. If you add CREATE INDEX ON orders (status), the same statement can never be HOT again. This is a real cost of an index that people often miss. Condition 2 is why a lower fillfactor (for example 80 or 90) helps tables with many UPDATEs. The free space on each page is kept for new tuples of rows on that page. Check the result with n_tup_upd and n_tup_hot_upd in pg_stat_user_tables. n_tup_newpage_upd counts the updates that had to move to a different page.



The xmin horizon and long transactions


A dead tuple can be removed only when no open snapshot can see it. PostgreSQL finds the oldest xmin of all open snapshots and running transactions. This is the xmin horizon. A tuple deleted by an XID at or above the horizon must stay. So one transaction that stays open for 6 hours (a forgotten BEGIN in a psql window, a batch job, a session "idle in transaction") keeps every tuple deleted in those 6 hours, in every table of the database. VACUUM runs, finds them, and cannot remove them. Replication slots and prepared transactions can hold the horizon back the same way (chapter 13). Find the old ones with SELECT pid, xact_start, state, backend_xmin FROM pg_stat_activity ORDER BY age(backend_xmin) DESC;. The setting idle_in_transaction_session_timeout ends sessions that sit idle inside a transaction.



Read Committed and Repeatable Read


The isolation level decides when the snapshot is taken. Read Committed (the default) takes a new snapshot at the start of each statement. So two SELECTs in one transaction can see different data. Repeatable Read takes one snapshot at the first statement of the transaction and keeps it. If a Repeatable Read transaction tries to update a row that another transaction changed and committed after the snapshot, it fails with ERROR: could not serialize access due to concurrent update, and the application must retry. Chapter 27 covers the levels and their anomalies in detail.



The other design: undo logs


Oracle and MySQL InnoDB also use MVCC, but they change the row in place and copy the old version to a separate undo log. A reader that needs the old version rebuilds it from the undo log. With this design the table does not fill up with dead tuples and indexes point to a stable row. In exchange, a rollback must apply the undo records, which takes time, and old versions that a long query needs can be purged from undo before it finishes. PostgreSQL's design makes rollback free and reads simple, and it moves the cost to VACUUM and to write amplification on UPDATE.




 
Lab: three sessions on the customers table

 
Run statements in any order in sessions A, B and C. A statement outside BEGIN runs as its own transaction. Watch the tuples on the page, their xmin and xmax, each session's snapshot, and which tuples each session can see. The UPDATE changes country, which has no index, so it is a HOT update. XIDs start at 100 (an example). The first three rows were loaded by XID 90, which committed.

 
Try this. In A, run BEGIN and UPDATE row 1, then SELECT in B (B still sees the old tuple). In B, UPDATE row 1 while A is open (B waits). Then COMMIT or ROLLBACK A and see what B does. Try it with B in Repeatable Read after B has already run a SELECT. A session without an open Repeatable Read snapshot shows what its next statement would see. Hover a cell for the reason.

 

 
lpxminxmaxt_ctidrowA seesB seesC seesnote

 

 











13. VACUUM, bloat and XID wraparound


Covers: what VACUUM does step by step, VACUUM FULL and pg_repack, bloat, autovacuum and its trigger formulas, cost-based delay, per-table settings, XID wraparound and freezing, the visibility map, ANALYZE, monitoring. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Routine Vacuuming" (§24.1), "Vacuuming" settings (§19.10), VACUUM, "Progress Reporting" (VACUUM phases, §27.4), "Visibility Map" (§66.4), "The Cumulative Statistics System" (pg_stat_all_tables, §27.2), "Explicit Locking" (§13.3), Release 18 notes. Checked 2026-09-30.


VACUUM removes dead tuples from a table and its indexes and records the free space so that new tuples can use it. Autovacuum runs VACUUM for you when enough tuples have died. VACUUM also freezes old tuples, which PostgreSQL needs because XIDs are only 32 bits and run in a circle.



What VACUUM does, step by step


Chapter 12 showed that UPDATE and DELETE leave dead tuples. Pruning during normal reads can free the tuple bytes on one page (chapter 11), but it cannot free line pointers that indexes still point to. Only VACUUM does the full job, in these phases (the names are the ones pg_stat_progress_vacuum shows):



 

  
visibility mapskip pages marked all-visible

  
↓

  
scanning heapprune, freeze, collect dead TIDs

  
↓ list of dead TIDs in memory

  
vacuuming indexesdelete entries that point to the dead TIDs

  
↓

  
vacuuming heapdead line pointers become unused

  
↓

  
truncate, update FSM, VM, pg_class

 

 

  
VACUUM reads the visibility map first. A page marked all-visible has no dead tuples, so VACUUM skips it. On a large table where only recent rows change, VACUUM reads a small part of the heap.

  
For each other page, VACUUM prunes it. The space of dead tuples is freed and their line pointers are marked dead. It also freezes tuples that are old enough (see below). It adds the TIDs of the dead line pointers to a list in memory. The size of the list is limited by maintenance_work_mem (default 64MB), or autovacuum_work_mem for autovacuum.

  
Each index is read and every entry that points to a TID in the list is deleted. A B-tree index is read in full for this. If the list filled up before the heap scan ended, VACUUM runs this phase and the next, then goes on with the heap scan. More memory means fewer full passes over the indexes.

  
Now no index points to the dead line pointers, so VACUUM goes back to those heap pages and marks the line pointers unused. New tuples can take them.

  
If the last pages of the file are now empty, VACUUM cuts them off and gives the space to the operating system. This needs a short ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock, and VACUUM gives up the truncation if it cannot get the lock. The setting vacuum_truncate turns this step off. Then it updates the free space map, sets all-visible and all-frozen bits in the visibility map, and writes new relpages, reltuples and relfrozenxid into pg_class.

 

 




Plain VACUUM takes a SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE lock. SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE go on during it. It does not make the file smaller, except for empty pages at the very end. The free space stays inside the file for new tuples of the same table. If a table has grown to 50 GB with 40 GB of free space, it stays 50 GB.


VACUUM FULL writes a new copy of the table with no free space, rebuilds all indexes, then drops the old files. It needs an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock for the whole run, so no one can even read the table, and it needs free disk space about the size of the table and its indexes. CLUSTER does the same with a sort. On a production table this is usually an outage. The pg_repack extension (not part of core PostgreSQL) builds the new copy while the table stays in use and takes the exclusive lock only for a short swap at the end.



Bloat


Bloat is space in a table or index that holds no live data. It is made of dead tuples that are not removed yet, plus free space that VACUUM found but no new tuple has used. Some free space is normal. The docs describe the goal as a steady state, where the table has its minimum size plus the space used between two VACUUM runs. Bloat becomes a problem when dead tuples are made faster than they are removed. The usual causes are:


    

  • A long-running transaction. It holds back the xmin horizon (chapter 12). VACUUM runs but may not remove any tuple that died after the transaction started.
    • 

  • An abandoned replication slot. A slot for a standby or a logical consumer that is gone keeps the horizon (xmin or catalog_xmin in pg_replication_slots) and also keeps WAL (chapter 30).
    • 

  • An old prepared transaction (PREPARE TRANSACTION without COMMIT PREPARED). See pg_prepared_xacts.
    • 

  • Autovacuum that starts too late or runs too slowly: the default thresholds on a very large table, a low cost limit, too few workers for many busy tables, or autovacuum that is cancelled again and again by DDL or LOCK TABLE.
    • 



Bloat has costs. A Seq Scan reads all pages, dead or not. Fewer live rows fit in each page, so shared buffers and the OS cache hold less useful data. Indexes bloat too, because a half-empty B-tree page stays in the index (chapter 17). Backups and standbys get larger. Index-only scans (chapter 18) need the all-visible bit, which a page with dead tuples does not have.



Autovacuum


The autovacuum launcher is a background process. It tries to start a worker in each database every autovacuum_naptime (default 1min). At most autovacuum_max_workers (default 3) workers run at the same time. A worker checks each table in its database with these formulas (reltuples is the row count estimate in pg_class; the counts of changes come from the statistics system):




ActionRuns whenDefaults


VACUUM (dead tuples)dead tuples > min(autovacuum_vacuum_max_threshold, autovacuum_vacuum_threshold + autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor × reltuples)max 100,000,000 (new in PostgreSQL 18), threshold 50, scale factor 0.2


VACUUM (inserts)tuples inserted since last vacuum > autovacuum_vacuum_insert_threshold + autovacuum_vacuum_insert_scale_factor × reltuples × fraction of pages not all-frozen1000, 0.2. The "not frozen" factor is new in PostgreSQL 18.


ANALYZEtuples inserted, updated or deleted since last analyze > autovacuum_analyze_threshold + autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor × reltuples50, 0.1


VACUUM (anti-wraparound)age of relfrozenxid > autovacuum_freeze_max_age200 million. Runs even if autovacuum is off.




For orders with 1,000,000 rows (example), autovacuum starts after 50 + 0.2 × 1,000,000 = 200,050 dead tuples. That is fine. For a table with 400,000,000 rows, 20% is 80 million dead tuples before the first run. That is tens of gigabytes of dead space, and the VACUUM that finally runs must scan all of it and pass over every index. Before PostgreSQL 18, a table with 2 billion rows waited for 400 million. The new cap of 100 million helps only the very largest tables. The fix is per-table settings:


ALTER TABLE orders SET (
  autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.01,     -- 1% of the table
  autovacuum_vacuum_threshold    = 10000,
  autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor = 0.02
);


Cost-based delay


VACUUM reads and writes a lot. To limit its effect on queries, it counts a cost for each page: 1 for a page found in shared buffers (vacuum_cost_page_hit), 2 for a page read from the OS (vacuum_cost_page_miss), 20 for a page it makes dirty (vacuum_cost_page_dirty). When the total reaches the limit, it sleeps. For autovacuum the limit is vacuum_cost_limit (200) and the sleep is autovacuum_vacuum_cost_delay (2 ms). The limit is shared by all running workers. From these defaults you can compute a ceiling (the time of the work itself is not counted). It allows 100 page misses per 2 ms, so at most about 50,000 pages or 390 MB per second read. But if every page is also made dirty, each page costs 22, so about 9 pages per 2 ms, or about 35 MB per second. On a busy system with a large table, that can be slower than the rate at which tuples die. Raising autovacuum_vacuum_cost_limit (for example to 1000 or 2000 on fast disks) is a common tuning step. Manual VACUUM has vacuum_cost_delay = 0, so it runs at full speed.


A normal autovacuum gives way. If another session asks for a lock that conflicts with its SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE lock (for example ALTER TABLE), the autovacuum is cancelled. An anti-wraparound autovacuum (shown in pg_stat_activity as "(to prevent wraparound)") is not cancelled.




 
Lab: autovacuum on a busy table

 
This simulates 6 hours, one step per autovacuum_naptime (1 minute). Each UPDATE makes one dead tuple. It uses the real trigger formula with the PostgreSQL 18 defaults. It treats each VACUUM as instant and as the only process that frees space, and it assumes 100 bytes per tuple including overhead (example values). With the long transaction on, a transaction is open from hour 1 to hour 3. VACUUM still runs, but it cannot remove tuples that died after that transaction started.

 

  
  
 

 

  
  
 

 

 
 
 
Top: dead tuples (red), the trigger line (dashed) and each autovacuum run (ticks). Bottom: table size in MB (green) and the size with no dead space at all (dashed).





XID wraparound and freezing


An XID is 32 bits. PostgreSQL compares XIDs modulo 232: for any XID, the 2 billion before it are "in the past" and the 2 billion after it are "in the future". The space is a circle. So a tuple with xmin = 100 looks like the past for the next 2 billion transactions. After that it would look like the future, and the row would disappear from every query. The data would still be on disk, but no query could see it.


The fix is freezing. When VACUUM finds a tuple whose xmin is older than vacuum_freeze_min_age (default 50 million transactions) and has committed, it sets a flag in the tuple header that means "this tuple is older than every XID". Since PostgreSQL 9.4 the original xmin stays in place for forensics. A frozen tuple is visible to everyone until it is deleted, no matter how far the counter goes. For each table, pg_class.relfrozenxid is the oldest XID that can still be unfrozen in it. For each database, pg_database.datfrozenxid is the oldest of those.


Normal VACUUM skips all-visible pages, so it can leave old unfrozen tuples behind. So PostgreSQL has a ladder of safety steps, measured by age(relfrozenxid):




AgeSettingWhat happens


50 millionvacuum_freeze_min_ageTuples older than this are frozen when VACUUM visits their page.


150 millionvacuum_freeze_table_ageThe next VACUUM of the table is aggressive: it reads every page that is not all-frozen, not only pages with dead tuples.


200 millionautovacuum_freeze_max_ageAutovacuum starts an anti-wraparound VACUUM on the table, even if autovacuum is off. Lock conflicts do not cancel it.


1.6 billionvacuum_failsafe_ageFailsafe: VACUUM stops the cost delay, skips index vacuuming and stops using its small buffer ring, to freeze as fast as possible.


40 million XIDs leftfixedEvery new XID gives WARNING: database "acmeshop" must be vacuumed within N transactions.


3 million XIDs leftfixedThe server refuses to assign new XIDs: ERROR: database is not accepting commands that assign new transaction IDs to avoid wraparound data loss. Only read-only transactions work. Writes are down until VACUUM finishes.




To get out of the last state, the docs list the steps in order: commit or roll back old prepared transactions, end long-running transactions, drop old replication slots, then run a database-wide VACUUM (not VACUUM FULL, which needs an XID, and not VACUUM FREEZE, which does more work than needed). Single-user mode is not needed any more.


A write-heavy system can use 200 million XIDs in days or hours, so anti-wraparound VACUUMs are normal events on large tables. They are a problem when they cannot finish, because something holds the horizon, or the table is so large that the VACUUM with the default cost limit takes days. Since PostgreSQL 18, a normal VACUUM can also freeze some all-visible pages early ("eager" scanning, vacuum_max_eager_freeze_failure_rate), which makes the later aggressive VACUUM shorter.



 
Lab: XID age of the oldest table

 
Move the slider to set age(datfrozenxid). The marks on the bar are the real default thresholds. The wraparound point is at 231 (about 2.147 billion).

 
 

 

 





The visibility map


The visibility map (chapter 11) has two bits per heap page. All-visible means every tuple on the page is visible to every current and future snapshot. VACUUM sets it after it cleans a page, and any INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE on the page clears it. It gives two things. VACUUM can skip the page. And an Index Only Scan can return a value from the index without reading the heap page, because it knows the tuple is visible (chapter 18). All-frozen means every tuple on the page is frozen, so even an aggressive VACUUM can skip it. On a large table where old rows never change, this makes anti-wraparound VACUUMs cheap.



ANALYZE


ANALYZE reads a random sample of a large table (the sample grows with default_statistics_target, default 100) and stores statistics per column in pg_statistic: the fraction of NULLs, the number of distinct values, the most common values and a histogram. The planner uses them to estimate how many rows each plan node returns (chapter 21). Autovacuum runs it by the formula above. Run it by hand after a large load or a large delete, because a plan built on old statistics can be very wrong. Autovacuum does not analyze partitioned parent tables. You must run ANALYZE on those yourself.



Monitoring


-- Tables with the most dead tuples, and when autovacuum last ran
SELECT relname, n_live_tup, n_dead_tup, last_autovacuum, last_autoanalyze,
       autovacuum_count
FROM pg_stat_user_tables
ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC LIMIT 10;

-- XID age per table: watch anything that goes past 200 million
SELECT c.oid::regclass AS table, age(c.relfrozenxid) AS xid_age,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_table_size(c.oid)) AS size
FROM pg_class c
WHERE c.relkind IN ('r','m','t')
ORDER BY age(c.relfrozenxid) DESC LIMIT 10;

-- What holds the horizon back
SELECT pid, state, xact_start, age(backend_xmin) FROM pg_stat_activity
 WHERE backend_xmin IS NOT NULL ORDER BY age(backend_xmin) DESC;
SELECT slot_name, active, age(xmin), age(catalog_xmin) FROM pg_replication_slots;
SELECT gid, prepared, age(transaction) FROM pg_prepared_xacts;


n_dead_tup is an estimate. VACUUM (VERBOSE) orders prints how many dead tuples it removed and how many it found but could not remove ("dead but not yet removable"). A high second number points to a held horizon. Set log_autovacuum_min_duration (for example to 10s) to log slow autovacuum runs with the same details.









14. Shared buffers: the page cache


Covers: why a cache inside the server, buffer descriptors, the buffer mapping table, pins and usage counts, the lookup path, the clock sweep, dirty pages and who writes them, double buffering and sizing, buffer rings, asynchronous I/O in PostgreSQL 18, how to observe it. Sources: src/backend/storage/buffer/README (REL_18_STABLE), src/include/storage/buf_internals.h, src/backend/access/heap/heapam.c (ring condition); PostgreSQL 18 docs "Resource Consumption" (§19.4), "Query Planning" (effective_cache_size, §19.7), pg_buffercache (§F.25), "The Cumulative Statistics System" (pg_stat_io, §27.2), Release 18 notes. Checked 2026-09-30.


Shared buffers is a large area of shared memory that holds copies of 8 kB pages. All backends use the same copies. Every page that PostgreSQL reads or changes, from a table, an index, a TOAST table or a visibility map, is first loaded into a buffer. Queries read and change the page there, never on disk directly.



Why a cache inside the server


The operating system already caches files. PostgreSQL still needs its own cache for three reasons. First, many backends must work on the same page at the same time, with locks and pins that the OS cache does not know about. Second, a changed page must not reach disk before its WAL record does (chapter 15). PostgreSQL can enforce that only if it decides when a page is written. Third, a hit in shared buffers is a memory access. A hit in the OS cache is a system call plus a copy of 8 kB.



The structure


    

  • The buffer array: an array of shared_buffers ÷ 8 kB page slots. With the default 128MB, that is 16,384 buffers.
    • 

  • Buffer descriptors: one small header per buffer. It holds the buffer tag (tablespace, database, relfilenode, fork, block number), a reference count (the pin count), a usage count from 0 to 5, flags such as dirty, valid and I/O in progress, and a content lock.
    • 

  • The buffer mapping table: a shared hash table from buffer tag to buffer number. It is split into partitions, each with its own lock, so that backends that look up different pages do not wait for each other.
    • 



A pin means "a backend is using this buffer now". A pinned buffer is never taken for another page. A Seq Scan keeps a pin on its current page while it returns the tuples from it. The usage count means "this page was used recently". Each pin adds 1 up to the maximum of 5 (BM_MAX_USAGE_COUNT). The clock sweep takes 1 away. So a page that is used often keeps a high count and stays in memory.



How a backend gets a page



 

  
backend needs orders, main fork, page 7

  
↓ hash the tag

  
buffer mapping table

  
hit → pin it, usage count + 1

  
miss → free buffer, else clock sweep picks a victim

  
victim is dirty → flush WAL up to its pd_lsn, write the page

  
read page 7 from the file, update the mapping table, pin it

 

 

  
The backend builds the buffer tag and looks it up in the mapping table under a shared lock on one partition.

  
Hit. The page is already in buffer number N. The backend pins it (the usage count goes up by 1, to at most 5), takes a content lock (shared to read, exclusive to change) and uses it. No I/O.

  
Miss. The backend needs an empty buffer. In PostgreSQL 18, buffers that hold no page yet are on a free list and are used first. When the free list is empty, which is the normal state on a busy server, the clock sweep runs. The "hand" is one shared buffer number. The backend looks at the buffer under the hand and moves the hand on by one. If the buffer is pinned or its usage count is above 0, it takes 1 from the usage count and looks at the next one. The first buffer that is unpinned with usage count 0 is the victim.

  
If the victim is dirty (changed since it was read), its old page must be written first. The WAL rule applies. WAL up to the page's pd_lsn must be on disk before the page is written. So a backend that finds dirty victims can wait for a WAL flush and a page write before it can even start its read.

  
The backend removes the old tag from the mapping table, adds the new one, reads the page from the file into the buffer (through the OS cache) and pins it. A new page starts with usage count 1.

 

 




The clock sweep is an approximation of "evict the least recently used page". It needs no list that every access must update, only one counter per buffer. A buffer with usage count 5 survives five full turns of the hand without use. A page read once has count 1 and is gone after one turn. The trade-off is this. If all buffers have high counts, a backend may have to walk several full turns and take 1 from each buffer before it finds a victim.
