Dirty pages and who writes them


A change to a page (an INSERT, a hint bit, a VACUUM) only changes the buffer and marks it dirty. The write to the file comes later, from one of three processes:




WhoWhenSettings


Background writerEvery bgwriter_delay it looks ahead of the clock hand for dirty, unpinned buffers with usage count 0 (the next victims) and writes them. It writes about as many as backends needed recently, times bgwriter_lru_multiplier.bgwriter_delay 200ms, bgwriter_lru_maxpages 100, bgwriter_lru_multiplier 2.0


CheckpointerAt each checkpoint it writes every dirty buffer, spread over time (chapter 15).checkpoint_timeout, max_wal_size


A backendWhen the victim it picked is dirty. This adds write time to a query, so a high count here is a bad sign.none




A write goes to the OS cache first. The data is safe on disk only after fsync, which the checkpointer does at the end of each checkpoint. Crash safety comes from the WAL, not from these writes (chapter 15).



Double buffering and sizing


PostgreSQL uses normal buffered file I/O. So a page that is read goes through the OS page cache, and it can then be in memory twice, once in shared buffers and once in the OS cache. This is double buffering. The docs advise, for a dedicated server with 1 GB or more of RAM, to start with shared_buffers at 25% of RAM, and say more than 40% is unlikely to work better, because PostgreSQL also relies on the OS cache. A larger shared_buffers usually needs a larger max_wal_size, so that checkpoints do not come too often. The default is 128MB, which is far too small for a production server. Changing it needs a restart.


effective_cache_size (default 4GB) allocates nothing. It is only the planner's estimate of how much cache (shared buffers plus the OS cache) one query can use. A higher value makes index scans look cheaper, because repeated reads of the same pages are more likely to be hits (chapter 21). A common value is 50% to 75% of RAM; that is common advice, not a value from the docs.



Buffer rings: large scans do not flush the cache


A Seq Scan over a 50 GB table reads each page once. With the normal strategy, it would push every useful page out of shared buffers. So PostgreSQL gives such operations a small private ring of buffers that they reuse:


    

  • A Seq Scan of a table larger than a quarter of shared_buffers uses a 256 kB ring (32 buffers).
    • 

  • VACUUM and ANALYZE use a ring of vacuum_buffer_usage_limit (default 2MB, since PostgreSQL 16). In the failsafe (chapter 13) VACUUM stops using it.
    • 

  • COPY FROM and CREATE TABLE AS use a 16 MB ring (at most 1/8 of shared buffers).
    • 



As a result, after a full scan of orders, the hot pages of customers and of the indexes are still in memory. The cost is that the scan itself gets few hits, and a second scan right after the first reads from the OS cache again. If a ring buffer is dirty, a Seq Scan leaves it and takes another, so a bulk UPDATE over a whole table works almost like the normal strategy.



Asynchronous I/O in PostgreSQL 18


PostgreSQL 18 adds an asynchronous I/O subsystem. A backend can queue several reads and go on with work while they run, instead of one read, one wait, one read. The release notes name sequential scans, bitmap heap scans and VACUUM as the main users. io_method selects how. The options are worker (the default, where io_workers background processes, default 3, do the reads), io_uring (Linux, if PostgreSQL was built with liburing) or sync (the old behaviour). Reads next to each other can be combined into one larger read up to io_combine_limit (default 128kB). The defaults of effective_io_concurrency and maintenance_io_concurrency went up to 16. The view pg_aios shows the I/Os in progress. All of this changes how pages get into shared buffers, not how the cache itself works.



How to see it


EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) shows buffer use per plan node. Since PostgreSQL 18, EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows it without the BUFFERS option. "shared hit" counts pages found in shared buffers. "read" counts pages that had to be read from the OS (from its cache or from disk; PostgreSQL cannot tell which). "dirtied" and "written" count pages this query changed or had to write out.


CREATE INDEX orders_customer_id_idx ON orders (customer_id);
EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) SELECT * FROM orders WHERE customer_id = 42;

 Index Scan using orders_customer_id_idx on orders  (cost=0.42..40.51 rows=10 width=45)
                                  (actual time=0.031..0.090 rows=11.00 loops=1)
   Index Cond: (customer_id = 42)
   Index Searches: 1
   Buffers: shared hit=12 read=3
 Planning:
   Buffers: shared hit=8
 Planning Time: 0.120 ms
 Execution Time: 0.115 ms              -- all numbers are examples


Run a query twice. The second run often shows only hits, because the pages were loaded by the first run. That is why the first run of a query after a restart is slower. To see what is in the cache, use the pg_buffercache extension:


CREATE EXTENSION pg_buffercache;
SELECT c.relname, count(*) AS buffers,
       count(*) FILTER (WHERE b.isdirty) AS dirty,
       round(avg(b.usagecount), 1) AS avg_usage
FROM pg_buffercache b
JOIN pg_class c ON b.relfilenode = pg_relation_filenode(c.oid)
 AND b.reldatabase = (SELECT oid FROM pg_database WHERE datname = current_database())
GROUP BY c.relname ORDER BY buffers DESC LIMIT 10;


pg_stat_io counts I/O per process type and context: reads, hits, writes, evictions, reuses (ring buffers). Many writes by client backend in context normal mean that backends write their own victims, so the background writer or checkpoints are not keeping up. Many evictions in context normal can mean that shared buffers is too small.


SELECT backend_type, context, reads, hits, writes, evictions, reuses
FROM pg_stat_io WHERE object = 'relation' AND (reads > 0 OR writes > 0);


The overall hit ratio per database is blks_hit / (blks_hit + blks_read) from pg_stat_database. A high ratio is good, but it hides the fact that a "read" may come from the OS cache. Use it as a trend, not as a target.




 
Lab: the clock sweep with 8 buffers

 
Shared buffers here has 8 buffers, and orders has 16 pages (a small example). Request pages and watch hits, misses, the usage counts (the green dots, 0 to 5), the clock hand (blue ring) and the victim. "Write" makes the page dirty. Click a buffer to pin or unpin it. The sweep takes 1 from a pinned buffer's count but never takes the buffer. The workload sends 80% of requests to pages 0 to 3 (a hot set, example numbers). The Seq Scan reads all 16 pages, with or without a ring (2 buffers here, scaled down from 32).

 
Read page:

 

  
  
  
  
  
  
  
 

 

 

 




In the lab, run the workload a few times, and pages 0 to 3 reach high usage counts and stay. Then run the Seq Scan with the ring off. The scan pushes out the hot pages, and the next workload starts with misses. With the ring on, the scan uses only 2 buffers and the hot set stays. Make pages dirty and request new ones. The log shows when a backend must write a victim before its read. A background writer round cleans the next victims first.








15. WAL, checkpoints and crash recovery


Covers: the WAL rule, LSN, WAL segments and buffers, the COMMIT path, group commit, checkpoints, full-page writes, crash recovery, durability settings, unlogged tables. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs chapter 28 "Reliability and the Write-Ahead Log" (§28.1 Reliability, §28.3 Write-Ahead Logging, §28.4 Asynchronous Commit, §28.5 WAL Configuration, §28.6 WAL Internals), §19.5 "Write Ahead Log" settings, §66.6 "Database Page Layout", §27.2 "pg_stat_checkpointer", CREATE TABLE (UNLOGGED). Checked 2026-09-30.


The WAL (write-ahead log) is a set of files where PostgreSQL writes a record of each change before it changes the data files. A transaction is committed when its WAL records are safe on disk. After a crash, PostgreSQL reads the WAL and does the changes again. The changed pages themselves can stay in memory and go to disk later.



The problem


Chapter 14 showed that a query changes a page in shared buffers, not on disk. The page is now dirty. If the server loses power, memory is gone, and so is the change. So something must reach the disk before PostgreSQL tells the client "COMMIT".


The simple answer is to write every dirty page to its data file at each commit. That is slow. One UPDATE orders SET status = 'paid' WHERE id = 42 changes a heap page, and it can change one page in each index. These pages are in different files at random places. Each is a full 8 kB write followed by an fsync per file. A shop that commits thousands of small orders per second would spend its time waiting for random disk writes. There is a second problem. A page write is not atomic. PostgreSQL writes 8,192 bytes, which is 16 sectors of 512 bytes. A power loss can stop the write half-way and leave a torn page, part old and part new (§28.1).



The WAL rule


PostgreSQL follows one rule with two parts (§28.3):


    

  1. Before COMMIT returns, the WAL up to and including the transaction's commit record is flushed to durable storage.
    2. 

  2. Before a dirty page is written to its data file, the WAL up to the last change of that page is flushed.
    3. 



Part 1 makes a commit durable. The data pages can be lost in a crash, because the WAL can rebuild them. Part 2 makes sure the disk never holds a change that the WAL does not know about. If a data page reached disk before its WAL record, and a crash then lost the tail of the WAL, the page would hold a change that recovery cannot see. An index page could then point to a heap tuple that recovery never creates.


The WAL is fast because it is sequential. New records are always appended at the end. A commit costs one append and one flush of the WAL file, not a scatter of random page writes. When many sessions commit at the same moment, one flush can cover the commit records of all of them (§28.3). This is group commit.



LSN, segments and WAL buffers


Each WAL record has a position called the LSN (log sequence number). The LSN is a byte offset into the WAL. It only grows. It is printed as two hex numbers, for example 0/1A2B3C48. Two LSNs can be subtracted to get the number of WAL bytes between them. Replication (chapter 30) uses this to measure lag. Each page header stores pd_lsn, the LSN of the byte just after the WAL record of the last change to that page (§66.6). Part 2 of the rule uses this value.


SELECT pg_current_wal_lsn();                    -- 0/1A2B3C48   (example)
SELECT pg_walfile_name(pg_current_wal_lsn());   -- 00000001000000000000001A
SELECT pg_wal_lsn_diff('0/1A2B3C48', '0/1A000000');   -- 2833480 bytes


On disk the WAL is a series of segment files in pg_wal/ under the data directory. Each is 16 MB by default (set with initdb --wal-segsize). The name holds the timeline and the segment number, so the file above is segment 0x1A of timeline 1 (§28.6). Old segments are recycled (renamed for future use) or removed after a checkpoint.


Records are first built in WAL buffers, an area of shared memory. The default size (wal_buffers = -1) is 1/32 of shared_buffers, at least 64 kB and at most one segment (16 MB). With the default 128 MB of shared_buffers that is 4 MB. A background process, the WAL writer, flushes the WAL buffers every wal_writer_delay (200 ms by default). Commits do not wait for it. They flush for themselves.



The COMMIT path, step by step



 

  
ClientUPDATE orders SET status = 'paid' WHERE id = 42

  
↓

  

   
Backendchanges the page

   
→

   
Shared bufferspage 7 of orders, dirty, pd_lsn = 0/1A2B3C48

  

  
↓ record of the change

  
WAL bufferschange record, then commit record

  
↓ write + fsync up to the commit record

  
pg_wal/00000001000000000000001Asequential append

  
↑ "COMMIT" to the client

  

   
Background writer or checkpointerlater

   
→

   
Data file of orderspage 7, random write

  

 

 

  
The client sends a change. Nothing is on disk yet.

  
The backend finds the page in shared buffers (chapter 14), locks it and writes the new tuple (chapter 12). The page is now dirty. Its pd_lsn is set to the end of the WAL record that describes this change.

  
The WAL record goes into the WAL buffers in shared memory. This is a memory copy, so it is fast. At COMMIT the backend adds a small commit record for its XID.

  
The backend flushes the WAL up to the end of its commit record. If another session already flushed past that point, there is nothing to do. This is where group commit saves work.

  
Only now does the client get "COMMIT". The transaction is durable, but the data file still has the old page.

  
Seconds or minutes later, the background writer or the checkpointer writes the dirty page. Before the write, it checks that the WAL is flushed up to the page's pd_lsn (part 2 of the rule). Many commits to the same page cost one page write.

 

 




commit_delay (default 0 microseconds) makes the first session that is about to flush wait a little, so that more commit records join the same flush. It only waits when at least commit_siblings (default 5) other transactions are active. With the default of 0, group commit still happens for sessions that arrive while a flush is in progress (§28.5).



Checkpoints


If pages were never written, the WAL would grow forever and a crash would replay all of it. A checkpoint fixes both. It is a point in the WAL where every data page change before it is known to be in the data files (§28.5). The checkpointer process does this:


    

  1. It notes the current WAL position. This is the redo point.
    2. 

  2. It writes every page that was dirty at that moment to the data files, and calls fsync on them.
    3. 

  3. It writes a checkpoint record to the WAL that names the redo point, and saves its position in the file pg_control.
    4. 

  4. WAL segments before the one that holds the redo point are no longer needed for crash recovery. They are recycled or removed (after archiving, if archiving is on).
    5. 



A checkpoint starts every checkpoint_timeout (default 5 minutes) or when the WAL since the last one is about to pass max_wal_size (default 1 GB), whichever comes first. If no WAL was written, a timed checkpoint is skipped. The SQL command CHECKPOINT forces one. The page writes are spread out so that the checkpoint ends at about checkpoint_completion_target (default 0.9) of the interval. This keeps the I/O smooth instead of a burst every 5 minutes.


The trade-off is recovery time against write cost. More frequent checkpoints mean less WAL to replay after a crash. They also mean a hot page is written more often, and more full-page images (next section). If checkpoints come closer together than checkpoint_warning (default 30 s) because of WAL volume, the server log says so and suggests a larger max_wal_size. Since PostgreSQL 17 the view pg_stat_checkpointer counts timed checkpoints (num_timed) and requested ones (num_requested). Many requested checkpoints usually mean that max_wal_size is too small for the write load.



Full-page writes and torn pages


A normal WAL record only says "put this tuple at line pointer 5 of page 7". Replay needs a sound page to apply it to. A torn page is not sound. So, with full_page_writes = on (the default), the first change to a page after a checkpoint puts a copy of the whole 8 kB page into the WAL. Replay always starts at a checkpoint, so replay restores that full image first and then applies the later small records. A torn write of the data file does not matter any more.


This has a visible cost. Right after each checkpoint, almost every change touches a page for the first time since the checkpoint, so WAL volume jumps. It then falls as the same pages are changed again. A table with random updates across many pages, such as orders, feels this most. wal_compression (off by default; pglz, lz4 or zstd) compresses the full-page images at a CPU cost. Longer checkpoint intervals also reduce the number of full-page images. Turn full_page_writes off only if the storage guarantees that 8 kB writes are atomic (the docs name ZFS as an example).



Crash recovery


At start after a crash, the server reads pg_control, finds the last checkpoint record, and starts REDO at its redo point (§28.6). It reads WAL records forward to the end of the valid WAL. For each record it looks at the target page. If the page's pd_lsn is already at or past the record, the change is on disk and is skipped. If not, the change is applied. A full-page image replaces the page. The end of the valid WAL is the first record that is missing or fails its checksum.


Recovery does not undo anything. A transaction that was running at the crash has no commit record, so its XID is never marked committed. Its tuples are in the heap, but MVCC treats them as the tuples of an aborted transaction (chapter 12), and VACUUM removes them later (chapter 13). Recovery time depends on the WAL volume since the redo point, which checkpoint_timeout and max_wal_size limit.




 
Lab: crash the server at any moment

 
Eight orders sit on four pages (two orders per page). Each transaction updates the status of one order. Run some transactions, write a page or take a checkpoint, then press Crash and Replay. Try synchronous_commit = off and crash before the WAL writer runs. Then try fsync = off. WAL record sizes are example numbers.

 

  
  
  
  
 

 

  
  
  
  
  
  
  
 

 

  
Memory lost at a crash

  
Disk survives a crash

 

 

 




Things to try in the lab. With the defaults, every commit the client was told about survives, whatever you do before the crash. With synchronous_commit off, a crash before the WAL writer runs loses the last commits, but the result is still consistent. With fsync off, the operating system decides what reaches the disk and in which order. Recovery can then find pages from the future, lost checkpoints or half-applied transactions.



The durability settings




SettingDefaultWhat changes when you move it


fsynconOff stops PostgreSQL from forcing any write to disk. A power loss or OS crash can then cause unrecoverable corruption, not only lost commits. The docs allow it only when the whole cluster can be rebuilt from outside data, for example during an initial load. Good hardware is not a reason. Never turn it off on a database you want to keep.


synchronous_commitonOff returns "COMMIT" before the WAL flush. A crash can lose the last commits the client was told about, up to 3 × wal_writer_delay (600 ms at the default). It cannot cause corruption, because WAL is replayed in order up to the last flushed record, so the database looks as if those transactions aborted. It can be set per transaction with SET LOCAL synchronous_commit = off. Some commands, such as DROP TABLE, always commit synchronously. The values remote_write, remote_apply and local matter with standbys (chapter 30).


full_page_writesonOff removes the full-page images. A torn page after a crash then gives unrecoverable or silent corruption.


wal_levelreplicaminimal logs only what crash recovery needs, and skips row data for tables created or rewritten in the same transaction. It rules out archiving and standbys. logical adds what logical decoding needs (chapter 30). Changed only at server start.


commit_delay0A wait in microseconds before a WAL flush, so that more commits share it. It adds latency to each flush. Useful only with many concurrent commits and slow flushes.




Two things are easy to mix up. synchronous_commit = off gives most of the speed of fsync = off without the corruption risk (§28.4). Use it for data where losing the last fraction of a second is acceptable, such as page-view events. Do not use it where the app acts on the commit, such as payments for an order.


An unlogged table writes no WAL at all. You create one with CREATE UNLOGGED TABLE cart_sessions (...). Writes are much faster. After a crash or an unclean shutdown the table is truncated, and it is not replicated to standbys. It suits data you can rebuild, such as a cache or a staging table for a load.



Other uses of the WAL


The WAL describes every change in order, so it is also the input for other features. A standby server replays the primary's WAL to stay a copy of it (streaming replication). Archived WAL plus a base backup lets you restore the database to any moment (point-in-time recovery). Logical decoding turns WAL into row changes. Chapter 30 covers all three.


In production, two points follow from the WAL rule. The WAL flush is on the path of every commit, so commit latency is the latency of an fsync on the WAL disk. You can measure it with pg_test_fsync. And disks or controllers that report a write as done while it is still in a volatile cache break the rule without any error. A power loss can then corrupt the database (§28.1, §28.6).









16. The B-tree index: structure, search, insert


Covers: index file and metapage, root, internal and leaf pages, index tuples, high key and right-link (Lehman and Yao), tree height, search, range scan, supported operators, ORDER BY, NULLs, insert, page split, fillfactor, ascending vs random keys, unique check. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "B-Tree Indexes" (§65.1, §65.1.4.1 "B-Tree Structure"), "Index Types" (§11.2.1), "Indexes and ORDER BY" (§11.4), "Operator Classes and Operator Families" (§11.10), "CREATE INDEX" (fillfactor, NULLS NOT DISTINCT), "Database Page Layout" (§66.6), "UUID Functions" (§9.14), PostgreSQL 18 release notes; source tree src/backend/access/nbtree/README, nbtsplitloc.c, nbtinsert.c, nbtree.h (REL_18_STABLE). Checked 2026-09-30.


A B-tree index is a separate file of 8 kB pages that holds the values of one or more columns in sorted order. Each value comes with the TID of the tuple that has it. The pages form a tree, so PostgreSQL can find any value by reading only 3 or 4 pages, even in a table with millions of rows.



The problem it solves


Take SELECT * FROM orders WHERE id = 42. The orders table has 1,000,000 rows (an example number). Assume about 100 tuples fit on one heap page (also an example). Then the heap has about 10,000 pages. The heap stores tuples in the order they arrived, not in the order of id. Without an index, the only way to find id = 42 is a Seq Scan. It reads all 10,000 pages and tests every tuple. For one row, that is 9,999 wasted page reads.


An index keeps a sorted copy of the key plus a pointer to the tuple. Sorted data can be searched by halving the search range at each step. A B-tree puts this sorted data into pages and adds levels of pages on top that only guide the search. Each page read removes most of the remaining search space. The primary key orders.id gets a B-tree index orders_pkey automatically.



What the index is on disk


The index is its own relation, with its own file in the data directory, next to the heap file of the table. The file is an array of 8 kB pages, the same page size and the same page format as the heap (see chapter 11). Every page starts with the 24-byte page header and the array of 4-byte line pointers. At the end of each B-tree page there is a 16-byte special space. It holds the page number of the left sibling, the page number of the right sibling, the level of the page (0 for leaves), flag bits, and a VACUUM cycle ID.



 

  
Page 0: metapagepoints to the root page and stores the tree level

  
↓

  
Root page (level 2)p9: [−∞ → p3] 500,000 [→ p8]

  
Internal page p3 (level 1)[−∞ → p1] 367 [→ p2] 733 [→ p4] ...
Internal page p8 (level 1)[−∞ → ...] ...

  
Leaf p11, 2, 3 ... 366
high key 367
⇆
Leaf p2367 ... 732
high key 733
⇆
Leaf p4733 ...
⇆ ...

  
Leaf tuplekey 42, TID (0,42)
→TID
Heap page 0, line pointer 42the tuple of the row with id 42

 

 

  
Page 0 of every B-tree is the metapage. It does not hold keys. It stores the page number of the root and the level of the root. A search starts here. A backend keeps a copy of the metapage data in memory, so most searches go straight to the root.

  
The root is the top page. In a small index the root is also the only leaf. When the root fills up and splits, PostgreSQL makes a new root above it and changes the pointer in the metapage. The root is not at a fixed page number.

  
Internal pages hold pivot tuples. Each pivot tuple is a separator key and a downlink (the page number of a child page one level down). The first downlink on a page has no key. It stands for "minus infinity", so a page with n separators has n + 1 downlinks. The separator is the lowest key that can be in the child to its right.

  
Leaf pages are level 0. The docs say that typically over 99% of all pages are leaf pages. Each leaf page links to its left and right sibling, so each level is a doubly linked list of pages in key order. Each page except the rightmost on its level stores a high key, the upper bound for the keys on that page.

  
A leaf tuple is the key value plus the TID of the heap tuple, for example (0,42) means heap page 0, line pointer 42. The index does not hold the other columns and it does not hold visibility information. To return the row, PostgreSQL reads the heap page (chapter 18 covers when it can skip this).

 

 




The separator keys and page numbers in this picture are example values. Some details in the real layout:




Page kindWhat its tuples holdNotes


Metapage (page 0)Root page number, root level, a "fast root" pointer and a few countersFixed position at the start of the first segment file of the index.


Internal page (level 1 and up)Pivot tuples: separator key + downlink to a child pageSeparators are only for navigation. A separator can hold a key value that no longer exists in the table.


Leaf page (level 0)Key values + TID of a heap tuple. Since PostgreSQL 13, duplicates can be stored as one key with a list of TIDs (chapter 17)Linked to left and right siblings. The high key is stored as the first item of the page, so it does not move when new items are added.




Two details make every key unique inside the tree. First, the heap TID is a hidden last key column. Duplicate values are sorted by TID. Second, a separator is cut to the shortest prefix of columns that still divides the left page from the right page. The README calls this suffix truncation. It makes pivot tuples small, so more of them fit on an internal page.



High keys and right-links: searches without a tree lock


Many backends read and change the same index at the same time. A simple design would lock the whole path from root to leaf during a search, so that no split could move the keys. That would make the root a point that every backend waits on. PostgreSQL uses the algorithm of Lehman and Yao (1981) instead. It adds two things to each page. One is the right-link to the right sibling. The other is the high key. With them, a reader can find out that a page split happened after it read the parent, and correct its path.



 

  
Readersearching for key 70
→
Parentseparator 50, keys ≥ 50 go to p5

  
Writerinserts into p5, p5 is full
→
p5 splitskeys 50 to 64 stay, keys 65 to 90 move to new page p12

  
Reader reads p5high key of p5 is now 65
→70 ≥ 65
follow right-link to p1270 is here

  
Writer adds separator 65 + downlink p12 to the parentnew searches go straight to p12

 

 

  
The reader reads the parent, finds the downlink to p5, and releases the lock on the parent. It holds no lock while it moves to the child.

  
At this moment another backend splits p5. Half of the keys move to a new right sibling p12. The writer sets the right-link of p5 to p12 and sets the high key of p5 to 65. The parent does not know about p12 yet.

  
The reader arrives at p5 and compares its search key with the high key. 70 is not below 65, so the key cannot be on p5. The reader follows the right-link. It can repeat this step if the page split more than once. Keys only move to the right in a split, so moving right always finds them.

  
The writer then adds the new downlink to the parent. PostgreSQL holds a short shared lock only on the one page it reads at a time, so that the page does not change while it is read. It does not hold locks on the path.

 

 





How tall is the tree


The height depends on how many tuples fit on one page (the fan-out). Here is the arithmetic for a bigint key such as orders.id. The byte sizes come from the page layout docs and the source headers. The result is an estimate, not a measured value.




StepValue


Space for tuples on a page8,192 − 24 (page header) − 16 (B-tree special space) = 8,152 bytes


One leaf tuple8 (index tuple header: 6-byte TID + 2-byte info) + 8 (the bigint) = 16 bytes, plus a 4-byte line pointer = 20 bytes


Tuples per full page8,152 / 20 = 407


Leaf page at fillfactor 90about 366 tuples (example fan-out)


Internal page at 70% (the fill that CREATE INDEX uses for upper pages)about 284 downlinks (example fan-out)


1,000,000 rows1,000,000 / 366 = 2,733 leaf pages. 2,733 / 284 = 10 internal pages. 10 / 284 = 1 root. 3 levels.


100,000,000 rows273,225 leaves, then 963, then 4, then 1 root. 4 levels.


Maximum for 3 levels284 × 284 × 366 = about 29.5 million rows. Four levels hold about 8.4 billion.




So a point lookup in a 1,000,000-row table reads 3 index pages and 1 heap page, compared with about 10,000 heap pages for a Seq Scan. The root and the internal pages are read by every search, so they almost always stay in shared buffers (chapter 14). In practice a lookup often needs only one or two reads from disk, or none. Wider keys lower the fan-out. A text key such as an email address makes each tuple larger, so the tree can get one level taller. It still grows by only one level for each factor of a few hundred rows.



Search step by step


For WHERE id = 42:


    

  1. Read the metapage (usually from the backend's memory copy) to get the root page number.
    2. 

  2. On the root, do a binary search over the separator keys. Find the last separator that is less than or equal to 42. Follow its downlink.
    3. 

  3. On each internal page, first compare 42 with the high key. If 42 is not below it, follow the right-link (a concurrent split happened). Then do the same binary search and go down one level.
    4. 

  4. On the leaf, binary search to the first tuple with key ≥ 42. The leaf keeps all matching TIDs in the backend's memory and releases the page lock.
    5. 

  5. For each TID, read the heap page and check that the tuple is visible to the snapshot (chapter 12). Only then return the row.
    6. 



A range scan such as WHERE id BETWEEN 100 AND 5000 does the same descent for the lower bound 100. It then does not go back up the tree. It reads leaf pages to the right by the right-link until it finds a key above 5000. Internal pages are read once per scan, not once per page of results.


EXPLAIN (COSTS OFF) SELECT * FROM orders WHERE id = 42;
 Index Scan using orders_pkey on orders
   Index Cond: (id = 42)

EXPLAIN (COSTS OFF) SELECT * FROM orders ORDER BY id DESC LIMIT 10;
 Limit
   ->  Index Scan Backward using orders_pkey on orders


The second plan has no Sort node. The leaves are already in key order, so reading them from right to left by the left-links gives ORDER BY id DESC. The Limit node stops the scan after 10 rows, so the scan reads one or two leaf pages, not the whole index.



Which conditions a B-tree can use




ConditionCan use a B-tree?Why


=, <, <=, >=, >YesThese five operators define the sort order of the operator class.


BETWEEN, IN (...)YesBETWEEN is a pair of >= and <=. IN is a set of = searches.


IS NULL, IS NOT NULLYesNULLs are stored in the index. By default they sort after all other values (NULLS LAST).


LIKE 'abc%', ~ '^abc'Only with the C locale, or with an index that uses text_pattern_opsA fixed prefix is a range. Other collations do not compare strings byte by byte, so the prefix is not a range in their order.


LIKE '%abc', <>NoThere is no range of keys to search. (See chapter 20 for trigram GIN indexes.)


lower(email) = 'x'Only with an expression index on lower(email)The index stores email, not lower(email). See chapter 19.


ORDER BY on the index columnsYes, forward or backwardNo Sort node is needed. This matters most with LIMIT.




For an index on several columns, PostgreSQL 18 can also do a "skip scan" when the query has no condition on the first column. Chapter 19 covers multicolumn indexes.



Insert: find the leaf, add the tuple, split if full


An INSERT into orders first writes the heap tuple. Then, for each index on the table, it builds an index tuple (key, TID) and inserts it. The insert does the same descent as a search, but it keeps a stack of the parent pages it passed. It takes an exclusive lock on the target leaf page and puts the new tuple at its sorted position. The line pointers are kept in key order. The tuple data itself can be anywhere in the free space of the page.


If the page has no room, it must split. Some other steps come first. PostgreSQL tries to delete index tuples that are known to be dead, and it may try deduplication (chapter 17). Only if these do not free enough space does the split happen:



 

  
Leaf p5 is full50 52 55 58 60 61 64 66 ... and the new key 57 must go here

  
p5 (left)50 ... 60
high key 61
⇆
new page p12 (right)61 ... 66
high key = old high key of p5

  
Parent gets separator 61 + downlink to p12if the parent has room, the insert is done

  
Parent is full tooit splits the same way, and its middle separator moves up one more level

  
The root splitsnew root with two downlinks, metapage updated, the tree is one level taller

 

 

  
The new tuple does not fit. PostgreSQL allocates a new page at the end of the index file (or reuses a free page that VACUUM recorded).

  
It chooses a split point. For a page in the middle of the index it tries to put about half of the bytes on each side. It moves the upper part to the new right page. The new page takes over the old right-link and the old high key. The left page gets a new high key and a right-link to the new page. The old right sibling's left-link is changed to point to the new page.

  
The first key of the new page, cut short by suffix truncation, becomes the separator. It goes into the parent with a downlink to the new page. The insert finds the parent from its stack. If the parent split in the meantime, it moves right to find it.

  
If the parent is full, the parent splits. For an internal page, the separator at the split point is not copied. It moves up to the next level. The docs say page splits "cascade upwards".

  
When the root splits, PostgreSQL makes a new root one level above it and points the metapage at the new root. This is the only way the tree gets taller. It grows at the top, so all leaves are always at the same depth.

 

 





Ascending keys, random keys and the split point


The split point rule has a special case for the rightmost page of a level, from nbtsplitloc.c. If the full page is the rightmost leaf, PostgreSQL does not split 50:50. It leaves the left page fillfactor percent full. The default fillfactor for B-tree leaves is 90 and the allowed range is 10 to 100 (CREATE INDEX ... WITH (fillfactor = 70)). For a rightmost internal page it uses 70%.


This rule exists for keys that always increase, such as an identity column, a sequence or created_at. Every new key goes into the rightmost leaf. With a 50:50 split, each left page would stay half full forever, because no new key would ever go there. With the rightmost rule, pages are left 90% full and the index stays compact. A backend also caches the rightmost leaf page it inserted into. If the next key belongs on the same page, it skips the descent.


Random keys behave differently. A UUID version 4 (gen_random_uuid()) puts each new key at a random place in the index. Splits happen everywhere, and each split leaves two half-full pages. Pages fill up again over time, so the average fill settles near 70%. The comment in nbtsort.c gives 70% as the usual estimate for the steady state. There is a second cost that is often larger. Each insert needs a different leaf page, so inserts touch pages all over the index. When the index is larger than shared buffers, inserts start to read from disk. PostgreSQL 18 adds uuidv7(), which makes UUIDs that start with a millisecond timestamp. These keys increase over time, so inserts go to the right edge like an identity column.


SELECT uuidv7();   -- PostgreSQL 18: time-ordered, index-friendly
SELECT uuidv4();   -- random, same as gen_random_uuid()



Unique indexes: how the check works


A unique index, for example on customers.email, checks each new key during the insert. The heap tuple is already written at that point. The check goes like this (from nbtinsert.c):


    

  1. Descend to the first leaf page where the key could be, and take an exclusive lock on it. Every backend that inserts the same key must lock the same page, so only one of them can do the check at a time.
    2. 

  2. Look at every index tuple with an equal key. For each one, read its heap tuple. If the heap tuple is dead, it is no conflict (the index tuple can be marked dead in passing). If the heap tuple is live and committed, raise duplicate key value violates unique constraint. If it was written by a transaction that is still in progress, release the lock, wait for that transaction to end, and do the check again.
    3. 

  3. If there is no conflict, insert the new tuple while still holding the lock, so a later inserter must see it.
    4. 



After an error, the new heap tuple stays in the heap as a dead tuple until VACUUM removes it. NULL keys skip the check, because NULL is not equal to NULL. So a unique index allows many NULLs, unless you create it with UNIQUE NULLS NOT DISTINCT (since PostgreSQL 15).




 
Lab: insert keys into a B-tree and watch it split

 
Each page holds at most 4 keys or 4 downlinks. This is an example capacity so that splits are easy to see. A real 8 kB page holds hundreds. The split rules are the real ones. A middle page splits 50:50. The rightmost leaf keeps the fillfactor. The rightmost internal page keeps 70%. Try "10 ascending" a few times and look at the leaf fill. Then press Reset and try "10 random". Hover a key to see its TID. Try to insert a key that exists.

 

 

 

 

 

 
With 4 slots, 90% of a page rounds to 4 keys, so ascending pages end 100% full here. On a real page they end about 90% full.






 
Lab: step through a search on the same tree

 
This lab uses the tree from the lab above. Heap pages hold 4 tuples each (example), in the order the keys were inserted. Start a point lookup or a range scan, then press Next. Blue pages are read, with the read order. Compare the page reads with a Seq Scan, which reads every heap page. Then insert random keys above and do the same range scan again. The heap visits now jump around. In this tiny example a Seq Scan often reads fewer pages. That is also true for small real tables (chapter 18).

 

 

 
Heap pages:

 

 

 





Edge cases and limits


    

  • Maximum key size. An index tuple cannot be larger than about one third of a page (after TOAST compression). An index on a long text column can fail on insert with "index row size exceeds maximum". Index a hash of the value or a prefix instead (chapter 19).
    • 

  • Deletes do not shrink the tree at once. A DELETE does not touch the index. Dead index tuples are removed later (chapter 17). VACUUM can delete a leaf page only when it is completely empty. The tree never gets shorter by merging half-empty pages, so an index where most keys in each range were deleted stays large until REINDEX.
    • 

  • Splits and crashes. The split of one page (left page, new right page, and the left-link of the old right sibling) is one WAL record. The insert of the downlink into the parent is a second WAL record. Between the two, the left page carries an "incomplete split" flag. If a crash or an out-of-disk error happens in that gap, searches still find every key by the right-link, and the next insert that sees the flag adds the missing downlink (chapter 15 covers WAL).
    • 

  • Contention on the rightmost page. With ascending keys, every backend inserts into the same rightmost leaf page. At very high insert rates, backends wait for the exclusive lock on that one page. Random keys spread this contention but cost more I/O. This is a trade-off, not a free choice.
    • 










17. How an index is built and kept up to date


Covers: CREATE INDEX step by step (lock, heap scan, sort, bottom-up load), parallel build, CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, REINDEX and REINDEX CONCURRENTLY, index maintenance on INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE, HOT, LP_DEAD marking, simple and bottom-up deletion, deduplication, write cost, unused indexes, bloat, indexes and MVCC. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "CREATE INDEX" (including "Building Indexes Concurrently"), "REINDEX", "B-Tree Indexes" §65.1.4 "Implementation" (bottom-up deletion, deduplication), "Resource Consumption" §19.4 (maintenance_work_mem, max_parallel_maintenance_workers), "Explicit Locking" §13.3, "pg_index" catalog, "Routine Reindexing" §24.2, "Heap-Only Tuples (HOT)" §66.7, "The Cumulative Statistics System" §27.2 (pg_stat_all_indexes); source tree src/backend/access/nbtree/README, nbtsort.c, planner.c (plan_create_index_workers), REL_18_STABLE. Checked 2026-09-30.


CREATE INDEX reads the whole table once, sorts all the keys, and then writes the B-tree from the bottom up, page by page. After that, the index is kept up to date one tuple at a time. Every INSERT adds an index tuple to every index. Most UPDATEs do too. A DELETE adds nothing, but it leaves old index tuples behind for later cleanup.



CREATE INDEX step by step


Take CREATE INDEX orders_created_at_idx ON orders (created_at); on the 1,000,000-row orders table (example size). You could build the index by running the insert path from chapter 16 one million times. Each insert would descend the tree, do binary searches, and split pages. PostgreSQL does something much cheaper, in nbtsort.c:



 

  
SHARE lock on ordersSELECT goes on. INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE wait.

  
↓

  
Heap scanone pass over all heap pages, one (created_at, TID) pair per tuple to index

  
↓

  
tuplesortin memory up to maintenance_work_mem (64MB default), else sorted runs in temp files, then a merge

  
↓

  
Leaf levelfill pages left to right up to fillfactor 90%, link each to its left neighbour

  
↑

  
Upper levelsfirst key of each finished page goes up as a separator. Upper pages are filled to 70%.

  
Metapagepage 0 points to the root, the single page on the top level

 

 

  
CREATE INDEX takes a SHARE lock on the table. SHARE conflicts with the ROW EXCLUSIVE lock that INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE take, so writes wait until the build ends. It does not conflict with the ACCESS SHARE lock of SELECT. SHARE does not conflict with itself, so several plain CREATE INDEX commands can run on the same table at the same time.

  
One sequential scan reads every heap page. For each tuple that some snapshot may still need, it forms a (key, TID) pair. Tuples that are dead to every transaction are skipped. Recently dead tuples, which an older snapshot can still see, are indexed. For a HOT chain (chapter 12), the pair has the TID of the first tuple in the chain, because that is the line pointer the index must point to.

  
The pairs go into a tuplesort, ordered by key and then by TID. If they fit in maintenance_work_mem, this is a quicksort in memory. If not, the sort writes sorted runs to temporary files and merges them. A larger maintenance_work_mem means fewer runs and a faster build. For a unique index, the sort finds equal keys next to each other and the build fails with "could not create unique index".

  
The sorted stream is cut into leaf pages. Each page is filled up to the fillfactor (90 by default), then closed and written. Equal keys are merged into posting list tuples during this step (deduplication, see below). Pages are written directly and logged to WAL as whole pages. They do not go through shared buffers.

  
Each time a leaf page is finished, its first key and its page number are added to a page one level up. When that page reaches 70%, it is finished too and passes its own first key up. So all levels grow at the same time, from the left. Upper pages are packed to 70%, not 90%, so that the first inserts after the build do not split them at once.

  
At the end, one level has only one page. That page is the root. The metapage is written to point to it. No search and no page split happened during the whole build.

 

 




Why this is much faster than inserting one row at a time:


    

  • A sort costs about n log n comparisons, with good CPU cache use. One million separate inserts cost one million descents of the tree, each with binary searches on 3 pages.
    • 

  • Each index page is written once, in order. Row by row, the same leaf page is changed again and again, and every change writes a WAL record.
    • 

  • There are no page splits. With random keys, row-by-row inserts leave pages about 70% full on average. The bulk build leaves leaves 90% full. So the index is smaller, and a range scan reads fewer pages.
    • 

  • Leaf pages are written in key order, so logically adjacent pages are also next to each other in the file. The docs note that this makes a freshly built index slightly faster to read.
    • 




Parallel build


B-tree, GIN and BRIN indexes can be built by several processes. Parallel workers each scan a part of the heap and sort their part. The leader merges the sorted results and writes the tree. max_parallel_maintenance_workers (default 2) is the upper limit, and a cost model based on the table size picks the number. maintenance_work_mem is the limit for the whole build, not per worker, and each participant must get at least 32MB of it, the leader included. So with the default 64MB, the build can use at most one worker plus the leader. To use two workers, set at least 96MB for the session:


SET maintenance_work_mem = '1GB';
SET max_parallel_maintenance_workers = 4;
CREATE INDEX orders_created_at_idx ON orders (created_at);




 
Lab: the bulk build, step by step

 
A small heap with unsorted keys. Each index page holds 10 keys (example capacity). Press Next to scan, sort, pack the leaves and build the upper levels. The table at the bottom compares the result with the same keys inserted one by one, with the real split rules from chapter 16. Change the fillfactor, or switch the row-by-row order to ascending. The lab rounds the fillfactor down to whole slots.

 

 

 

 

 

 




Look at the ascending case. Row-by-row inserts of increasing keys give almost the same page count as the bulk build, because the rightmost split keeps pages at the fillfactor. They still cost one descent and one WAL record per key, plus two records per split. With random order, the index has more pages and more splits. In this lab the heights are often the same. With real fan-outs of hundreds the height is also usually the same, but the page count differs by the same ratio as here.



CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY


On a large table a plain CREATE INDEX can take minutes or hours. For all that time, every INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on the table waits. In a production app this is an outage of all writes to that table. CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY builds the index without blocking writes. It takes a SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE lock, which allows reads and writes but blocks other schema changes, VACUUM, and a second concurrent build on the same table.


The hard part is that rows change while the index is built. The docs describe how PostgreSQL does it in three transactions:



 

  
Transaction 1: add the index to the catalogpg_index: indisready = false, indisvalid = false

  
Waitfor every transaction that has changed the table to end

  
Transaction 2: first table scan and buildwith a snapshot, like a plain build. Then indisready = true

  
Waitagain, for transactions that changed the table

  
Transaction 3: second table scanadd the tuples that are missing from the index

  
Waitfor transactions with a snapshot older than the second scan

  
indisvalid = truethe planner can now use the index

 

 

  
The index is created in the catalog but is not ready. indisready = false means INSERT and UPDATE ignore it. indisvalid = false means the planner does not use it. This transaction commits so that all new transactions see that the index exists.

  
Transactions that started before the index existed do not know about it. They could make HOT updates that change the indexed column (chapter 12). The build waits for all transactions that have changed the table to finish.

  
The first scan builds the index from a snapshot, with the same sort and bottom-up load as a plain build. Writes continue during the scan, and they do not go into the index yet. At the end, indisready is set to true. From now on, every INSERT and UPDATE also adds tuples to the new index.

  
The build waits until every transaction that may have missed the "ready" state has ended. After this point, no new write can skip the index.

  
Rows written between the first snapshot and the "ready" point are in the heap but not in the index. The second scan takes a new snapshot, compares the heap with the TIDs already in the index, and inserts the missing ones. For a unique index, uniqueness is enforced against other transactions from the start of this scan.

  
Transactions with an older snapshot could get wrong results from the new index. The build waits for all of them to end.

  
The index is marked valid and the command returns. Even now, a transaction older than the build may not be able to use it yet.

 

 




The costs and the traps:


    

  • It does two table scans and waits three times. It takes longer than a plain build and adds CPU and I/O load while it runs.
    • 

  • The waits have no time limit. A session that changed the table, or holds an old snapshot, and then stays "idle in transaction" for an hour makes the build wait for an hour.
    • 

  • It cannot run inside a transaction block (BEGIN ... COMMIT). Many migration tools wrap each migration in a transaction, so you must turn that off for this step.
    • 

  • If it fails (a deadlock, a duplicate key in a unique index, a cancel), it leaves an index marked INVALID. The planner does not use it, but every write still updates it, and a unique one still enforces uniqueness. Drop it and try again, or run REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY on it.
    • 



-- find invalid indexes left behind by failed concurrent builds
SELECT indexrelid::regclass AS index, indrelid::regclass AS table
FROM pg_index WHERE NOT indisvalid;

DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY orders_created_at_idx;
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY orders_created_at_idx ON orders (created_at);


REINDEX CONCURRENTLY (since PostgreSQL 12) uses the same method to rebuild an existing index. It builds a new index with the suffix _ccnew, then swaps the names and drops the old one. If it fails, an invalid _ccnew index is left, which you drop. A plain REINDEX is worse than it looks. It blocks writes to the table and takes an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock on the index. The planner tries to take a lock on every index of a table when it plans any query on that table, so a plain REINDEX can block reads too, even reads that would not use that index.



Keeping the index up to date


After the build, the index changes one tuple at a time, with the insert path from chapter 16. What each statement does to the indexes of orders:




StatementHeapEach index


INSERTone new tupleone new index tuple in every index


UPDATE, HOTone new tuple on the same pagenothing. The index still points to the first line pointer of the HOT chain


UPDATE, not HOTone new tuple, with a new TIDone new index tuple in every index, even indexes on columns that did not change


DELETEsets xmax on the old tuplenothing. The old index tuples stay




An UPDATE is HOT only if no column used by any index changes and the new tuple fits on the same heap page (chapter 12). BRIN indexes do not count here, because they only summarize page ranges. For a non-HOT update, the docs say that changing one column covered by one index still needs a new index tuple in each and every index on the table, because each index must point to the new TID. A table with 8 indexes writes 8 index tuples for an UPDATE that changes one indexed column.




 
Lab: what one write costs

 
Set the number of B-tree indexes on orders and the kind of statement. For an UPDATE, choose whether an indexed column changes and whether the heap page has room. The WAL count is rough. It is one heap record plus one record per index insert, without full-page images.

 

 

 

 

 





How dead index tuples go away


Old index tuples point to tuples that become dead. They take space in leaf pages and every scan that finds them must visit the heap to learn that they are dead. PostgreSQL removes them in four ways:


    

  1. LP_DEAD marking by index scans. When a plain Index Scan follows an index tuple to a heap tuple that is dead to all transactions, it sets the LP_DEAD bit on the line pointer of the index tuple. The source calls this the kill_prior_tuple optimization. Later scans skip that index tuple without a heap visit. This needs only a shared lock, like a hint bit. Bitmap scans do not do it, because they do not visit the index and the heap in step. A unique check on insert also sets these bits.
    2. 

  2. Simple deletion. When an insert finds a full leaf page, it first physically removes the index tuples with LP_DEAD set. If that frees enough space, the page does not split.
    3. 

  3. Bottom-up deletion (since PostgreSQL 14). Before a split that looks like it is caused by non-HOT UPDATEs of the same rows ("version churn"), PostgreSQL checks the duplicates on the page against the heap and deletes those that are dead. The docs say the size of some indexes may never grow by even one page despite constant version churn.
    4. 

  4. VACUUM. It scans the whole index and removes every index tuple that points to a dead heap tuple it is removing (chapter 13). It is the only method that guarantees a limit on how old the remaining dead tuples can be. Only after that can the heap line pointers be reused.
    5. 




Deduplication


Since PostgreSQL 13, a B-tree leaf can store many equal keys as one posting list tuple. It holds the key once, followed by a sorted array of TIDs. An index on orders.status has only 4 distinct values in 1,000,000 rows, so it gets much smaller. Deduplication runs lazily, only when a leaf page is full and deletion did not free enough space. CREATE INDEX and REINDEX build posting lists directly. It is on by default and can be turned off per index with deduplicate_items = off. It cannot be used for numeric, jsonb, float4, float8, text with a nondeterministic collation, container types such as arrays, or indexes with INCLUDE columns.



Indexes and MVCC


An index tuple has no xmin and no xmax. The index does not know which transactions can see the tuple it points to. So an index tuple can point to a tuple that was deleted, to a tuple whose insert has not committed, or to an old version of an updated row. When a row is updated in a non-HOT way, the index has two tuples, one for each version. A search for the old key finds the old version. A search for the new key finds the new one. Only the heap tuple header decides which one the snapshot can see (chapter 12).


This has two effects. First, every Index Scan must visit the heap to check visibility, even when the index already has all the needed columns. The visibility map allows an exception, which chapter 18 explains. Second, an index cannot answer SELECT count(*) by itself, because it counts tuples, not visible rows.



The write cost of indexes and how to find unused ones


Every index makes every INSERT slower, most UPDATEs slower, and adds WAL, which also makes backups and replication (chapter 30) bigger. Every index also takes space in shared buffers. Some indexes are never used by any query. pg_stat_user_indexes.idx_scan counts the index scans started on each index:


SELECT s.indexrelid::regclass AS index, s.idx_scan, s.last_idx_scan,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid)) AS size
FROM pg_stat_user_indexes s
JOIN pg_index i ON i.indexrelid = s.indexrelid
WHERE s.idx_scan = 0 AND NOT i.indisunique
ORDER BY pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid) DESC;


Check three things before you drop an index with idx_scan = 0. The counters are per server, so a query that runs only on a standby does not count on the primary. The counters can be reset, so check when they were last reset (stats_reset in pg_stat_database). A unique index or primary key does its job on every insert even with zero scans, which is why the query above excludes them. last_idx_scan exists since PostgreSQL 16.



Index bloat and REINDEX


VACUUM removes dead index tuples, but it deletes a page only when the page is completely empty. Pages never merge. If most, but not all, keys in each key range are deleted, many pages stay allocated with only a few tuples each. The index is then larger than it needs to be, and range scans read more pages. The docs recommend periodic reindexing for such usage patterns. Typical causes are a queue table where old rows are deleted in bulk, or a random-key index after mass deletes. REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY builds a fresh, compact copy with the bulk build above and swaps it in without blocking writes.









18. Scan types: Seq Scan, Index Scan, Index Only Scan, Bitmap Scan


Covers: Seq Scan, Index Scan, heap fetch by TID, correlation, Index Only Scan, visibility map, Heap Fetches, Bitmap Index Scan, Bitmap Heap Scan, BitmapAnd and BitmapOr, lossy bitmaps and Recheck Cond, planner choice, random_page_cost, "index exists but Seq Scan", LIMIT with ORDER BY, parallel scans. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Index-Only Scans and Covering Indexes" (§11.9), "Combining Multiple Indexes" (§11.5), "Using EXPLAIN" (§14.1), "Statistics Used by the Planner" (§14.2), "pg_stats" view (correlation), "Planner Cost Constants" (§19.7.2), "Resource Consumption" (§19.4, work_mem, io_method), PostgreSQL 18 release notes; source tree src/backend/optimizer/path/costsize.c (cost_index, cost_bitmap_heap_scan, index_pages_fetched), src/backend/nodes/tidbitmap.c, src/backend/storage/buffer/README, REL_18_STABLE. Checked 2026-09-30.


A scan type is the way a plan node reads the tuples of one table. A Seq Scan reads every heap page in order. An Index Scan walks the index and fetches each matching tuple from the heap by its TID. An Index Only Scan answers from the index and reads the heap only for pages it cannot trust. A Bitmap Scan first collects all matching TIDs, sorts them by page, and then reads each heap page once in file order.



Seq Scan


A Seq Scan reads heap pages 0, 1, 2 and so on to the last page. On each page it checks the visibility of each tuple against the snapshot (chapter 12) and tests the WHERE condition. It reads every page, no matter how many rows match, so its cost does not depend on the selectivity. The planner estimates it as:


cost = pages × seq_page_cost + tuples × cpu_tuple_cost + tuples × cpu_operator_cost (per condition)
     = pages × 1.0            + tuples × 0.01           + tuples × 0.0025


Sequential reads are the cheapest reads for any storage. The operating system reads ahead, and since PostgreSQL 18 the asynchronous I/O subsystem (io_method) can queue several reads at once for sequential scans and bitmap heap scans. A large Seq Scan uses a small ring of buffers (256 kB) instead of the whole of shared buffers, so one big scan does not push every other page out of the cache (chapter 14).


EXPLAIN (COSTS OFF) SELECT * FROM orders WHERE total > 100;
 Seq Scan on orders
   Filter: (total > '100'::numeric)


"Filter" means the condition is tested on each tuple after it was read. Compare it with "Index Cond" below, where the index finds only the matching tuples.



Three ways to use an index



 

  
B-tree on orders(customer_id)descend to the first match, walk leaves to the right
→TIDs in key order
(7,3) (91,1) (7,9) (40,2) ...

  
Index Scanone TID at a time
→heap 7, 91, 7, 40
page 7 is read twicerandom order, one read per TID

  
Index Only Scanneeds only customer_id
→
visibility mappage all-visible? skip the heap
→only if not
heap

  
Bitmap Index Scanall TIDs into a bitmap, one bit per tuple, grouped by page
→
Bitmap Heap Scanpages 7, 40, 91 in file order, each read once

 

 

  
All index paths start the same way. They descend the B-tree to the first match and walk the leaf chain (chapter 16). The result is a stream of TIDs in key order. Key order is not heap order. The TIDs here are example values.

  
An Index Scan fetches the heap tuple for each TID as it comes. If the next TID is on a different page, it reads that page. If the matching rows are spread over the table, each match costs one random page read, and the same page can be read many times. The rows come out in index order, which is why an Index Scan can satisfy ORDER BY.

  
If every column the query needs is in the index, the Index Only Scan takes the values from the index tuple. It must still know if the tuple is visible. It checks the visibility map bit of the heap page. If the bit says all-visible, it skips the heap.

  
A Bitmap Index Scan reads the index to the end first and puts every TID into an in-memory bitmap. The Bitmap Heap Scan then reads the heap pages in page order, each page once, and takes the marked tuples from it. The index order is lost, so an ORDER BY needs a Sort node on top.

 

 





Index Scan


Take SELECT * FROM orders WHERE customer_id = 1 with an index orders_customer_id_idx. Ana (customer 1) has, say, 12 orders placed over two years. Orders are appended to the heap in time order, so her 12 orders are on 12 different heap pages. The Index Scan reads 3 index pages and then 12 heap pages, in the order of the index tuples, which is not the order of the pages. Each heap read is a random read. This is cheap for 12 rows. For 50,000 rows it is 50,000 random reads, and many of them read a page that was already read before and maybe already pushed out of the cache.


How spread out the matches are depends on how well the physical order of the heap follows the order of the column. ANALYZE measures this as correlation, from −1 to +1. For orders.id and orders.created_at it is near 1, because rows arrive in that order. For orders.customer_id it is near 0.


SELECT attname, correlation FROM pg_stats
WHERE tablename = 'orders' AND attname IN ('id', 'created_at', 'customer_id');


The planner uses it in cost_index. It computes the heap I/O for a perfectly uncorrelated table (every page fetch is random, random_page_cost each) and for a perfectly correlated one (one random read, then sequential reads of the next pages). It then interpolates between the two with correlation². A correlation of 0.7 counts only 0.49 of the way toward the cheap case.



Index Only Scan


If the query needs only columns that are in the index, the heap has nothing to add except visibility. The index tuple has no xmin or xmax (chapter 17). The heap has them, but reading the heap would remove the benefit. The visibility map solves this. It has 2 bits per heap page, and one of them says "all tuples on this page are visible to all current and future transactions". VACUUM sets the bit. Any INSERT, UPDATE or DELETE on the page clears it.


For each index tuple, the Index Only Scan checks the bit of its heap page. If it is set, the scan returns the values from the index. If not, it reads the heap tuple to check visibility, like an Index Scan. EXPLAIN ANALYZE counts these heap visits as Heap Fetches. The docs note that the visibility map is four orders of magnitude smaller than the heap, so it usually stays in memory.


EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, COSTS OFF, TIMING OFF, BUFFERS OFF, SUMMARY OFF)
SELECT count(*) FROM orders WHERE customer_id BETWEEN 100 AND 199;
 Aggregate (actual rows=1.00 loops=1)
   ->  Index Only Scan using orders_customer_id_idx on orders (actual rows=1012.00 loops=1)
         Index Cond: ((customer_id >= 100) AND (customer_id <= 199))
         Heap Fetches: 0
         Index Searches: 1


The row count in this output is an example. A large number in Heap Fetches means VACUUM has not run on the table since many pages changed. On a table with steady updates, part of the pages is always not all-visible. On an insert-only table, autovacuum runs because of the insert count since PostgreSQL 13, which also keeps the visibility map current. To make more queries index-only, add the extra columns to the index with INCLUDE (chapter 19).




 
Lab: Index Only Scan and the visibility map

 
The query needs only customer_id, so it runs as an Index Only Scan. The 12 heap pages and the match counts are example values. Click a page to toggle its all-visible bit. Run some UPDATEs, then VACUUM, and watch Heap Fetches.

 

 

 

 





Bitmap Index Scan and Bitmap Heap Scan


The bitmap plan has two nodes. The Bitmap Index Scan reads the index and sets one bit for each matching TID in a bitmap in memory (a TID bitmap, tidbitmap.c). It returns no rows. The Bitmap Heap Scan above it walks the bitmap in page order, reads each page with at least one bit, and takes the marked tuples. So every heap page is read at most once, and the reads go forward through the file. If the pages are close together, many reads are sequential.


EXPLAIN (COSTS OFF) SELECT * FROM orders WHERE customer_id BETWEEN 100 AND 199;
 Bitmap Heap Scan on orders
   Recheck Cond: ((customer_id >= 100) AND (customer_id <= 199))
   ->  Bitmap Index Scan on orders_customer_id_idx
         Index Cond: ((customer_id >= 100) AND (customer_id <= 199))


Bitmaps can be combined. With an index on customer_id and one on status, WHERE customer_id = 1 AND status = 'new' can build one bitmap from each index and AND them, so only pages that match both are read. OR conditions use BitmapOr. The docs warn that this is not always a win, because it reads both indexes. A single index on both columns (chapter 19) is often better.


 Bitmap Heap Scan on orders
   Recheck Cond: ((customer_id = 1) AND (status = 'new'::text))
   ->  BitmapAnd
         ->  Bitmap Index Scan on orders_customer_id_idx
               Index Cond: (customer_id = 1)
         ->  Bitmap Index Scan on orders_status_idx
               Index Cond: (status = 'new'::text)


Lossy bitmaps and Recheck Cond


The bitmap must fit in work_mem (default 4MB). If it grows too large, PostgreSQL makes parts of it lossy. For those pages it keeps only one bit per page, "read this page", and forgets which tuples matched. The Bitmap Heap Scan must then test the condition again on every tuple of a lossy page. That is the Recheck Cond line. EXPLAIN always prints it, but it only does work for lossy pages (and for index types that return candidates, such as GiST). EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows the split as Heap Blocks: exact=812 lossy=3301 (example numbers), and Rows Removed by Index Recheck counts the tuples that failed the recheck. If you see many lossy blocks, a larger work_mem for that query removes them.



How the planner chooses




ScanCost shapeBest when


Seq ScanFixed. All pages sequential, all tuples tested.A large fraction of the rows matches, or the table is small.


Index ScanGrows with each match. One random heap read per TID, less if the column is correlated.Very few rows match, or the rows are needed in index order (ORDER BY, LIMIT).


Index Only ScanLike an Index Scan, but heap reads only for pages that are not all-visible.The index has all needed columns and the table is mostly all-visible.


Bitmap ScanGrows with the number of distinct pages. Each page once, in order. Extra CPU for the bitmap and the recheck.A medium number of rows, spread over many pages. Also for combining indexes.




The crossover points depend on random_page_cost. Its default is 4.0 and seq_page_cost is 1.0. The docs explain that random reads on storage are much more than four times as expensive as sequential reads, but 4.0 assumes most random index reads are found in cache. On SSDs, and when the data mostly fits in memory, it is common practice to set random_page_cost to about 1.1. That is a practice, not a default in the docs. The docs only say that a lower value fits when data is likely to be in cache. A lower value makes index paths look cheaper, so the planner picks them for larger fractions.




 
Lab: which pages each scan reads, and what the planner picks

 
The heap has 120 pages with 40 tuples each (example sizes). The query is WHERE key < n with a B-tree on key. Green fill shows how many tuples on a page match. Numbers show the read order, "x3" means a page was read three times. Move the slider, change the layout and random_page_cost, and press Play to watch the read order.

 

 

 

 

 

 

 
The cost model is simplified. It uses the real constants (seq_page_cost 1.0, cpu_tuple_cost 0.01, cpu_index_tuple_cost 0.005, cpu_operator_cost 0.0025) and the real formulas from costsize.c for the heap part. These are the Mackert and Lohman page estimate, the correlation² interpolation for the Index Scan, and the sqrt interpolation of page cost for the Bitmap Heap Scan. It leaves out the cost of the tree descent, startup cost, the effect of effective_cache_size (it assumes the table fits in cache), and parallel plans. The correlation is computed from the layout shown.




Things to see in the lab. With the scattered layout, the Index Scan reads a page again each time the next TID is on another page, so it reads more pages than the table has once many rows match. The bitmap never reads a page twice. With the clustered layout, the Index Scan reads the pages in order and stays the cheapest for a long time. At a large fraction, every plan must read nearly every page, and the Seq Scan wins because it does the least work per tuple.



"The index exists, but PostgreSQL uses a Seq Scan"


This is often the right choice. The usual reasons:


    

  • Too many rows match. WHERE status = 'shipped' may match 70% of orders. Reading 70% of the rows through the index costs more random reads than reading the whole heap once.
    • 

  • The table is small. A table of 20 pages is read in 20 sequential reads. An index lookup costs a few index pages plus heap pages, and there is not much to save.
    • 

  • The statistics are wrong. If ANALYZE has not run since a large load, the planner guesses the row count wrong (chapter 21).
    • 

  • The condition cannot use the index. A function on the column (WHERE date(created_at) = '2026-09-30'), a type mismatch, or LIKE '%abc' (chapters 16 and 19).
    • 



To test the planner's choice, compare real timings, not only costs. SET enable_seqscan = off in one session makes the planner avoid a Seq Scan if it can, and EXPLAIN ANALYZE then shows whether the index plan is really faster. Do not use it in production code.



LIMIT with ORDER BY


SELECT * FROM orders ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20 with an index on created_at reads the index backward and stops after 20 rows. It needs about 3 index pages and up to 20 heap pages, and no Sort. This is the fastest shape for a "latest items" page. There is a trap. For WHERE customer_id = 4 ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20, the planner may also walk the created_at index and filter on customer_id, hoping to find 20 matches soon. If customer 4 (Dara) has no orders, the scan reads the whole table through the index before it can stop. An index on (customer_id, created_at) removes the problem (chapter 19).



Parallel scans


Seq Scan, Index Scan, Index Only Scan and Bitmap Heap Scan have parallel versions, where several parallel workers share the pages of one table and a Gather node combines their rows. Chapter 22 explains how.









19. Index design: multicolumn, partial, expression, covering


Covers: multicolumn B-tree and column order, skip scan (PostgreSQL 18), indexes for ORDER BY, partial and partial unique indexes, expression indexes, covering indexes with INCLUDE, why an index is not used, foreign key indexes, the write cost and redundant indexes. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Indexes" (§11.3 Multicolumn Indexes, §11.4 Indexes and ORDER BY, §11.5 Combining Multiple Indexes, §11.6 Unique Indexes, §11.7 Indexes on Expressions, §11.8 Partial Indexes, §11.9 Index-Only Scans and Covering Indexes, §11.10 Operator Classes and Operator Families, §11.12 Examining Index Usage), "Index Types" (§11.2), CREATE INDEX, "Constraints" (§5.5), "Using EXPLAIN" (§14.1), PostgreSQL 18 release notes (§E.6). Checked 2026-09-30.


Index design is the choice of which columns go into an index, in which order, for which rows, and in which form. A B-tree index keeps its entries sorted. A query gets a fast plan only when its conditions select one contiguous part of that sorted order, or a few such parts.


Chapter 16 explains the B-tree structure and chapter 18 explains the scan types. This chapter is about the shape of the index. Each index makes every INSERT and many UPDATEs slower (chapter 17). So the goal is a small number of indexes, each of which serves many of your frequent queries. The examples use the orders table (1,000,000 rows, an example number).



A multicolumn B-tree is sorted by the first column, then the second


CREATE INDEX orders_cust_created_idx ON orders (customer_id, created_at);


The index entries are sorted by customer_id. Inside one customer_id, they are sorted by created_at. Inside equal keys, the TID is the last tiebreaker. There is no separate order by created_at alone. The created_at values are sorted only inside each customer group. Step through the diagram. Each step runs one query against the same 16 index entries (sample data). Green entries are the ones the scan reads.



 

 
Index entries of (customer_id, created_at), in index order
1 | 09-011 | 09-031 | 09-081 | 09-121 | 09-132 | 09-012 | 09-042 | 09-062 | 09-092 | 09-112 | 09-143 | 09-023 | 09-043 | 09-073 | 09-103 | 09-15

 
customer_id = 2
1 | 09-011 | 09-031 | 09-081 | 09-121 | 09-132 | 09-012 | 09-042 | 09-062 | 09-092 | 09-112 | 09-143 | 09-023 | 09-043 | 09-073 | 09-103 | 09-15

 
customer_id = 2 AND created_at >= '2026-09-06'
1 | 09-011 | 09-031 | 09-081 | 09-121 | 09-132 | 09-012 | 09-042 | 09-062 | 09-092 | 09-112 | 09-143 | 09-023 | 09-043 | 09-073 | 09-103 | 09-15

 
customer_id = 2 ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 3
1 | 09-011 | 09-031 | 09-081 | 09-121 | 09-132 | 09-012 | 09-042 | 09-062 | 09-092 | 09-112 | 09-143 | 09-023 | 09-043 | 09-073 | 09-103 | 09-15

 
created_at >= '2026-09-12'
1 | 09-011 | 09-031 | 09-081 | 09-121 | 09-132 | 09-012 | 09-042 | 09-062 | 09-092 | 09-112 | 09-143 | 09-023 | 09-043 | 09-073 | 09-103 | 09-15

 

 

  
The 16 entries in index order. Each entry is customer_id | created_at and a TID (not shown). The gaps mark where customer_id changes.

  
customer_id = 2. The B-tree descends to the first entry with customer_id = 2 and reads to the right until the value changes. The matches are one contiguous run.

  
Add created_at >= '2026-09-06'. Inside the customer 2 group the entries are sorted by created_at, so the scan starts at the first entry with (2, 09-06) and stops at the end of the group. The run is shorter.

  
ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 3. The scan goes to the last entry of customer 2 and reads backward. The entries arrive in the requested order, so there is no Sort node. After 3 entries the Limit node stops the scan.

  
created_at >= '2026-09-12' with no condition on customer_id. The matches (green) are in every customer group. There is no single start point, so a normal index scan must read the whole index (yellow). With 100,000 customers the planner picks a Seq Scan, or a different index. PostgreSQL 18 skip scan changes this only when the leading column has few distinct values (see below).

 

 




The documentation gives the exact rule. Equality conditions on the leading columns, plus an inequality condition on the first column that has no equality condition, limit the part of the index that is scanned. Conditions on columns to the right of that are checked in the index. They save heap visits, because a failed entry does not need its heap tuple. But they do not make the scanned part smaller. So for (customer_id, created_at):




Query conditionWhat the index does


customer_id = ?One contiguous run.


customer_id = ? AND created_at > ?One shorter run. Both conditions set the start and the end.


customer_id = ? ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 10One run, read backward, in order. No Sort. Stops after 10 entries.


customer_id IN (1, 3)One descent per value (EXPLAIN ANALYZE in PostgreSQL 18 shows Index Searches: 2).


created_at > ?No start point. The whole index or nothing, unless skip scan applies.





Skip scan in PostgreSQL 18


Before PostgreSQL 18, a condition only on a later column could not limit the scan. PostgreSQL 18 added skip scan for B-tree. The B-tree code makes an internal equality condition that matches each value of the leading column. Then it does one search per value. Take an index on (status, created_at) and the query WHERE created_at >= '2026-09-12'. There are 4 status values. The scan does the search status = 'cancelled' AND created_at >= ..., then the same search for 'new', 'paid' and 'shipped'. It jumps over the leaf pages between the groups.


The limits, from the documentation:


    

  • It pays only when the skipped column has few distinct values. The docs say that if the column has "no more than several hundred distinct values", searches on the later column run reasonably fast. With many distinct values, the scan reads almost the whole index, and the planner usually prefers a Seq Scan.
    • 

  • The planner decides from statistics (chapter 21). It is a cost choice, not a guarantee.
    • 

  • It needs a useful condition on a later column. The internal condition is made only for a column that has no equality condition and only when a later column has a condition.
    • 

  • It can also skip inside a range. For an index on (a, b, c) and a = 5 AND b >= 42 AND c < 77, the scan can jump from the end of each (5, b) group where c >= 77 starts to the next b value. This may or may not be done.
    • 

  • It does not change the output order. The output of a skip scan on (status, created_at) is sorted by status first. It cannot serve ORDER BY created_at without a sort.
    • 



Skip scan makes a wrong column order cost less for low-cardinality leading columns. It does not replace the column order rule.



The column order rule: equality first, then the range or sort column


Put the columns that the query tests with = first. Put the column with the range condition (>, <, BETWEEN) or the ORDER BY column after them. The reason is the sort order. After a range on a column, the entries inside the range have many different values of that column. The next column is sorted only inside each single value, so across the range it is not sorted. A range on a column ends the useful ordering for every column after it.


Compare the two orders for WHERE customer_id = 42 AND created_at >= '2026-09-01':


    

  • (customer_id, created_at). The run starts at (42, '2026-09-01') and ends at the last entry of customer 42. Every entry read is a match.
    • 

  • (created_at, customer_id). The run is every entry from '2026-09-01' to the end, for all customers. The customer_id = 42 test is done on each entry in the index. If the month has 80,000 orders, the scan reads 80,000 entries to find perhaps 2 (example numbers).
    •
