When two columns both have equality conditions, their order does not change the size of the run. Then choose the order that lets other queries use the index too. (customer_id, status) also serves customer_id = ? alone. (status, customer_id) serves status = ? alone.



Indexes and ORDER BY


Only B-tree gives sorted output. By default the entries are stored ascending with NULLs last. A forward scan gives ORDER BY x ASC NULLS LAST. A backward scan gives ORDER BY x DESC NULLS FIRST, which is what DESC means by default. For one column, backward scans cover both directions. The options matter for mixed directions on more than one column:


-- serves ORDER BY status ASC, created_at DESC (forward)
-- and ORDER BY status DESC, created_at ASC (backward)
CREATE INDEX orders_status_created_desc_idx ON orders (status, created_at DESC);


A plain (status, created_at) index cannot give status ASC, created_at DESC. A scan goes one direction for all columns. Also check NULLs. ORDER BY created_at DESC NULLS LAST does not match a default index read backward, because that gives NULLs first. Declare the index as (created_at DESC NULLS LAST) for that query.


An index for ORDER BY helps most with LIMIT. An explicit Sort must read all input rows before it returns the first one. An index in the right order returns the first 10 at once and the scan stops. For a query that returns a large part of the table, a Seq Scan and a Sort is often faster than a scan in index order, because heap reads in index order are random.




 
Lab: can this index serve this query?

 
Build an index, then pick a query. The lab applies the real rules: the partial predicate must be implied, conditions must be on the plain column, the scan range comes from the equality prefix plus one range column, skip scan needs a low-cardinality skipped column, and ORDER BY must follow the index order. Distinct value counts for skip scan use the example sizes: 100,000 customers, 4 statuses, created_at nearly unique.

 

  
  

 

  

 

 

 

 
read, heap visited, returned read, rejected in the index heap visited, rejected by Filter not read

 

 

 





Partial indexes


A partial index has a WHERE clause, the predicate. It contains entries only for the rows that match the predicate.


CREATE INDEX orders_new_created_idx ON orders (created_at) WHERE status = 'new';


If 2% of the orders are new (an example number), this index is about 2% of the size of a full index. It fits in shared buffers more easily. Writes to rows outside the predicate add no entries to it, so many updates do not touch it. The work queue query uses it:


SELECT id FROM orders WHERE status = 'new' ORDER BY created_at LIMIT 50;


The planner uses a partial index only when it can prove that the query's WHERE implies the predicate. The proof is simple. It recognises the predicate when it appears as written in the query, and simple inequality implications, for example x < 1 implies x < 2. It does not prove equal expressions written in different forms. The proof happens at plan time. So a generic plan for WHERE status = $1 cannot use the index, because $1 could be any value. A custom plan with the actual value can. Chapter 10 explains generic and custom plans.


A partial unique index enforces uniqueness only among the rows that match the predicate. A customer may have many cancelled orders but only one open cart:


CREATE UNIQUE INDEX one_new_order_per_customer ON orders (customer_id) WHERE status = 'new';


Do not make one partial index per value (WHERE category = 1, WHERE category = 2, and so on). The planner must test each one at plan time and does not know they do not overlap. Use one index with the column first, or partitioning (chapter 31).



Expression indexes


An index key can be an expression of the columns. The value is computed at INSERT and at each non-HOT UPDATE and stored in the index. A search does not compute it again.


CREATE UNIQUE INDEX customers_email_lower_idx ON customers (lower(email));
SELECT * FROM customers WHERE lower(email) = lower('Ana@AcmeShop.com');


The query must use the same expression as the index. WHERE email = ... cannot use this index, and WHERE lower(email) = ... cannot use a plain index on email. The unique form also blocks two emails that differ only in case, which a plain UNIQUE constraint cannot do.


All functions and operators in an index definition must be IMMUTABLE. The result must depend only on the arguments, never on the time, a table or a setting. If the result could change, the stored key would be wrong and searches would miss rows. lower(text) is immutable. The cast created_at::date of a timestamptz is not, because the date depends on the TimeZone setting. CREATE INDEX ... ((created_at::date)) fails. Write the range form of the condition instead (see the table below).



Covering indexes with INCLUDE


An Index Only Scan (chapter 18) returns values from the index without reading the heap, if the visibility map says the heap page is all-visible. It needs every column the query uses to be in the index. INCLUDE adds columns as payload:


CREATE INDEX orders_cust_created_incl_idx ON orders (customer_id, created_at) INCLUDE (total);
SELECT created_at, total FROM orders WHERE customer_id = 42 ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 20;


Payload columns are stored only in the leaf pages. Suffix truncation removes them from the upper levels, so the inner pages stay small. They are not part of the key. They do not affect the sort order, the search or uniqueness. UNIQUE (customer_id) INCLUDE (total) is unique on customer_id alone. They also do not need a B-tree operator class, so any type can go there. The limits: expressions cannot be INCLUDE columns, only B-tree, GiST and SP-GiST support INCLUDE, and a too-wide index entry makes the INSERT fail. Add payload only if the table changes slowly. If the heap page must be read for the visibility check anyway, the payload saves nothing.



Why an index is not used


Run ANALYZE first, then EXPLAIN. If the plan still shows a Seq Scan, set enable_seqscan = off in your session only to test. If the planner still does not use the index, the query cannot use it (a cause in the first part of the table). If it now uses it, compare the real times with EXPLAIN ANALYZE. The planner may be right, or its estimates may be wrong.




CauseWhyFix


Function on the column: WHERE date(created_at) = '2026-09-01'The index stores created_at, not date(created_at). The planner does not reverse functions.created_at >= '2026-09-01' AND created_at < '2026-09-02', or an expression index if the function is immutable.


Cast on the column: WHERE id::text = '42'Same cause. The cast is a function on every row.Cast the value, not the column: WHERE id = 42.


Type mismatch: WHERE customer_id = 42.042.0 is numeric. The operator becomes numeric = numeric and the bigint column is cast. The B-tree operator family for integers does not contain it. Drivers that send a parameter with the wrong type cause the same thing.Send the parameter with the column's type.


Leading wildcard: WHERE email LIKE '%@acmeshop.com'A B-tree can use only a fixed prefix of the pattern. With no prefix there is no start point.A trigram GIN index: CREATE INDEX ON customers USING gin (email gin_trgm_ops) (extension pg_trgm, chapter 20).


Prefix LIKE under a non-C collation: WHERE email LIKE 'ana%'The default operator class sorts by the collation rules, and LIKE compares character by character. The planner can make a range from the prefix only in the C collation.CREATE INDEX ON customers (email text_pattern_ops). Keep a default index too if you need < or > or ORDER BY.


Low selectivity: WHERE status = 'paid'If the value is in more than a few percent of the rows, a Seq Scan reads fewer pages than random heap visits.Not a bug. A partial index for the rare values, or a composite index that makes the query selective.


OR across columns: WHERE customer_id = 42 OR status = 'new'One index scan uses only AND-ed conditions on its own columns.An index on each column. The planner combines them with a BitmapOr. Or rewrite as UNION. (PostgreSQL 18 turns an OR on one column into = ANY, which one index can use.)


Small tableThe whole table fits in a few pages. No plan beats reading them.Nothing to fix.


Stale statisticsThe planner thinks the table or the value has a different size than it has, for example just after a bulk load.ANALYZE orders. Check last_autoanalyze (chapter 32). Raise the statistics target for skewed columns (chapter 21).


Wrong column order: index (created_at, customer_id), query WHERE customer_id = 42No condition on the leading column. Skip scan does not help with a nearly unique leading column.An index with the equality column first.


Null-safe comparison: WHERE customer_id IS NOT DISTINCT FROM 42B-tree supports < <= = >= >, IS NULL and IS NOT NULL. IS [NOT] DISTINCT FROM is not in the list.Use customer_id = 42 when the value is not NULL, and customer_id IS NULL when it is.


INVALID indexA failed CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY leaves an index marked INVALID. The planner ignores it, but every write still updates it.Find it with \d orders or pg_index.indisvalid = false. DROP INDEX CONCURRENTLY and build it again, or REINDEX INDEX CONCURRENTLY.




A condition that an index can use as a search condition is called sargable. Most rows of the table follow one rule. The column must stand alone on one side of an operator that belongs to the index's operator class.




 
Lab: sargable predicate checker

 
Choose which indexes exist and the database collation. Each WHERE clause shows which index can serve it, or why none can and the rewrite that fixes it.

 

 

 





Foreign key columns need their own index


A foreign key constraint does not create an index on the referencing column. PostgreSQL leaves the choice to you. But each DELETE FROM customers or change of customers.id must find the orders that reference the row. Without an index on orders.customer_id, that is a Seq Scan of 1,000,000 orders for each deleted customer. It happens inside the delete and holds its locks for that time. Joins from customers to orders need the same index. (customer_id, created_at) serves both, because customer_id is its leading column. The same holds for order_items.product_id. order_items.order_id is already covered by the primary key (order_id, product_id).



The cost side: fewer, better indexes


Each index gets a new entry on every INSERT and on each UPDATE that is not HOT (chapter 17). VACUUM must remove the old entries later. An index on a column that changes often can also stop HOT updates. Each index takes space in shared buffers and in the WAL. So remove what does not earn its place:


    

  • Redundant indexes. (customer_id) is redundant when (customer_id, created_at) exists, because the longer index serves every query the short one serves. It is a little larger, so single-column searches are a little slower. The primary key and UNIQUE constraints already create a unique index. A second index on the same column is a copy.
    • 

  • Unused indexes. pg_stat_user_indexes.idx_scan = 0 over a long time (check the standbys too) points to an index you can drop. Keep the ones that enforce a constraint (chapter 32).
    • 

  • Index combination. The planner can AND or OR the bitmaps of separate indexes (chapter 18). Two single-column indexes on x and y serve queries on x, on y and on both. A (x, y) index is faster for queries on both, and serves x alone. The docs advise that indexes with more than three columns are rarely useful.
    • 










20. Other index types: Hash, GIN, GiST, SP-GiST, BRIN


Covers: Hash, GIN (jsonb, arrays, full-text search, trigrams, the pending list), GiST (ranges, nearest-neighbour search, exclusion constraints), SP-GiST, BRIN (summaries, correlation, summarization), comparison, extension index types. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Index Types" (§11.2), "Multicolumn Indexes" (§11.3), "Index-Only Scans and Covering Indexes" (§11.9), "GiST Indexes" (§65.2), "SP-GiST Indexes" (§65.3), "GIN Indexes" (§65.4), "BRIN Indexes" (§65.5), "Hash Indexes" (§65.6), "JSON Types" (§8.14.4 jsonb Indexing), "Preferred Index Types for Text Search" (§12.9), pg_trgm (§F.35), btree_gist (§F.8), bloom (§F.6), CREATE INDEX (storage parameters), CREATE TABLE (WITHOUT OVERLAPS), "Client Connection Defaults" (gin_pending_list_limit), PostgreSQL 10 and 18 release notes, src/backend/access/gist/README, src/backend/access/gin/ginarrayproc.c, the pgvector README. Checked 2026-09-30.


PostgreSQL has six built-in index types: B-tree, Hash, GiST, SP-GiST, GIN and BRIN. B-tree is the default and serves values that can be sorted. The other types exist for questions that a sorted list cannot answer well: "which rows contain this element", "which shapes overlap this one", "what is nearest to this point", and "which part of a huge table can I skip".


You choose the type with USING. The operator class of each column decides which operators the index supports. An index is used only when the query uses one of those operators on the indexed column. tags @> ARRAY['wool'] can use a GIN index on tags. tags[1] = 'wool' cannot. The examples add some columns to the shop schema. Each one is marked where it first appears.



Hash


A Hash index stores a 32-bit hash code of each value, not the value. The hash code picks a bucket. A search computes the hash of the search value and goes straight to that bucket page, without the descent through B-tree levels. Inside a page the entries are sorted by hash code, so a binary search finds them. When a bucket is full, overflow pages are chained to it. When the index grows, one bucket at a time is split into two.


-- example column: orders.tracking_url text, long URLs, looked up only by equality
CREATE INDEX orders_tracking_url_hash ON orders USING hash (tracking_url);
SELECT id FROM orders WHERE tracking_url = 'https://track.example.com/...';


    

  • Operators. Only =. No ranges, no ORDER BY, no LIKE.
    • 

  • Limits. One column only. Cannot be UNIQUE. No Index Only Scan, because the value is not stored. Every scan is lossy, so the heap tuple is checked again.
    • 

  • Size. Each entry holds 4 bytes of hash, however long the value. For long keys (URLs, long strings) a Hash index can be much smaller than a B-tree.
    • 

  • Failure modes. Many rows with the same value all land in one bucket and make a long overflow chain, which can be slower than a B-tree. Bucket splits happen during your INSERT. The index never shrinks without REINDEX.
    • 

  • Crash safety. Since PostgreSQL 10, Hash indexes are WAL-logged. Before that they were not crash-safe and were not replicated to standbys. Old advice "never use Hash indexes" comes from that time.
    • 



B-tree stays the default because one B-tree does equality, ranges, sorting, uniqueness, multicolumn keys and Index Only Scans. B-tree deduplication also keeps repeated values small. Choose Hash only for equality on long values in a large table, and measure first.



GIN: the inverted index


Some values contain many parts: an array of tags, a jsonb document, the words of a text. The query asks which rows contain a given part. A B-tree on the whole value cannot answer that, because the part can be anywhere inside the value. GIN (Generalized Inverted Index) turns the relation around. It stores each part (a key) once, with the list of TIDs of the rows that contain it.


-- example column: products.tags text[]
ALTER TABLE products ADD COLUMN tags text[] NOT NULL DEFAULT '{}';
CREATE INDEX products_tags_gin ON products USING gin (tags);
SELECT id, name FROM products WHERE tags @> ARRAY['outdoor', 'waterproof'];



 

  
(0,1) Trail shoe{outdoor, shoes, waterproof}
(0,2) Rain jacket{outdoor, jacket, waterproof}
(0,3) City sneaker{shoes, casual}

  
↓ extractValue: one key per array element(0,1) gives 3 keys, (0,2) gives 3 keys, (0,3) gives 2 keys

  
Entry tree: a B-tree over the keyscasual · jacket · outdoor · shoes · waterproof

  
outdoor → (0,1) (0,2)posting list, inside the leaf entry
shoes → (0,1) (0,3)posting list
waterproof → (0,1) (0,2) …a long list becomes a posting tree

  
Pending listnew entries, unsorted, merged later

  
tags @> '{outdoor,waterproof}'look up 2 keys, intersect: (0,1) (0,2)

 

 

  
Three rows of products with their TIDs. Each tags value is an item with several elements.

  
The operator class extracts keys from each item. For arrays a key is an element. For jsonb_ops it is each key and each value. For tsvector it is each lexeme. For pg_trgm it is each 3-character group.

  
Inside, a GIN index is a B-tree over the keys. Each key is stored once, no matter how many rows contain it. That is why GIN is compact when keys repeat.

  
Each key points to the TIDs of the rows that contain it. A short list is stored in the leaf entry (a posting list). A long list moves to its own B-tree of TIDs (a posting tree). The lists are sorted by TID and compressed.

  
One new row adds one entry per key, in many places of the tree. To make writes cheaper, with fastupdate on (the default) new entries go to an unsorted pending list first. VACUUM, autoanalyze, gin_clean_pending_list(), or a list larger than gin_pending_list_limit (default 4MB) moves them into the tree in bulk.

  
A query extracts keys from the search value, looks up each key, and combines the TID lists. For @> a row must have all keys, so the lists are intersected. For && any key is enough, so they are joined. The pending list is scanned too. The result goes to a Bitmap Heap Scan (GIN is read only through bitmaps).

 

 





 
Lab: GIN lookup and the pending list

 
Click tags to build the search array. Switch between @> (contains all) and && (overlaps). Insert a row with fastupdate on and watch the search scan the pending list until VACUUM merges it.

GIN in the shop, with the operator class and operators:




DataIndexOperators


Arrays (products.tags)USING gin (tags), array_ops@>, <@, &&, =


jsonb (example column products.attrs jsonb)USING gin (attrs), jsonb_ops (default)@>, ?, ?|, ?&, @?, @@


jsonb, containment onlyUSING gin (attrs jsonb_path_ops)@>, @?, @@


Full-text searchUSING gin (to_tsvector('english', name))@@ with a tsquery


Substring search (customers.email)USING gin (email gin_trgm_ops) (extension pg_trgm)LIKE, ILIKE, ~, ~*, =, similarity %




jsonb_ops vs jsonb_path_ops. jsonb_ops makes a separate key for each JSON key and each value. So attrs ? 'color' (does the key exist) works. jsonb_path_ops makes one key per value. The key is a hash of the value and the path of keys that leads to it. For {"color": "red"} it stores one hash of color and red together. It is usually much smaller and more selective for @>, because the search looks up one key instead of intersecting a common key list ("color") with a value list ("red"). It cannot answer key-exists queries, and it makes no entry for structures with no value, such as {"a": {}}.


Full-text search. A tsvector is a sorted list of lexemes (normalised words). GIN stores one key per lexeme and is the preferred index for text search. It does not store the weight labels, so a query that uses weights rechecks the heap row. GiST can also index tsvector, but it stores a fixed-size signature per document and is lossy (see below).


Trigrams. pg_trgm splits a string into groups of 3 characters (each word gets two spaces in front and one after). A LIKE '%acmeshop%' query becomes "rows that have all the trigrams of acmeshop", followed by a recheck. This is the fix for the leading-wildcard LIKE from chapter 19. A pattern with no complete trigram, such as '%ab%', becomes a scan of the whole index.


The write cost. One INSERT with 10 tags adds 10 entries in 10 places of the tree. That is why GIN is slow to update and why the pending list exists. The pending list has two costs. Every search must read the whole pending list in addition to the tree, so a large list makes searches slow. And the INSERT that pushes the list over gin_pending_list_limit does the merge itself and is much slower than the others. If you need even response times, let autovacuum clean the list or set fastupdate = off for that index. For a bulk load, drop the GIN index and build it again after the load. The build is faster with a large maintenance_work_mem, and PostgreSQL 18 can build GIN indexes in parallel. GIN supports multicolumn indexes. Unlike B-tree, the column order does not matter. GIN has no Index Only Scans, because an entry holds only a part of the value.



GiST: a tree of bounding keys


GiST (Generalized Search Tree) is a balanced tree for data that has no single sort order: rectangles, points, ranges, IP networks. Each entry in an inner page holds a key that covers every entry below it. For boxes the key is a bounding box. For ranges it is a range that contains all child ranges. A search goes down into every child whose key can match the query. The keys of neighbour entries can overlap, so a search can follow more than one path. The quality of the index depends on how little the keys overlap. The operator class decides this through its Penalty (where to insert) and PickSplit (how to split a full page) functions.


    

  • Ranges and geometry. range_ops supports && (overlaps), @>, <@, <<, >> and more. The geometric classes (box_ops, point_ops, poly_ops, circle_ops) support the operators for "left of", "contains", "overlaps".
    • 

  • Nearest neighbour. ORDER BY location <-> point '(52.52,13.40)' LIMIT 5 returns the 5 nearest stores (example column stores.location point). The index returns the rows in distance order, so the scan stops after 5. A B-tree cannot do this.
    • 

  • Exclusion constraints. A UNIQUE constraint says "no two rows are equal". An exclusion constraint says "no two rows have this operator true". A product may have only one active promotion at a time:

    CREATE EXTENSION btree_gist;   -- GiST operator classes for bigint, text, timestamptz, ...
CREATE TABLE promotions (       -- example table
  product_id bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES products(id),
  active     tstzrange NOT NULL,
  pct_off    int NOT NULL,
  EXCLUDE USING gist (product_id WITH =, active WITH &&)
);
    
The index checks each new row against the existing ones. A second promotion for the same product with an overlapping period fails with conflicting key value violates exclusion constraint. btree_gist is needed because product_id is a plain bigint, and core PostgreSQL has no GiST operator class for = on it. PostgreSQL 18 adds the short form PRIMARY KEY (product_id, active WITHOUT OVERLAPS), which behaves like the same GiST exclusion constraint.
    • 

  • PostGIS, the geographic extension, builds its spatial indexes on GiST.
    • 



GiST supports multicolumn indexes (the first column matters most), INCLUDE, and Index Only Scans for some operator classes. For text search it is lossy. It stores a fixed-length signature (default 124 bytes) where each word sets one bit, so two words can set the same bit and give a false match that the heap recheck removes. Build speed does not depend on maintenance_work_mem, but some operator classes (btree_gist, and ranges since PostgreSQL 18) support a faster sorted build.



SP-GiST: space partitioned, no overlap


SP-GiST (space-partitioned GiST) splits the search space again and again into parts that do not overlap. Examples are a quadtree (each point goes to one of four quadrants around a centre point), a k-d tree, and a radix tree for text (each level adds the next characters, and a node can store a common prefix). Because the parts do not overlap, a search follows one path for an exact value. The tree is not balanced. SP-GiST maps its many small nodes onto pages so that a search reads few pages.


    

  • IP networks. inet_ops: ip <<= inet '203.0.113.0/24' (is inside the network) on an example column logins.ip inet.
    • 

  • Text prefixes. text_ops supports ^@ (starts with), =, < and >: WHERE sku ^@ 'SHOE-'.
    • 

  • Points and ranges. quad_point_ops (default), kd_point_ops, range_ops, box_ops, with nearest-neighbour ordering for points.
    • 



SP-GiST does not support multicolumn indexes. It fits data that divides cleanly, such as points spread over an area, or strings with shared prefixes. For heavily overlapping shapes, GiST is the right tool.



BRIN: a summary per range of pages


BRIN (Block Range INdex) does not point to rows at all. It divides the heap into block ranges of pages_per_range consecutive pages (default 128). For each range it stores a summary. For sortable types the summary is the minimum and the maximum value in the range (the minmax operator classes). A query reads the summaries, skips every range whose min to max cannot contain a match, and reads all pages of the other ranges with a Bitmap Heap Scan. BRIN is lossy, so every row in a read range is checked again against the condition.


CREATE INDEX orders_created_brin ON orders USING brin (created_at);
SELECT count(*) FROM orders WHERE created_at >= '2026-09-10' AND created_at < '2026-09-12';


The index has one entry per range, so it is tiny. It is small enough that scanning all of it costs little compared with a Seq Scan. But it works only when the column's value correlates with the physical row order. orders is append-only by time. New orders go to the end of the heap, so each range holds a narrow window of created_at. A column such as customer_id has every value in every range. Its summaries would each span almost the whole domain and no range could be skipped. pg_stats.correlation (chapter 21) shows how close the column is to the physical order.



 
Lab: BRIN range skipping

 
12 block ranges of 128 pages (the default). Each range shows the min and max of created_at from its sample rows (20 sample rows per range, example data for September 2026). Pick a date range. Then move the slider to scatter rows to random places, as updates that move rows or a load in random order do.

 

 
0%
  

 

 




What to know about BRIN in production:


    

  • Updates scatter values. An UPDATE that is not HOT writes the new tuple wherever there is free space, often in an old range. That range's summary widens to include the new value. Summaries only grow. They shrink only when you run brin_desummarize_range() and summarize again, or rebuild the index.
    • 

  • New ranges are not summarized at once. When the heap grows into a new range, that range gets no summary until VACUUM (or autovacuum) runs, or you call brin_summarize_new_values(). An unsummarized range is always read. With autosummarize = on (default off), an insert into the next range asks autovacuum to summarize the previous one.
    • 

  • Precision vs size. A smaller pages_per_range gives more, narrower summaries and skips more, but the index grows. The minmax_multi classes store several min to max intervals per range, which helps when a few outliers widen a range. The bloom classes store a Bloom filter per range. They support only equality, but the values in a range do not need to be close together.
    • 

  • Good for very large, append-only tables queried by time or by a sequence: event logs, sensor data, old orders. Not good for point lookups, uncorrelated columns, or tables with many updates.
    • 




Comparison

GINB-tree of keys, each with a posting list or posting tree of TIDs@> <@ && ? @@, trigram LIKEArrays, jsonb, full-text search, substring searchTables with heavy writes to the indexed column, sortingCompact when keys repeat. Slowest to update, softened by the pending list


GiSTBalanced tree of keys that cover their children and can overlap&& @> <@ << >>, <-> orderingRanges, geometry, nearest neighbour, exclusion constraintsPlain equality (use B-tree), heavily overlapping dataMedium to large. Can be lossy


SP-GiSTUnbalanced tree of non-overlapping partitions<<= ^@ << >>, <-> for pointsPoints, IP networks, text prefixesOverlapping shapes, multicolumnMedium


BRINOne summary (min and max) per range of pages< <= = >= > (minmax)Huge append-only tables, a column that follows the physical orderUncorrelated columns, point lookups, updated tablesTiny. Almost no write cost. Lossy





Index types from extensions


bloom (contrib, §F.6) builds one Bloom-filter signature per row over many columns. One bloom index can answer equality on any combination of those columns, where B-tree would need many indexes. It supports only equality and is lossy. pgvector is a third-party extension, not part of PostgreSQL. It adds a vector type and two approximate nearest-neighbour index types for embeddings: HNSW (a multilayer graph, better speed for a given recall, slower to build) and IVFFlat (lists around cluster centres, faster to build, needs data in the table before the build). Approximate means a search can miss some of the true nearest rows.








21. The planner: statistics and cost


Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Planner/Optimizer" (§51.5), "Using EXPLAIN" (§14.1), "Statistics Used by the Planner" (§14.2), "Controlling the Planner with Explicit JOIN Clauses" (§14.3), "Query Planning" (§19.7), "Row Estimation Examples" (§69.1), "Multivariate Statistics Examples" (§69.2), "pg_stats" (§53.29), "ANALYZE", "CREATE STATISTICS", "Vacuuming" (§19.10), "CREATE TABLE" (temporary tables), "Genetic Query Optimization" (§61.3); source src/backend/optimizer/path/costsize.c, src/backend/commands/analyze.c, src/include/utils/selfuncs.h, src/backend/statistics/dependencies.c (REL_18_STABLE). Checked 2026-09-30.


The planner is the part of PostgreSQL that chooses how to run a query. It builds many plans that all give the same result, estimates a cost for each, and keeps the cheapest. The estimates come from statistics about each table that ANALYZE collects in advance.


The planner cannot run the plans to see which is fastest. That would take longer than the query. So it predicts. Its prediction has two parts. The first is how many rows each step will produce (the row estimate). The second is how much work each step costs for that many rows (the cost model). Almost every bad plan in production comes from a bad row estimate, not from a bad cost model. So most of this chapter is about where the row estimates come from and how they go wrong.



What the planner must decide




DecisionChoicesChapter


How to read each tableSeq Scan, Index Scan, Index Only Scan, Bitmap Heap Scan (and which index)18


In which order to join the tablesFor n tables, a very large number of orders and tree shapesthis chapter


How to do each joinNested Loop, Hash Join, Merge Join23


How to group and sortHashAggregate or sort then GroupAggregate; full Sort, top-N sort, Incremental Sort, or an index that is already in order24


Whether to run in parallelGather with 0, 1, 2 ... workers22






 

  
ANALYZEreads a random sample
→
pg_statistic / pg_statsper column
pg_classrelpages, reltuples

  
↓

  
selectivity of each conditionfraction of rows that pass, from MCVs and histograms

  
↓ × table rows

  
row estimate for each plan node

  
↓ + cost constants

  
cost of each pathstartup cost .. total cost

  
↓ for each join level

  
join searchdynamic programming, or GEQO for 12+ items

  
↓

  
cheapest path → plan tree

 

 

  
ANALYZE (run by you or by autovacuum) reads a sample of the table and stores a summary per column in pg_statistic. The readable view is pg_stats. The table size is in pg_class.

  
For each WHERE condition, a selectivity function looks at the summary and returns a fraction between 0 and 1. For = it is eqsel, for < it is scalarltsel. The function is stored with the operator in pg_operator.

  
Rows = table rows × selectivity. For a join, rows = outer rows × inner rows × join selectivity. Each estimate feeds the next level up, so one error at the bottom grows as it moves up the plan.

  
The cost model turns rows and pages into a number. Each path gets two numbers, the startup cost (work before the first row comes out) and the total cost (work to return all rows).

  
For joins, the planner builds the best path for each single table, then for each pair, then for each group of three, and so on. It keeps only the cheapest path for each set of tables (plus paths with a useful sort order).

  
The cheapest complete path becomes the plan tree that the executor runs (chapter 22). EXPLAIN shows it with the estimates. EXPLAIN ANALYZE adds the real numbers so you can compare (chapter 25).

 

 





Statistics: what ANALYZE stores


ANALYZE does not read the whole table. It reads a random sample of 300 × statistics target rows. With the default default_statistics_target = 100, that is 30,000 rows, whether the table has 100,000 rows or 1 billion. The number 300 comes from a result about histogram error that is cited in analyze.c. A sample of this size keeps the error in each histogram bucket small, and the size of the table has only a weak effect. The largest target of any column decides the sample size for the table.


The table size lives in pg_class, in relpages (pages) and reltuples (rows), as of the last VACUUM or ANALYZE. At plan time, the planner also reads the real current number of pages (a cheap call) and scales reltuples by it. So a table that doubled in size since the last ANALYZE still gets a row count close to the truth. The value distribution does not get this correction.


Per column, pg_stats shows:




ColumnMeaningExample for orders.status (example values)


null_fracFraction of rows where the column is NULL0


n_distinctNumber of distinct values. A negative value is a fraction of the row count. -1 means every row is different (a unique column), -0.5 means half as many distinct values as rows. The negative form lets the estimate grow with the table.4


most_common_vals, most_common_freqsThe MCV list, with the most common values and the fraction of rows for each. At most "statistics target" entries.{shipped,paid,cancelled,new} / {0.85,0.12,0.025,0.005}


histogram_boundsValues that split the rest of the rows (not in the MCV list) into buckets of equal row count. At most "statistics target" buckets.none (all values are in the MCV list)


correlationA value from -1 to +1 that shows how well the physical order of the rows follows the sorted order of the column. Near ±1 makes an index scan cheaper, because the heap pages are read in order.near 0; for created_at it is near 1, because new rows go to the end of the heap




Autovacuum runs ANALYZE on a table when the number of changed rows since the last ANALYZE passes autovacuum_analyze_threshold + autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor × rows, which is 50 + 10% of the table by default. For the 1,000,000-row orders table that is 100,050 changed rows.



From statistics to row estimates


The docs chapter "Row Estimation Examples" works through the real functions on the tenk1 table of the regression test database (10,000 rows). The numbers below are from that chapter.


Equality with a value in the MCV list. WHERE stringu1 = 'CRAAAA'. The MCV list has CRAAAA with frequency 0.003. The selectivity is that frequency. Rows = 10,000 × 0.003 = 30.


Equality with a value not in the MCV list. WHERE stringu1 = 'xxx'. The planner knows the value is not one of the 10 MCVs, which cover 0.03033 of the rows. It assumes the rest is spread evenly over the other distinct values (n_distinct 676):


selectivity = (1 - sum(mcv_freqs)) / (n_distinct - num_mcv)
            = (1 - 0.03033333) / (676 - 10) = 0.0014559
rows        = 10000 × 0.0014559 = 15


Range with a histogram. WHERE unique1 < 1000. The column is unique, so there is no MCV list. The histogram has 10 buckets: {0,993,1997,3050,...,9995}. The value 1000 is in the second bucket (993 to 1997). The planner counts the full first bucket and a straight-line fraction of the second:


selectivity = (1 + (1000 - 993) / (1997 - 993)) / 10 = 0.100697
rows        = 10000 × 0.100697 = 1007


Two conditions with AND. WHERE unique1 < 1000 AND stringu1 = 'xxx'. The planner assumes the two conditions are independent and multiplies:


selectivity = 0.100697 × 0.0014559 = 0.0001466
rows        = 10000 × 0.0001466 = 1


For a non-unique column the real function combines both parts. It checks the condition against each MCV exactly, uses the histogram for the rest, and adds them weighted by the share of rows each part covers. The independence assumption in the last example is the weak point. It is right for unrelated columns and badly wrong for related ones. Lab 2 below shows how wrong.


For a join, the docs example t1.unique2 = t2.unique2 with two unique columns and no MCVs gives selectivity 1 / max(rows1, rows2), so 50 outer rows × 10,000 inner rows × 0.0001 = 50 join rows.



The cost model


Cost has no unit of time. By convention one sequential page read costs 1.0, and everything else is set relative to that. These are the settings, with their PostgreSQL 18 defaults:




SettingDefaultCharged for


seq_page_cost1.0each page read as part of a sequential run


random_page_cost4.0each page read out of order. The docs say random reads on disk are much more than 4 times slower, but 4.0 assumes most random reads (such as index reads) hit the cache.


cpu_tuple_cost0.01processing each row


cpu_index_tuple_cost0.005processing each index entry


cpu_operator_cost0.0025each operator or function call, such as one = test


effective_cache_size4GBNot a cost. It is the planner's guess of how much of the data fits in shared buffers plus the OS cache. Higher values make repeated index page reads look cheaper. It allocates nothing.




The Seq Scan formula from cost_seqscan in costsize.c:


total cost = relpages × seq_page_cost
           + reltuples × (cpu_tuple_cost + cpu_operator_cost × number of filter operators)
startup cost = 0


The docs example: tenk1 has 345 pages and 10,000 rows, so 345 × 1.0 + 10000 × 0.01 = 445. With WHERE unique1 < 7000 it adds 10,000 × 0.0025 = 25, so 470. For acmeshop, say orders has 1,000,000 rows in 8,400 pages (example numbers):


EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'new';

 Seq Scan on orders  (cost=0.00..20900.00 rows=5000 ...)
   Filter: (status = 'new'::text)

 8400 × 1.0  +  1000000 × 0.01  +  1000000 × 0.0025  =  8400 + 10000 + 2500  =  20900
 rows = 1000000 × 0.005 (MCV frequency of 'new') = 5000


Note that the filter lowers the row estimate but not the cost. A Seq Scan must read every page and test every row, however few pass.


An Index Scan has two parts of cost, walking the index and fetching heap pages for each match. The heap part in cost_index is the interesting one. If the table order has no relation to the index order (correlation 0), each match may be on a different page. The planner estimates the number of distinct pages with the Mackert and Lohman formula, which also accounts for pages that stay in cache (from effective_cache_size), and charges random_page_cost for each. If the order matches perfectly (correlation ±1), the matches sit on neighbouring pages, so the cost is one random read and then sequential reads. For values in between, it interpolates using correlation squared. Lab 1 uses this formula.




 
Lab 1: Seq Scan or Index Scan?

 
Move the selectivity and watch where the Index Scan stops being cheaper. Then lower random_page_cost (as people do for SSDs) or raise the correlation and watch the crossover move. The defaults are the orders example, 1,000,000 rows in 8,400 pages. The model is simplified. The index part is index pages read × random_page_cost + index entries × (cpu_index_tuple_cost + cpu_operator_cost) without the small B-tree descent charge; the index has 1 leaf page per 300 entries (example); Bitmap Heap Scan (chapter 18) is left out. The heap part is the real cost_index formula for a single scan.

 

  

   

   

   

  

  

   

   

   

  

 

 

 




Three results from the lab are worth remembering. First, the crossover is at a low selectivity when correlation is near 0. An index scan that returns more than a few percent of an unordered table is usually slower than reading it all. Second, random_page_cost moves the crossover a lot. On SSDs many teams set it between 1.1 and 2 (a common practice, not a default), which makes index scans win more often. Third, correlation matters as much as selectivity. The same query on created_at (correlation near 1) can use an index for a much larger share of the table.



Startup cost, total cost and LIMIT


EXPLAIN prints cost=startup..total. The startup cost is the work before the first row comes out. A Seq Scan starts at 0. A Sort must read all its input before it can return anything, so its startup cost is almost its total cost. Usually the planner compares total costs. With LIMIT, or with a cursor, it compares the cost to get only the first rows. The Limit node's cost is startup + (total - startup) × (rows wanted / rows expected).


SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'new' ORDER BY created_at LIMIT 10;


Plan A: Seq Scan all 1,000,000 rows, keep the 5,000 new rows, top-N sort, return 10. High startup cost. Plan B: walk an index on created_at from the oldest entry and stop at the tenth new row. The planner assumes new rows are spread evenly (selectivity 0.005), so it expects to read about 10 / 0.005 = 2,000 entries. Plan B looks far cheaper.


But new orders are the newest orders. They are all at the end of the created_at order. Plan B reads almost the whole index and fetches almost every heap page before it finds one new row. This is a classic failure. LIMIT is combined with a filter on a value that is correlated with the sort order. The statistics cannot see this relationship between two columns. Two fixes help. One is an index on (status, created_at) (chapter 19), which serves the filter and the order at once. The other is a partial index WHERE status = 'new'.



Join order: dynamic programming and GEQO


The number of join orders grows very fast. Counting only left-deep orders (join one new table at each step), 4 tables have 4! = 24 orders and 10 tables have 10! = 3,628,800. The planner uses dynamic programming. Level 1 holds the best paths to read each single table. Level 2 builds joins of two tables from level 1, and keeps the cheapest path for each pair (plus paths with a useful sort order, which a later Merge Join or ORDER BY can use). Level 3 joins a level-2 result with one more table, and so on. The docs note that it first considers pairs linked by a join condition in the WHERE clause. Pairs without one (a cross join) are only tried when there is no other choice.


This search is exact but its cost still grows fast with the number of tables. Two limits keep planning time under control:


    

  • geqo_threshold (12): with 12 or more FROM items, the planner uses the genetic query optimizer. It tries random join orders, keeps the better ones and combines them over several rounds. Planning is faster, but the plan can be worse than the one the full search would find. The random generator restarts from geqo_seed for each query, so the same query and statistics give the same plan. geqo_effort (default 5) trades planning time for quality.
    • 

  • from_collapse_limit and join_collapse_limit (8): the planner merges subqueries and explicit JOIN clauses into one list to reorder, only if the list stays at 8 items or fewer. Above that, each part is planned on its own, and the planner does not move tables from one part to another. join_collapse_limit = 1 makes the planner keep your written JOIN order exactly.
    • 




How estimates go wrong


Stale statistics after a bulk load. You load 5 million rows into an empty table and query it at once. Autovacuum has not analyzed it yet. The page count correction fixes the total row count, but the MCV list and histogram still describe the old data (or there are none). A filter on a new date range falls outside the histogram and is estimated at a few rows. With a B-tree index on the column, the planner reads the true current minimum or maximum from the index, which helps for ranges at the ends. Without one, the estimate can be far too low. The fix is simple. Run ANALYZE right after every bulk load, as part of the load job.


Correlated columns. The planner multiplies selectivities. For country = 'DE' AND currency = 'EUR' it gets 30% × 50% = 15% of customers, but every German customer pays in euros, so the true answer is 30%. Underestimates like this make the planner choose a Nested Loop for what is really a large join, and a query that should take 100 ms takes minutes. The fix is extended statistics:


CREATE STATISTICS customers_country_currency (dependencies, mcv)
  ON country, currency FROM customers;
ANALYZE customers;


There are three kinds. dependencies measures how often one column fixes the other (the degree, 0 to 1). ndistinct stores distinct counts for column groups, which fixes the group count estimate of GROUP BY country, currency. mcv stores the most common value combinations with their frequencies. Dependencies only help equality and IN conditions, and they assume the values are compatible. So country = 'US' AND currency = 'EUR' gets a large estimate even if no such rows exist. An MCV list knows the combination is missing. Extended statistics exist only for the column groups you name, because ANALYZE cannot measure every combination.


Expressions. WHERE lower(email) = 'ana@acmeshop.com' or WHERE date_trunc('day', created_at) = '2026-05-01' have no statistics, because statistics are per column and this is a computed value. The planner falls back to a fixed guess. There are two fixes. One is an index on the expression, because ANALYZE then collects statistics for the indexed expression. The other, since PostgreSQL 14, is CREATE STATISTICS ... ON (lower(email)) FROM customers, which needs no index.


No statistics at all. When there is nothing to use, the planner uses constants from selfuncs.h:




ConditionDefault selectivity


col = value0.005


col < value, col > value0.3333


col > a AND col < b0.005


col LIKE pattern0.005


number of distinct values200




Temporary tables are a common victim. The docs state that autovacuum cannot access temporary tables, so they are never analyzed unless your code runs ANALYZE on them after filling them.



No hints, but levers


Core PostgreSQL has no query hints such as "use this index". (The pg_hint_plan extension adds them, outside core.) The project prefers to fix the estimates. The levers, in the order to try them:


    

  1. Better statistics. ANALYZE; a higher target for one column with ALTER TABLE orders ALTER COLUMN status SET STATISTICS 1000; extended statistics; a manual n_distinct with ALTER TABLE ... ALTER COLUMN ... SET (n_distinct = ...).
    2. 

  2. Correct cost settings. random_page_cost for your storage, effective_cache_size for your RAM. These describe the hardware and apply to all queries.
    3. 

  3. The enable_* switches, for diagnosis. SET enable_seqscan = off in a session makes the planner avoid Seq Scans where it has another choice. If the new plan is much faster, the estimates or costs are wrong somewhere. Since PostgreSQL 18, EXPLAIN marks a plan node that was used anyway as Disabled: true. These switches are a diagnostic tool, not a fix for production.
    4. 





 
Lab 2: two related conditions

 
The example data has 100,000 customers and a currency column added to customers for this lab. DE and FR pay in EUR, US in USD, GB in GBP. Pick a query and a kind of statistics. The lab computes the estimate with the planner's formulas (it uses the exact frequencies, where ANALYZE would use a 30,000-row sample).

 

 

 

 




The noise toggle shows a detail of how the dependency degree is measured. ANALYZE groups the sample by the first column and counts a group as "supporting" only if every row in it has the same value in the second column. A few German customers paying in dollars make the whole DE group count as zero. The degree drops from 1.0 to 0.70, and the estimate gets worse for every country, not only for Germany.








22. The executor: plan trees, memory, parallel query


Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Executor" (§51.6), "Using EXPLAIN" (§14.1), "Resource Consumption" (§19.4), "Query Planning" (§19.7), "Parallel Query" (§15.1 How Parallel Query Works, §15.2 When Can Parallel Query Be Used?, §15.3 Parallel Plans, §15.4 Parallel Safety), "When to JIT?" (§30.2); PostgreSQL 13 and 15 release notes (hash aggregation spill, hash_mem_multiplier); source src/backend/executor/README, src/backend/optimizer/path/costsize.c, src/backend/optimizer/path/allpaths.c (REL_18_STABLE). Checked 2026-09-30.


The executor runs the plan tree that the planner chose. Each plan node, when it is called, returns its next tuple or says "no more". To get a tuple, a node calls its child nodes. The executor calls the top node again and again, and sends each tuple it returns to the client.


The docs call this a "demand-pull pipeline". Nothing moves until the top asks for it. This design has two big results. Rows can reach the client before the query has read all of its input. And a node such as Limit can stop the whole pipeline early just by not asking for more. The cost is that some nodes cannot give their first tuple until they have read all of their input, and those nodes need memory.



A plan tree, built from the bottom


This query finds the five customers with the most paid orders:


SELECT c.name, count(*) AS paid
FROM customers c
JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE o.status = 'paid'
GROUP BY c.name
ORDER BY paid DESC
LIMIT 5;



 

  
ExecutorRun loopcalls the top node, sends each tuple to the client

  
↓ next?  ↑ tuple

  
Limitstops after 5 tuples

  
↓↑

  
SortSort Key: (count(*)) DESC. Reads all input first

  
↓↑

  
HashAggregateGroup Key: c.name. Reads all input first

  
↓↑

  
Hash JoinHash Cond: (o.customer_id = c.id)

  

   
↓↑ outer
Seq Scan on orders oFilter: (status = 'paid'::text)

   
↓↑ inner
Hashbuilds a hash table of all its input
↓↑
Seq Scan on customers c

  

 

 

  
Purple nodes must read all of their input before they return anything. At the bottom, a Seq Scan reads orders. Each call returns the next tuple that passes the filter status = 'paid'. Scan nodes are the only nodes that read tables. They stream, which means one call gives one tuple.

  
The other input is customers, read by a Seq Scan under a Hash node. Hash is special. It is not called tuple by tuple. On the first request, the Hash Join asks it to read all of its input into a hash table keyed on c.id. This uses memory up to work_mem × hash_mem_multiplier.

  
The Hash Join pulls one orders tuple at a time from the outer side, looks up customer_id in the hash table and returns the joined tuple. After the hash table is built, it streams.

  
HashAggregate must see every input tuple before it knows any final count. It pulls the whole join result, keeps one counter per c.name in a hash table, and only then starts to return groups. It is a blocking node.

  
Sort is also blocking. It must see every group before it knows which one is first. Because a Limit of 5 sits above it, the planner lets it keep only the best 5 (EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows Sort Method: top-N heapsort), which needs very little memory.

  
Limit calls Sort 5 times and then returns "no more". It never asks again, so no further work is done.

  
The top-level loop in ExecutorRun calls ExecProcNode on the top node until it returns nothing, and sends each tuple as a DataRow message. The first DataRow leaves only after both blocking nodes have finished all their input.

 

 





The four executor phases


The executor README gives the control flow for every query:


    

  1. ExecutorStart builds a state tree with one state node for each plan node. The plan tree itself stays read-only. All changing data (counters, hash tables, the current position of a scan) lives in the state tree. This is why one cached plan can be run many times, which prepared statements need (chapter 10).
    2. 

  2. ExecutorRun calls ExecProcNode on the top node in a loop. Each call goes down the tree as far as it must.
    3. 

  3. ExecutorFinish runs work that must happen after the last tuple, such as AFTER triggers.
    4. 

  4. ExecutorEnd closes tables, drops buffer pins and frees memory.
    5. 



Memory is managed in memory contexts. A per-query context holds the state tree and lives until ExecutorEnd. A per-tuple context holds temporary values made while one tuple is processed (for example the result of lower(email)), and it is reset after each tuple. So a query over a billion rows does not slowly leak memory, even if a function forgets to free what it allocated.



Streaming nodes and blocking nodes




Plan nodeWhen the first tuple comes outMemory


Seq Scan, Index Scan, Index Only ScanAt onceSmall


Nested LoopAt once (after one outer tuple and its first match)Small


Merge JoinAt once, if both inputs already come sorted (for example from indexes)Small


LimitAt once. Stops its child after n tuplesNone


Hash JoinAfter the inner side is fully read into the Hashwork_mem × hash_mem_multiplier


SortAfter all input is read and sortedwork_mem, then disk


HashAggregateAfter all input is readwork_mem × hash_mem_multiplier, then disk


GroupAggregateAt the end of the first group (its input is sorted)Small


MaterializeAt once. It saves each tuple as it passes, so a rescan (for example the inner side of a Nested Loop) replays the saved tuples instead of running the child againwork_mem, then disk




This table explains the two cost numbers in EXPLAIN (chapter 21). A streaming node has a low startup cost. A blocking node has a startup cost close to its total cost, because all the work happens before the first tuple. It also explains why LIMIT is sometimes almost free and sometimes saves nothing. SELECT * FROM orders LIMIT 10 reads about one page. SELECT * FROM orders ORDER BY total LIMIT 10 must read all 1,000,000 rows into the Sort before the first row comes out, unless an index on total gives the rows in order and removes the Sort.




 
Lab: pull tuples through a plan tree

 
The query is SELECT name FROM customers WHERE country = 'DE', with an optional ORDER BY name DESC and LIMIT. The table has 8 rows (example data). Press Next to make one call or one return. Turn on LIMIT and see the scan stop early. Turn on ORDER BY and see the Sort read every row before the first tuple leaves.

 

 

  

  

   

   
Sort buffer(none)Sent to client(none)

  

 

 





work_mem and spilling to disk


work_mem (default 4MB) is the memory limit for one Sort or hash node, not for the query. Hash nodes (Hash for a Hash Join, HashAggregate, Memoize) may use work_mem × hash_mem_multiplier. The multiplier's default is 2.0 since PostgreSQL 15, so 8MB. Chapter 9 shows what this means for the memory of the whole server.


When a node needs more than its limit, it does not fail. It spills to temporary files in the pgsql_tmp directory:


    

  • Sort sorts chunks that fit in memory, writes each chunk to disk, then merges the chunks. EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows Sort Method: external merge  Disk: 51200kB instead of quicksort  Memory: 74kB.
    • 

  • Hash Join splits both inputs into batches by hash value. It keeps one batch in memory and writes the others to disk, then joins them one batch at a time. The Hash node shows Batches: 8 instead of Batches: 1.
    • 

  • HashAggregate can spill since PostgreSQL 13. Before 13, a hash aggregate that the planner underestimated could grow far past work_mem. Now it writes groups that do not fit to disk and processes them later, and EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows its batches and disk usage.
    • 



Spilling is correct but slower, because the data is written and read again. The signs in production are temp_files and temp_bytes in pg_stat_database, and log lines from log_temp_files. temp_file_limit (default -1, no limit) can cancel a query that writes too much. The fix is usually a higher work_mem for the session that runs the big query, not for the whole server.



Parallel query


One backend runs on one CPU core. For a large scan, PostgreSQL can split the work across parallel workers. These are extra background processes that run part of the plan for the backend, which is then called the leader.


EXPLAIN SELECT count(*) FROM orders;

 Finalize Aggregate
   ->  Gather
         Workers Planned: 2
         ->  Partial Aggregate
               ->  Parallel Seq Scan on orders


Read it from the bottom. Parallel Seq Scan is a "parallel-aware" scan. The table's pages are divided into ranges, and each process (the workers and the leader) asks for the next range when it finishes one. So each tuple is read by exactly one process. Partial Aggregate counts only the tuples its own process saw. Gather collects tuples from all processes into the leader, in no fixed order. Finalize Aggregate in the leader adds the partial counts. Gather Merge is used instead of Gather when each process produces sorted output. It merges the streams and keeps the order.


The plan below Gather must be a partial plan. Every process runs it, and together they must produce each row exactly once. The docs list how joins fit. In a Nested Loop or Merge Join, the inner side runs in full in each process. In a plain Hash Join, each process builds its own copy of the hash table. In a Parallel Hash Join, the processes build one shared hash table together.


How many workers


The planner picks a number from the table size (compute_parallel_worker in allpaths.c). Below min_parallel_table_scan_size (8MB) there is no parallel scan. At 8MB it plans 1 worker, and one more each time the size triples. So there are 2 workers from 24MB, 3 from 72MB, 4 from 216MB. Then it caps the number:




SettingDefaultLimits


max_parallel_workers_per_gather2workers for one Gather node. 0 turns parallel query off


max_parallel_workers8parallel workers running in the whole server


max_worker_processes8all background worker processes (parallel workers, logical replication workers, extensions)




The acmeshop orders table (8,400 pages, about 66MB, example numbers) gets 2 planned workers. The planned number is not promised. At run time, if the server has no free worker slots, the query runs with fewer workers or with none. Then the leader does all the work alone, with a plan that was costed for three processes.


When parallel is cheaper


The planner costs the parallel plan like any other. The CPU cost of the scan is divided by a "parallel divisor". This is the number of workers plus the leader's share, which the source estimates as 1 - 0.3 × workers (so 2.4 for 2 workers, and 4 for 4 workers, where the leader adds nothing). Page reads are not divided, because the disk does not get faster. Gather adds parallel_setup_cost (1000) to start the workers and parallel_tuple_cost (0.1) for each tuple that moves from a worker to the leader. So parallel query pays off for large scans that return few rows, such as aggregates. It does not pay off for a query that returns most of a big table, because every tuple must pass through Gather.


What cannot run in parallel


    

  • Queries that write or lock rows (INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, SELECT ... FOR UPDATE). The exceptions are CREATE TABLE ... AS, SELECT INTO and CREATE/REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW, whose SELECT part can be parallel.
    • 

  • Queries that may be suspended, such as a cursor from DECLARE CURSOR or a PL/pgSQL FOR x IN query LOOP.
    • 

  • Queries that call a function marked PARALLEL UNSAFE. User-defined functions are PARALLEL UNSAFE unless you mark them otherwise, so one custom function in a WHERE clause can silently turn off parallel query. Mark a function PARALLEL SAFE only if it does not write, use sequences or change settings.
    • 

  • A query inside a query that is already parallel.
    • 



Each worker is a full process with its own work_mem for each node. A parallel query with 4 workers can use about 5 times the CPU and memory of the serial query (the docs give this example).



JIT compilation


PostgreSQL can compile parts of a query to machine code with LLVM at run time (JIT). It compiles expressions, such as WHERE conditions, and the code that unpacks tuples into columns. It is on by default (jit = on) if the server was built with LLVM. The planner uses JIT when the query's total estimated cost is above jit_above_cost (100000), and adds function inlining and extra optimization above 500000. It helps long CPU-bound analytic queries. It can hurt short queries whose cost estimate is high by mistake, because the compile time is added to a query that would have finished quickly without it. EXPLAIN ANALYZE prints a JIT section with the compile time, so you can see when this happens. If that happens often, set jit = off for those sessions or raise the thresholds.









23. Join algorithms: Nested Loop, Hash Join, Merge Join


Covers: Nested Loop (plain, with an inner index, Materialize, Memoize), Hash Join (build, probe, batches, skew, Parallel Hash), Merge Join (sorted inputs, mark and restore), join types per algorithm, how the planner chooses, the row underestimate failure, join order. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Planner/Optimizer" (§51.5), "Using EXPLAIN" (§14.1), "Controlling the Planner with Explicit JOIN Clauses" (§14.3), "Planner Method Configuration" and "Other Planner Options" (§19.7.1, §19.7.4), "Resource Consumption: Memory" (§19.4.1, work_mem, hash_mem_multiplier), "Parallel Plans" (§15.3); release notes 11 (parallel hash join), 14 (Memoize), 16 (right anti join), 18 (right semi join); source comments in nodeHashjoin.c, nodeHash.c, nodeMergejoin.c, joinpath.c (REL_18_STABLE). Checked 2026-09-30.


A join algorithm is the method the executor uses to find the matching rows of two inputs. PostgreSQL has three: Nested Loop, Hash Join and Merge Join. The SQL join type (chapter 4) says which rows you get. The algorithm says how the server finds them. The planner picks one algorithm for each join in the plan.


Why does this matter? Take orders JOIN customers ON customers.id = orders.customer_id on the example shop (1,000,000 orders, 100,000 customers; example sizes). If you compare every order with every customer, that is 1011 comparisons. A good algorithm does the same join with about 1.1 million row operations. The three algorithms are three ways to avoid the full comparison. Each one needs something different: an index, memory, or sorted input.



Three plan shapes


Every join node has two children. EXPLAIN prints the outer side first and the inner side second. The algorithm decides what the join node does with them.



 

  

   
Nested Loopfor each outer tuple, run the inner side

   
↙ ↘

   
Seq Scan customersouter, runs once
Index Scan ordersinner, runs once per outer tuple

  

  

   
Hash Joinprobe once per outer tuple

   
↙ ↘

   
Seq Scan ordersouter, probe side
Hashbuilds the table
↓
Seq Scan customersinner, build side

  

  

   
Merge Joinwalk two sorted inputs

   
↙ ↘

   
Sortby customer_id
↓
Seq Scan orders
Index Scan customers_pkeyalready in id order

  

 

 

  
Nested Loop. The join node reads one tuple from the outer child. Then it runs the inner child to completion with that tuple's values, emits the matches, and repeats. The inner child has loops equal to the number of outer tuples in EXPLAIN ANALYZE.

  
Hash Join. The inner child sits under a Hash node. It runs first, one time, and its tuples go into a hash table in memory. Then the join node reads the outer child and looks up each tuple in the hash table.

  
Merge Join. Both children must return tuples sorted on the join key. A Sort node can do that, or an index scan that reads the index in key order. The join then moves forward through both inputs at the same time.

 

 





Nested Loop


The simplest algorithm. In pseudo code:


for each tuple o in outer:
    for each tuple i in inner(o):     -- inner child is re-run (rescanned) for each o
        if join_condition(o, i): emit (o, i)


With a plain scan on the inner side, the work is outer rows × inner rows. That is terrible for two big inputs. But the planner almost never uses it that way. The docs describe the good case. The inner side is an index scan that takes the join key of the current outer tuple as its search key. EXPLAIN shows this as Index Cond: (customer_id = c.id). Now each outer tuple costs one index descent (chapter 16), not a full scan. The work becomes outer rows × one index lookup. When the outer side is small (a few rows up to some thousands), this is the fastest plan there is. It also has no start-up cost. The first joined row comes out at once, so it works well under a LIMIT.


EXPLAIN (COSTS OFF)
SELECT o.id, o.total FROM customers c JOIN orders o ON o.customer_id = c.id
WHERE c.email = 'ana@acmeshop.com';

 Nested Loop
   ->  Index Scan using customers_email_key on customers c
         Index Cond: (email = 'ana@acmeshop.com'::text)
   ->  Index Scan using orders_customer_id_idx on orders o
         Index Cond: (customer_id = c.id)


(This plan assumes an index orders_customer_id_idx on orders(customer_id). The foreign key alone does not create one. Chapter 26 comes back to this.)


Nested Loop is the only algorithm that works for any join condition. Hash Join needs equality. Merge Join needs equality with a sort order. A condition such as ON o.created_at BETWEEN p.valid_from AND p.valid_to or ON a.total < b.total can only run as a Nested Loop, with the condition shown as Join Filter.


Materialize and Memoize on the inner side


Re-running the inner child is wasteful when it returns the same rows each time. Two plan nodes fix that.


    

  • Materialize stores the inner child's output the first time (in memory, or in a temporary file if it is bigger than work_mem). Later loops read the stored copy. The docs show it on top of an inner scan that does not depend on the outer tuple.
    • 

  • Memoize (since PostgreSQL 14) is a cache in front of a parameterized inner scan. The key is the value from the outer tuple. When the same key comes again, Memoize returns the cached inner rows and skips the scan. It is useful when many outer tuples share few key values, for example 1,000,000 orders that point to 10,000 products. EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows Cache Key, Cache Mode and a line Hits: … Misses: … Evictions: … Overflows: … Memory Usage: …kB. The cache uses hash memory (work_mem × hash_mem_multiplier) and evicts the least recently used entries. enable_memoize turns it off.
    • 




Hash Join


Hash Join trades memory for speed. It runs in two phases.



 

  
Hash JoinHash Cond: (o.customer_id = c.id)

  
↓ first, one time
Hash
←
Seq Scan customersinner: 100,000 rows

  
Hash table in memorybucket = hash(c.id) mod number of buckets; limit work_mem × hash_mem_multiplier

  
Seq Scan ordersouter: 1,000,000 rows
→ hash(o.customer_id), look in one bucket, compare keys

  
Batch files on diskonly if the hash table does not fit: Batches: 2, 4, 8 …

 

 

  
The join node knows the equality condition. It will hash the key on both sides with the same hash function.

  
Build phase. Before it returns any row, the join asks its inner child (under the Hash node) for all rows. So a Hash Join has a start-up cost. Nothing comes out until the build is done.

  
Each inner tuple goes into a bucket chosen by its hash value. The number of buckets is a power of two. Two different keys can land in the same bucket, so the probe must still compare the real key values.

  
Probe phase. For each outer tuple, hash the key, go to one bucket, compare with the few tuples in it, emit the matches. Each input is read once. The work is about inner rows + outer rows.

  
If the build side is bigger than the memory limit, the join splits both inputs into batches by hash value and writes all batches except the first to temporary files. Then it joins one batch at a time. The next section explains the cost.

 

 




The planner puts the smaller input on the inner (build) side, because the hash table must hold it. Here that is customers, not orders. Hash Join works only for equality conditions whose operator can hash (a hash operator family). It cannot do < or BETWEEN.


Memory and batches


The memory limit for one hash table is work_mem × hash_mem_multiplier. The defaults are 4MB and 2.0, so 8MB. EXPLAIN ANALYZE prints the result on the Hash node:


->  Hash  (actual time=41.2..41.2 rows=100000.00 loops=1)
      Buckets: 131072  Batches: 1  Memory Usage: 6386kB          -- fits in memory (example output)

->  Hash  (actual time=48.9..48.9 rows=100000.00 loops=1)
      Buckets: 32768  Batches: 4  Memory Usage: 1649kB           -- work_mem = 1MB: split into 4 batches


How batches work. The batch number comes from bits of the hash value, and the number of batches is always a power of two. While the join builds the table, tuples of batch 0 stay in memory and tuples of other batches go to temporary files. During the probe, an outer tuple of batch 0 is probed at once. An outer tuple of another batch is also written to that batch's file. When the outer input ends, the join loads batch 1 from disk into the hash table, probes it with the outer tuples of batch 1, and so on. So with N batches about (N−1)/N of both inputs is written once and read once more. That is extra disk I/O, not a disaster. If the planner underestimated the inner side, the executor doubles the number of batches during the build, and the Hash node then prints both numbers, as in Buckets: 65536 (originally 65536)  Batches: 8 (originally 2). The fix is more memory for this query (SET work_mem, or a higher hash_mem_multiplier) or a smaller build side (fewer columns, a filter applied earlier).


Skew. If a few key values are very common on the outer side, PostgreSQL keeps the inner tuples for those most common values (from the outer column's statistics) in a small separate "skew" table, about 2% of the hash memory, so those outer tuples always join in the first pass and are not written to batch files.


Parallel Hash Join (since PostgreSQL 11). In a parallel plan, a plain Hash Join makes every process build its own copy of the same hash table. A Parallel Hash Join over a Parallel Hash node lets the processes build one shared hash table together, with a memory budget of hash memory × the number of processes. Chapter 22 covers parallel query.



Merge Join


Merge Join needs both inputs sorted on the join key. Then it walks them in step, like merging two sorted lists:


o = first outer, i = first inner
while both have tuples:
    if o.key < i.key: advance o
    elif o.key > i.key: advance i
    else:
        mark i                               -- remember where this key starts in inner
        emit o with every inner tuple of the same key, advancing i
        advance o
        if o.key equals the marked key: restore i to the mark   -- duplicates on the outer side


Mark and restore. If the outer side has duplicate keys (three orders of customer 1), the inner pointer must go back to the first inner tuple with that key for each of them. The inner child must support "mark this position" and "go back to it". A Sort node and an index scan can do that. If the inner child cannot, the planner puts a Materialize node on top of it. Because of this, EXPLAIN ANALYZE can report more rows on the inner child than the inner table has. The docs note that the repeated rows are counted each time.


The join condition must be an equality whose operator belongs to a B-tree operator family. The algorithm needs "less than" and "greater than" to know which pointer to move, and B-tree operator families define that order. Several keys are fine (a.x = b.x AND a.y = b.y).


When is it the best choice? When both inputs are large and already come in key order, for example from indexes, or when the result must be sorted on the join key anyway. Then each input is read once, no memory is needed for a hash table, and there is no start-up delay. If one input must be sorted first, the sort (chapter 24) often costs more than a hash table. The docs also note that a Merge Join can stop early. When one input ends, the rest of the other input cannot match.



Lab: watch the three algorithms work



 
Lab: step through Nested Loop, Hash Join and Merge Join

 
Sample data: customers Ana (1), Ben (2), Chen (3), Dara (4, no orders) and six orders. The query is customers JOIN orders ON orders.customer_id = customers.id. Pick an algorithm and press Next. Watch the pointers, the hash buckets, and the counters. Try LEFT JOIN to see how each algorithm keeps Dara.

 

 

 

 

 

 

 

 

 




Count the work at the end of each run. The plain Nested Loop does 24 comparisons for 4 × 6 tuples. That number grows as the product of the two sizes. The index version does 4 lookups. Hash Join does 4 inserts and 6 probes. Merge Join does about one comparison per pointer move, plus the sorts it needed first.



Join types on each algorithm


The algorithm must also produce the right rows for the SQL join type (chapter 4). Each one supports some types directly. For the others the planner swaps the two inputs, which is why you see names such as Hash Right Join for a query that says LEFT JOIN.




Join typeNested LoopHash JoinMerge Join


INNERyesyesyes


LEFT (keep outer rows)yes. After the inner loop, it emits the outer tuple with NULLs if nothing matchedyes (Hash Left Join)yes (Merge Left Join)


RIGHT (keep inner rows)no. The planner swaps sides and runs it as a Left Joinyes (Hash Right Join). Each hash entry has a "matched" flag, and a final pass over the hash table emits the unmatched ones. This lets the planner hash the smaller, preserved sideyes (Merge Right Join)


SEMI (EXISTS, IN)yes (Nested Loop Semi Join). It stops at the first matchyes (Hash Semi Join); Hash Right Semi Join since PostgreSQL 18yes


ANTI (NOT EXISTS)yes (Nested Loop Anti Join). It emits only if there is no matchyes (Hash Anti Join); Hash Right Anti Join since PostgreSQL 16yes


FULLnoyes, if the condition is hashable (Hash Full Join)yes (Merge Full Join)




So a FULL JOIN must use Hash Join or Merge Join, and its condition must be an equality that can hash or merge. With any other condition PostgreSQL gives the error "FULL JOIN is only supported with merge-joinable or hash-joinable join conditions".



How the planner chooses


The planner (chapter 21) builds paths for each join with every algorithm that can run it, in both input orders where that is allowed, estimates the cost of each, and keeps the cheapest. The costs depend on three inputs: the estimated number of rows on each side, the indexes and sort orders that exist, and the memory settings. As a rule of thumb:




Nested Loop + inner indexHash JoinMerge Join


Workouter × index lookupinner + outerinner + outer (+ sorts)


Wins whenouter side is smallboth sides are big, no useful order, build side fits in memoryboth sides are big and already sorted, or sorted output is needed


Needsan index on the inner join column (or a tiny inner side)equality, hash memoryequality with B-tree order, sorted input


Start-upnone, first row at oncemust build the whole hash tablenone if inputs are pre-sorted; whole sort if not


Any conditionyesnono




The classic failure: a row underestimate. Suppose the planner estimates that the outer side returns 1 row, but it really returns 100,000. With 1 row, a Nested Loop with an inner index scan looks unbeatable, with one lookup. At run time the inner side runs 100,000 times. In EXPLAIN ANALYZE you see rows=1 in the estimate, rows=100000.00 in the actual, and loops=100000 on the inner node. A Hash Join would have read each input once. Worse, if there is no index on the inner side, the "cheap" inner Seq Scan runs 100,000 times. The cause is almost always a bad estimate lower in the tree: stale statistics, correlated columns, or a condition the planner cannot estimate. Chapter 25 shows how to find it and chapter 26 shows how to fix it.


Join order


With three or more tables the planner also picks the order: which two tables are joined first, and which side is outer. The order in your SQL text does not decide it. The docs say that explicit inner JOIN syntax "is semantically the same as listing the input relations in FROM, so it does not constrain the join order". The planner searches the orders almost exhaustively as long as the query has fewer than geqo_threshold (default 12) FROM items. It flattens explicit JOINs into one list only while the list stays within join_collapse_limit (default 8, the same as from_collapse_limit). Above that it keeps your written order for the rest. Outer joins limit the freedom. A FULL JOIN fixes the order completely, and most LEFT JOINs can be reordered only partly. Setting join_collapse_limit = 1 forces the written order, which is a tool for experts who know a better order.


The enable_nestloop, enable_hashjoin, enable_mergejoin, enable_memoize and enable_material settings do not forbid an algorithm. They add a big penalty so the planner avoids it when it has another choice (PostgreSQL 18 prints Disabled: true on a node it had to use anyway). Use them in a test session to see the second-best plan and its real time. Do not use them in production to hide a bad estimate.



Lab: where each algorithm wins



 
Lab: cost comparison in a simplified model

 
This is a simplified model, not the planner's cost formulas. One unit is one row read, one comparison or one hash operation. An index lookup costs log2(inner rows) comparisons plus 4 units for the random page visits (4 is the ratio of the default random_page_cost 4.0 to seq_page_cost 1.0). A sort costs n × log2(n). When memory is short, Hash Join uses 4 batches (an example) and a sort needs one extra write and read pass. Move the outer slider down to find the point where Nested Loop wins.

 

 

 

 




Three things to notice. First, the plain Nested Loop grows with outer × inner, so it only wins when one side has a handful of rows. Second, Nested Loop with an index grows with the outer side only, so it wins while the outer side is small and loses badly as it grows. That is why a wrong estimate of the outer side is so costly. Third, Hash Join and Merge Join are close when the inputs are sorted and memory is enough. When memory is short, a pre-sorted Merge Join needs no temporary files, and when the inputs are unsorted, the sorts push Merge Join above Hash Join.









24. Sort and aggregate algorithms


Covers: Sort (quicksort, top-N heapsort, external merge), Incremental Sort, sorts avoided by an index, aggregation nodes (Aggregate, HashAggregate, GroupAggregate, MixedAggregate), HashAggregate spill to disk, partial and finalize aggregation, DISTINCT, UNION, count(*), LIMIT and OFFSET. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Using EXPLAIN" (§14.1), "Resource Consumption: Memory" (§19.4.1, work_mem, hash_mem_multiplier), "Planner Method Configuration" (§19.7.1, enable_hashagg, enable_incremental_sort, enable_sort), "Parallel Aggregation" (§15.3.3), "LIMIT and OFFSET" (§7.6), "Statistics Used by the Planner" (§14.2), "Index-Only Scans and Covering Indexes" (§11.9), release notes 13 (incremental sort, hash aggregation to disk); source tuplesort.c, explain.c (REL_18_STABLE). Checked 2026-09-30.


Sorting puts rows in the order of some keys. Aggregation turns many rows into one row per group. PostgreSQL has more than one algorithm for each, and it picks one by the number of rows, the memory it may use (work_mem), and whether the input already comes in the right order.


Sorts appear for ORDER BY, for Merge Join inputs (chapter 23), for DISTINCT, for UNION and for grouping. Aggregates appear for GROUP BY, DISTINCT and plain count(*). The logical meaning of GROUP BY is in chapter 5. This chapter is about the work the executor does.



Sort: three methods
