The Sort node reads all its input, then returns it in order. It cannot return the first row before it has seen the last input row, because the last row could be the smallest. So a Sort has a high start-up cost. EXPLAIN ANALYZE tells you which method ran, on the Sort Method line.




Sort MethodWhenMemoryEXPLAIN ANALYZE line


quicksortall input tuples fit in work_memall tuplesSort Method: quicksort  Memory: 74kB


top-N heapsortthere is a LIMIT N (bound), and the input has more than 2 × N tuplesonly N tuplesSort Method: top-N heapsort  Memory: 27kB


external mergethe input does not fit in work_memwork_mem, the rest in temporary filesSort Method: external merge  Disk: 41872kB




(The numbers in the lines are example values. work_mem default is 4MB. It is a limit per sort or hash operation, not per query and not per connection. One query can run several sorts and hashes at the same time.)


Top-N heapsort


For ORDER BY total DESC LIMIT 10 the server needs only the 10 largest totals. It keeps a heap of at most 10 tuples, ordered so that the worst kept tuple is at the top. Each new tuple is compared with that top tuple. If the new one is worse, it is thrown away after one comparison. If it is better, it replaces the top and sinks to its place in about log2(10) steps. Memory stays at 10 tuples however large the input is. In tuplesort.c the switch happens when the sort has collected more than 2 × N tuples. OFFSET counts too: OFFSET 100000 LIMIT 20 makes the bound 100,020 tuples.


External merge sort



 

  
Input: 1,000,000 order tuplesabout 60 MB in this example, work_mem = 4MB

  
↓ fill memory

  
Sort one memory load in work_memquicksort of what fits

  
run 1
run 2
run 3
…
run k

  
↓ read the head of every run, always output the smallest

  
Mergeoutput in order

  
Cost: every tuple is written to disk and read back at least once

 

 

  
The input is larger than work_mem, so it cannot be sorted in one piece.

  
The sort fills work_mem with tuples and sorts them.

  
Each sorted piece is a run. It is written to a temporary file. Then memory is filled again. Temporary files go to temp_tablespaces or the default tablespace, and they count in temp_blks_written.

  
At the end the runs are merged. The merge keeps one small buffer per run and always takes the smallest head. If there are too many runs for one merge, it merges in several passes.

  
So an external sort costs extra writes and reads of the whole input. EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows Sort Method: external merge  Disk: …kB and Buffers: temp read=… written=….

 

 




Two ways remove an external sort. Give the sort more memory, only where it is needed: SET LOCAL work_mem = '64MB' inside one transaction, or ALTER ROLE reporting SET work_mem = '64MB' for a reporting role. Do not raise it for the whole server without arithmetic. For example, 200 connections × 3 sorts × 64MB is about 38GB. Or remove the sort: a B-tree index gives rows in key order (chapter 16), so ORDER BY created_at on an index scan of (created_at) needs no Sort node at all. With a LIMIT this is the fastest case, because the scan stops after N rows.


Incremental Sort (PostgreSQL 13)


Sometimes the input is already sorted on the first keys but not on all of them. For example, there is an index on orders(customer_id) and the query says ORDER BY customer_id, created_at. An Incremental Sort reads the input one group of equal customer_id at a time and sorts only inside that group. It can return the first rows early, which helps a LIMIT, and it needs only enough memory for one group. EXPLAIN shows Presorted Key: customer_id. enable_incremental_sort turns it off.


EXPLAIN (COSTS OFF)
SELECT * FROM orders ORDER BY customer_id, created_at LIMIT 50;

 Limit
   ->  Incremental Sort
         Sort Key: customer_id, created_at
         Presorted Key: customer_id
         ->  Index Scan using orders_customer_id_idx on orders




 
Lab: which sort method will run?

 
Set the input and the memory. The rule is the one in tuplesort.c. The memory per tuple is a model. It counts the row width plus 40 bytes of bookkeeping (the sort slot and a tuple header on a 64-bit build). That is an approximation, so the switch point in a real server is near, not exactly at, the value shown.

 

 

 

 

 
Top-N heapsort, step by step

 
Twelve order totals arrive in scan order. The query is ORDER BY total DESC LIMIT N. The heap keeps the N best totals, with the smallest kept total at the top (position 0).

 

 
input

 
heap

 

 





Aggregation: four nodes


An aggregate keeps a small transition state per group, for example a counter for count(*) or a running sum and count for avg(). Each input row updates the state of its group. At the end the state is turned into the result. The nodes differ in how they find the state for a row's group.




NodeHow it finds the groupNeedsMemoryOutput order


Aggregatethere is only one group (no GROUP BY)nothingone stateone row


HashAggregatehash table: key → statehashable key typesone entry per groupno order


GroupAggregateinput is sorted on the group keys; a new key means the previous group is completesorted input (Sort node, or an index that gives the order)one group at a timesorted by the group keys


MixedAggregatefor GROUPING SETS, ROLLUP, CUBE: some sets by hashing and some from sorted input in one passas above, per sethash tables + one sorted groupmixed




HashAggregate reads the input in any order. For each row it hashes the group key, finds or creates the entry, and updates the state. When the input ends, it emits all entries. Memory grows with the number of groups, not rows. 1,000,000 orders grouped by status need 4 entries, but grouped by customer_id they need 100,000. The limit is work_mem × hash_mem_multiplier (8MB with the defaults).


Spill to disk (since PostgreSQL 13). Before version 13 the planner avoided HashAggregate when it expected more than work_mem, and if it misestimated, the hash table grew without limit. Now, when the table is full, rows of groups that are already in the table still update their state, and rows of new groups are written to spill files, split into partitions by hash. After the input ends, the in-memory groups are emitted, and each spill partition is then processed as its own batch. EXPLAIN ANALYZE shows Batches: 1  Memory Usage: 1553kB when it fit and adds Disk Usage: …kB when it spilled (Batches > 1).


GroupAggregate needs sorted input, then it streams. It holds one group, and when the key changes, it emits that group and starts the next. It needs almost no memory and it returns the first group early. If an index already gives the order, there is no sort cost at all.


Two example plans:


EXPLAIN (COSTS OFF) SELECT status, count(*) FROM orders GROUP BY status;
 HashAggregate                         -- 4 groups: a tiny hash table
   Group Key: status
   ->  Seq Scan on orders

EXPLAIN (COSTS OFF) SELECT customer_id, count(*) FROM orders GROUP BY customer_id;
 GroupAggregate                        -- one possible plan: the index gives customer_id order
   Group Key: customer_id
   ->  Index Only Scan using orders_customer_id_idx on orders


How the planner chooses. It estimates the number of groups from the column statistics (n_distinct, chapter 21). Few groups that fit in hash memory favour HashAggregate. Many groups, input that is already sorted, or an ORDER BY on the group keys favour GroupAggregate, because the sort (or the index) does two jobs. A bad group estimate gives the same kind of failure as in joins. A HashAggregate chosen for "1,000 groups" that turn out to be 5,000,000 spills a lot. enable_hashagg = off shows the sorted alternative in a test session.


Parallel aggregation. In a parallel plan each process runs a Partial Aggregate (or Partial HashAggregate) on its part of the rows. A Gather node collects the partial states, and a Finalize Aggregate in the leader combines them. This needs aggregates with a combine function, and it is not used for aggregates with DISTINCT or ORDER BY inside. Chapter 22 covers Gather.




 
Lab: HashAggregate and GroupAggregate, row by row

 
Query: SELECT customer_id, count(*), sum(total) FROM orders GROUP BY customer_id on 10 sample orders of 6 customers. Step through each node. Watch the "groups held" counter. It is the memory the node needs. Turn on the memory limit to see a HashAggregate spill.

 

 

  
HashAggregate (input in scan order)

  
Sort + GroupAggregate (input sorted by customer_id)

 

 





DISTINCT and UNION


SELECT DISTINCT country FROM customers is grouping without aggregates. The planner uses the same two methods: a HashAggregate on the columns, or a Sort followed by a Unique node, which drops a row when it equals the previous one. UNION (without ALL) must remove duplicates across both inputs, so it adds the same work. It is an Append of both inputs and then a HashAggregate or a Sort + Unique. UNION ALL is only the Append. Write UNION ALL when duplicates cannot happen or do not matter. Wrapping a whole query in DISTINCT to "fix" duplicates from a join adds a sort or hash over the full result and hides a join mistake.



count(*) is not free


PostgreSQL does not store a row count per table. With MVCC (chapter 12) there is no single right number. Two transactions that run at the same time can see different counts. So SELECT count(*) FROM orders must visit every tuple and check if its snapshot can see it. The fastest plan reads a small index with an Index Only Scan, which skips the heap for pages that the visibility map marks all-visible (chapter 18). On a table with many recent changes, it still visits many heap pages. Large parallel counts use Partial Aggregate and Finalize Aggregate.


If an estimate is enough, use the planner's number: SELECT reltuples::bigint FROM pg_class WHERE oid = 'orders'::regclass. VACUUM, ANALYZE and CREATE INDEX update it, so it is usually a bit out of date, and it is -1 before the table has been vacuumed or analyzed. For a filtered count, a common trick is to run EXPLAIN and read the estimated rows. For an exact count that must be fast, keep a counter table updated in the same transaction, which costs a hot row that every writer updates.



LIMIT and OFFSET


A Limit node stops asking its child for rows after it has enough. Plans with no start-up cost (an index scan in the right order, a Nested Loop) stop early and cheaply. Plans with a Sort or a Hash under the Limit must finish that step first, although the Sort uses top-N heapsort. OFFSET 100000 does not skip work. The docs say "the rows skipped by an OFFSET clause still have to be computed inside the server". The child produces 100,000 rows that the Limit then throws away. Without ORDER BY, LIMIT returns any rows, and the order can change between runs and plans. Chapter 26 replaces deep OFFSET pages with keyset pagination.









25. How to read EXPLAIN


Covers: EXPLAIN vs EXPLAIN ANALYZE, the options (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, VERBOSE, SETTINGS, WAL, FORMAT and others), the fields of one node, per-loop numbers, the tree and outer/inner order, detail lines (Filter, Index Cond, Recheck Cond, Heap Fetches, Sort Method, Buckets/Batches, Buffers, Index Searches, Workers, never executed), the method to find the problem node, a catalogue of bad signs, caveats. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Using EXPLAIN" (§14.1, including EXPLAIN Basics, EXPLAIN ANALYZE, Caveats), the "EXPLAIN" command reference, "Parallel Plans" (§15.3), release notes 18 (BUFFERS on by default with ANALYZE, fractional row counts, index search counts); explain.c (REL_18_STABLE) for the exact output format. Checked 2026-09-30.


EXPLAIN shows the plan the planner picked for a statement. The plan is a tree of plan nodes with the planner's estimates. EXPLAIN ANALYZE also runs the statement and adds the real numbers for every node: time, rows and loops. Reading it well means finding the one node where the time goes, and the estimate that made the planner choose it.



EXPLAIN and EXPLAIN ANALYZE


Plain EXPLAIN only plans. It is safe on any statement and fast. But it shows only guesses. EXPLAIN ANALYZE executes the statement for real and throws away the result rows. For a SELECT that is harmless. For INSERT, UPDATE, DELETE or MERGE the change really happens, triggers fire, and locks are taken. The docs give the safe form:


BEGIN;
EXPLAIN ANALYZE UPDATE orders SET status = 'shipped' WHERE id = 42;
ROLLBACK;


The locks stay until the ROLLBACK, so do not leave that transaction open on a production primary.




OptionWhat it adds


ANALYZERuns the statement. Adds actual time, rows, loops per node, and Planning Time and Execution Time.


BUFFERSPages per node: shared hit (found in shared buffers), read (read from the OS or disk), dirtied, written; temp read/written for sorts, hashes and Materialize that went to disk; I/O timings if track_io_timing is on. Since PostgreSQL 18 it is on automatically with ANALYZE. Turn it off with BUFFERS OFF.


VERBOSEThe output column list of every node, schema-qualified names, and the query identifier if one is computed.


SETTINGSPlanner-related settings that differ from the built-in default, for example a changed work_mem or random_page_cost. Useful when you compare plans from two servers.


WALWith ANALYZE: WAL records, full page images and bytes generated (chapter 15). Useful for writes.


FORMAT JSONThe same data as JSON (also XML, YAML) for tools and plan visualizers.


othersTIMING OFF keeps row counts but skips per-node clock reads, which lowers overhead. SERIALIZE measures converting the result to text or binary (where TOAST fetches show up). SUMMARY, MEMORY (planner memory), COSTS OFF, GENERIC_PLAN (a plan for $1 parameters, not with ANALYZE).




A good default for investigation is EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS, SETTINGS). On PostgreSQL 18, EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, SETTINGS) gives the same, because BUFFERS is already on.



One node, field by field


Here is one node line from an example plan on the shop database (all plans in this chapter are example output in the PostgreSQL 18 format):


->  Index Scan using order_items_pkey on order_items oi  (cost=0.43..13.49 rows=3 width=12) (actual time=0.018..0.041 rows=3.00 loops=100000)




FieldMeaning


cost=0.43..13.49Estimated start-up cost (work before the first row can come out) and total cost (all rows). The unit is arbitrary. By tradition it is one sequential page read (seq_page_cost = 1.0). It is not milliseconds. The cost of a node includes its children. The cost is for one execution of the node.


rows=3Estimated rows this node emits per execution, not the rows it reads. A filter can read a million and emit ten.


width=12Estimated average size of one output row in bytes.


actual time=0.018..0.041Real milliseconds to the first row and to the last row, per loop, averaged. Includes the children.


rows=3.00Real rows emitted per loop, averaged. Since PostgreSQL 18 it has two decimals, so an average of 0.33 no longer prints as 0.


loops=100000How many times the node ran. The inner side of a Nested Loop runs once per outer row. Parallel workers count as loops too.




Multiply by loops. This node emitted 3.00 × 100,000 = 300,000 rows in total, and it spent 0.041 × 100,000 = 4,100 ms. The small per-loop numbers hide the fact that this node took most of the query's time. The docs make the same point: "Multiply by the loops value to get the total time actually spent in the node."



Reading the tree


Each -> starts a child node. Indentation shows the tree. Rows flow from the most indented nodes up to the top node, which returns the result. So read from the inside out. Start with the scans at the leaves, then the joins, sorts and aggregates above them. The lines under a node without an arrow (Index Cond, Buffers, …) are its details. For a join, the first child is the outer side and the second child is the inner side (for a Hash Join, the inner side is under the Hash node). Here is the tree of the first lab plan, built from the leaves:



 

  
Limitstops after 20 rows; here only 10 exist

  
↑

  
SortSort Key: o.created_at DESC · quicksort 25kB

  
↑

  
Nested Looprows=10.00 loops=1

  

   
↑ outer (first child)
Index Scan customers_email_keyrows=1.00 loops=1

   
↑ inner (second child)
Index Scan orders_customer_id_idxIndex Cond: (customer_id = c.id) · loops=1

  

 

 

  
Start at the most indented node that comes first. The outer side finds Ana's row through the unique index on email. Estimated 1 row, actual 1.00.

  
The inner side runs once for each outer row. It uses c.id from the outer row as its search key. With one outer row, loops=1.

  
The Nested Loop joins them and emits 10 rows. Its actual time includes both children.

  
The Sort must read all 10 rows before it returns the first one. That is why its start-up time is close to its total time.

  
The Limit at the top asks for 20 rows, gets 10 and ends the query. Execution Time at the bottom of the output is the time for the whole statement.

 

 





Detail lines




LineWhat it tells you


Index Cond vs FilterAn Index Cond was used to search the index, so only matching entries are read. A Filter is checked on each row after it was read. A condition in Filter on an index scan did not help the search. Check if a better index (chapter 19) could move it into Index Cond.


Rows Removed by Filter: 999990Rows read and thrown away. A large number next to a small rows means the node did a lot of work for little output.


Join FilterA join condition checked on each candidate pair, not used to find the pair. For an outer join, a row that fails it still comes out NULL-extended; a plain Filter removes it.


Recheck Cond, Heap Blocks: exact=… lossy=…On a Bitmap Heap Scan. If the bitmap did not fit in work_mem, it keeps whole pages ("lossy") and must recheck the condition on every row of those pages (chapter 18).


Heap Fetches: NOn an Index Only Scan, the number of rows for which the heap had to be visited because the page was not all-visible. High numbers mean the visibility map is behind. VACUUM more often (chapter 13).


Index Searches: NNew in PostgreSQL 18: how many times the index was searched from the root, across all loops. For IN lists and skip scans it can be more than 1 per loop.


Sort Method: … Memory/Diskquicksort, top-N heapsort or external merge (chapter 24). Disk means the sort spilled.


Buckets: … Batches: … Memory Usage: …On a Hash node. Batches above 1 means the hash table did not fit and both inputs went partly to temporary files (chapter 23). On a HashAggregate, Batches and Disk Usage mean the same.


Buffers: shared hit=… read=… dirtied=… written=…Pages for this node including its children. hit came from shared buffers, read came from the OS (maybe its page cache, maybe disk). dirtied pages were changed, often hint bits set on a first read after a bulk load. written pages were evicted and written by this backend. temp read/written is spill to disk.


Workers Planned: 2, Workers Launched: 1On Gather or Gather Merge. Fewer launched than planned means the server ran out of parallel workers (max_parallel_workers), so the query ran slower than planned.


(never executed)The node never ran, for example the inner side of a join whose outer side returned no rows. Its time is zero; it is not the problem.


Planning Time, Execution TimePlanning Time covers planning only (not parse and rewrite). Execution Time covers executor start, run and shutdown, and triggers, but not sending rows to the client.




Parallel plans need one extra rule. Under a Gather, loops counts the leader and each worker, and rows is the average per process:


Gather  (cost=1000.00..14580.33 rows=10 width=26) (actual time=0.412..28.914 rows=10.00 loops=1)
  Workers Planned: 2
  Workers Launched: 2
  ->  Parallel Seq Scan on orders  (cost=0.00..13579.33 rows=4 width=26) (actual time=17.051..26.890 rows=3.33 loops=3)
        Filter: (customer_id = 42)
        Rows Removed by Filter: 333330


The Parallel Seq Scan emitted 3.33 × 3 = 10 rows. Its 26.9 ms ran in three processes at the same time, so do not multiply time by loops here and do not subtract it from the Gather's time.



The method: find the node, then find the estimate


    

  1. Find where the time goes. For each node compute its exclusive time: its actual total time × loops, minus the same for each direct child. The node with the largest exclusive time is where the work happens. (Skip this arithmetic across Gather, as above, and note that an InitPlan or CTE can make the children's sum look odd.)
    2. 

  2. Compare estimated rows with actual rows at every node, per loop. A factor of 2 or 3 is normal. A factor of 10 is a warning. A factor of 100 or more almost always changed the plan.
    3. 

  3. Look low in the tree first. A bad estimate at a leaf spreads upward. Every join and sort above it was planned for the wrong size. The planner picks a Nested Loop because the outer side "has 1 row", or a Hash Join whose hash table then spills. Fixing the lowest bad estimate often fixes the whole plan.
    4. 

  4. Then ask why the estimate is wrong (chapter 21): stale statistics after a bulk load, correlated columns multiplied as if independent, a function or expression with no statistics, or a parameter value that differs from the typical value.
    5. 





 
Lab: an interactive plan reader

 
Pick a plan. Click any node name, any number in a node line, or any detail line. The panel explains it with that node's numbers: total rows (rows × loops), total and exclusive time, and estimate vs actual. Then turn on "find the problem".

 

 

 

 

 





Bad signs and what they usually mean




You seeIt usually meansLook at


Seq Scan with a large Rows Removed by Filter and few rows outNo index matches the condition, or the condition cannot use one (function on the column, type mismatch, leading wildcard)chapter 19, chapter 26


Nested Loop whose inner side has a high loopsThe outer side was underestimated. A Hash Join would read each side oncethe estimate on the outer side


Sort Method: external merge  Disk: …The sort does not fit in work_memmore work_mem for this query, or an index that gives the order


Hash with Batches > 1, HashAggregate with Disk UsageHash memory too small, often because rows were underestimatedwork_mem, hash_mem_multiplier, the estimate


Index Only Scan with a high Heap FetchesThe visibility map is not up to date; the scan is visiting the heap anywayVACUUM and autovacuum settings


Estimated rows off by 100× or moreStale or missing statistics, correlated columns, an expression without statisticsANALYZE, CREATE STATISTICS (chapter 21)


Many shared read compared with hitThe data was not in shared buffers. The cache was cold, or the query touches far more pages than the result needsrun it twice to compare; reduce the pages touched


Bitmap Heap Scan with lossy pagesThe bitmap did not fit in work_memwork_mem, a more selective index


Workers Launched lower than Workers PlannedThe server's parallel worker pool was busymax_parallel_workers, the load at that time





Caveats


The docs list things that make EXPLAIN ANALYZE differ from a normal run. Timing each node costs clock reads, and on some systems that overhead is large (pg_test_timing measures it; TIMING OFF avoids it). Network transfer is not included, and conversion of output values is not included unless you add SERIALIZE. A plan tested on a small table does not predict the plan on a large one. The docs warn that "results on a toy-sized table cannot be assumed to apply to large tables". Actual rows smaller than the estimate is normal under a Limit, and under a Merge Join that stopped early. A Merge Join can also count re-read inner rows twice. BitmapAnd and BitmapOr always report 0 actual rows. Finally, the first run of a query may read from disk and the second from cache, so compare Buffers as well as time.









26. Query optimization in practice


Covers: the method (find, measure, locate, explain, change one thing, measure again), pg_stat_statements, log_min_duration_statement, auto_explain; causes and fixes: missing index, wrong column order, predicates that cannot use an index, SELECT *, N+1 queries, OFFSET vs keyset pagination, correlated subqueries, OR across tables, NOT IN vs NOT EXISTS, stale statistics and correlated columns, spills, count(*), large IN lists, batch updates, COPY, lock waits, bloat, too many indexes; denormalization, materialized views, caches; what not to do. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Performance Tips" chapter ("Using EXPLAIN" §14.1, "Statistics Used by the Planner" §14.2, "Controlling the Planner with Explicit JOIN Clauses" §14.3, "Populating a Database" §14.4), "pg_stat_statements" (§F.32), "auto_explain" (§F.3), "Error Reporting and Logging" (§19.8, log_min_duration_statement), "Row Constructor Comparison" (§9.25.5), "LIMIT and OFFSET" (§7.6), "Index-Only Scans and Covering Indexes" (§11.9), "REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW", "Automatic Vacuuming" (§19.10.1), release notes 17 and 18; subselect.c (REL_18_STABLE) for when a NOT IN subquery is hashed. Checked 2026-09-30.


Query optimization means making a slow query do less work. You do not guess. You find the queries that cost the most, read their real plans, find the node where the time goes and the reason, change one thing, and measure again.


Most slow queries in real apps have a small set of causes: a missing or wrong index, a condition that cannot use an index, a bad row estimate, or an access pattern in the application (N+1 queries, deep OFFSET pages). This chapter is the practical checklist. It uses the scan types (chapter 18), index design (chapter 19), statistics (chapter 21), the join and sort algorithms (chapters 23, 24) and EXPLAIN (chapter 25).



The method



 

  
1. Find the queries that cost the mostpg_stat_statements by total_exec_time; slow query log; auto_explain

  
↓

  
2. Get the real planEXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS) with real parameter values, on production-size data

  
↓

  
3. Find the node that costs the timeexclusive time; Buffers

  
↓

  
4. Find the causeestimate vs actual; Filter vs Index Cond; Disk; loops

  
↓

  
5. Make one changean index, a rewrite, ANALYZE, a setting for one query

  
↓

  
6. Measure againsame query, same data; then watch pg_stat_statements after deploy

 

 

  
Sort by total time, not by the slowest single run. A 2 ms query called 5 million times a day costs more than a 3 s report that runs once. The pg_stat_statements extension keeps calls, total_exec_time, mean_exec_time, rows, shared_blks_hit/read and temp_blks_written per normalized query. It must be in shared_preload_libraries, so adding it needs a restart. log_min_duration_statement = 250ms logs every statement slower than that. auto_explain.log_min_duration logs the plan of slow statements (with auto_explain.log_analyze for actual numbers, at a cost to every query). Chapter 32 covers monitoring.

  
Run the query with the parameter values that are slow. A plan for customer_id = 42 can differ from the plan for a customer with 50,000 orders. Test on data of production size. The docs warn that a plan on a toy table does not predict the plan on a large one.

  
Use chapter 25: exclusive time per node, times loops. Look at Buffers to see how many pages each node touched.

  
Name the cause before you change anything. "Seq Scan with 999,990 rows removed, because there is no index on customer_id." "Estimate 1, actual 100,000 on orders, because the statistics are stale." Most causes are in the list below.

  
One change at a time. If you add an index and rewrite the query and change work_mem together, you do not know which one helped, and you keep the two that did nothing.

  
Run it again and compare time and Buffers. Run it twice, because the first run may read from disk and the second from cache. After the deploy, check the query in pg_stat_statements (pg_stat_statements_reset() clears old numbers).

 

 




-- the 10 statements that used the most execution time
SELECT calls, round(total_exec_time) AS total_ms, round(mean_exec_time::numeric, 2) AS mean_ms,
       rows, shared_blks_read, temp_blks_written, left(query, 80) AS query
FROM pg_stat_statements
ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC
LIMIT 10;



Indexes: missing, wrong order, or not usable


A missing index. PostgreSQL creates an index for a PRIMARY KEY and a UNIQUE constraint, but not for a foreign key. So orders.customer_id has no index in the example schema, and "orders of customer 42" is a Seq Scan over 1,000,000 rows. The same missing index makes DELETE FROM customers slow, because the foreign key check must find referencing orders.


-- before: Seq Scan on orders, Filter: (customer_id = 42), Rows Removed by Filter: 999990   (about 62 ms, example)
CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY orders_customer_id_created_at_idx ON orders (customer_id, created_at);
-- after: Index Scan Backward using orders_customer_id_created_at_idx, Index Cond: (customer_id = 42)   (well under 1 ms)


Use CONCURRENTLY on a live table so writes are not blocked during the build (chapters 17 and 28).


The wrong column order (chapter 19). For WHERE customer_id = $1 ORDER BY created_at DESC the index must start with the equality column: (customer_id, created_at). Then the rows of one customer sit together, already in date order. An index on (created_at, customer_id) has the rows of one customer spread over the whole index. PostgreSQL 18 can "skip scan" a leading column with few distinct values, but created_at has millions, so that does not help here.


A condition that cannot use the index. A B-tree index on a column is searched with that column's own values and operators. Three common ways to lose it:




ConditionWhy the index is not usedFix


WHERE lower(email) = $1The index stores email, not lower(email)An expression index ON customers (lower(email)), or store emails in lower case and compare email = lower($1)


WHERE created_at::date = '2026-09-29'A function (the cast) on the columnA range on the raw column: created_at >= '2026-09-29' AND created_at < '2026-09-30'


WHERE id = $1 with $1 sent as numericA type mismatch. bigint = numeric has no operator in the B-tree family for bigint, so PostgreSQL casts the column, id::numeric = $1Send the parameter with the column's type ($1::bigint, or fix the driver or ORM type mapping)


WHERE name LIKE '%phone%'A B-tree needs a fixed prefix; a leading wildcard has noneA trigram GIN index (pg_trgm, chapter 20) or full-text search




Selecting columns you do not need. SELECT * has three costs. It blocks an Index Only Scan, because the index does not hold every column (chapter 18). It fetches and decompresses large TOAST values (chapter 11) that the application then ignores. And it sends more bytes over the network. SELECT sum(total) FROM orders WHERE customer_id = $1 with an index ON orders (customer_id) INCLUDE (total) can run as an Index Only Scan. SELECT * cannot.



The N+1 query pattern


An ORM loads a list with one query, then loads a related object for each item with one more query each. For 50 orders that is 1 + 50 queries. Each query is fast on the server, so none shows up as "slow" in the log. But each one pays a network round trip, and the app waits for them one after another. The fixes send one or two queries:


-- N+1: one query for the list, then one per row
SELECT id, customer_id, total FROM orders WHERE status = 'new' ORDER BY id LIMIT 50;
SELECT id, name FROM customers WHERE id = 17;     -- repeated 50 times with different ids

-- fix 1: one JOIN
SELECT o.id, o.total, c.name
FROM orders o JOIN customers c ON c.id = o.customer_id
WHERE o.status = 'new' ORDER BY o.id LIMIT 50;

-- fix 2: the list, then all related rows in one query (what "eager loading" in most ORMs does)
SELECT id, name FROM customers WHERE id = ANY($1::bigint[]);   -- $1 = the 50 customer ids



 
Lab: N+1 queries against one JOIN

 
All times are example numbers. The round trip is the network time for one query and its answer. As a rough guide, it is well under 1 ms on the same host, around 1 ms inside one data centre, and tens of ms across regions. The server time per simple lookup is set to 0.05 ms and each extra row in a set query adds 0.005 ms (examples).

 

 

 




Slide the round trip up and the N+1 bar grows with list size × round trip. The server did almost no work. The time is waiting. N+1 also holds a pooled connection for the whole loop (chapter 29).



OFFSET pagination and keyset pagination


ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC OFFSET 100000 LIMIT 20 looks like it reads 20 rows. It reads 100,020. The Index Scan produces every row in order (and fetches each one from the heap), and the Limit node throws the first 100,000 away. The docs: "The rows skipped by an OFFSET clause still have to be computed inside the server; therefore a large OFFSET might be inefficient." Page 5,000 is 5,000 times the work of page 1. Also, if new orders arrive while a user pages, rows shift, and the user sees some rows twice.


Keyset pagination (also called seek pagination) remembers the last row of the previous page and asks for rows after it. With a row constructor comparison and a matching index, the index search starts exactly at that point:


CREATE INDEX orders_created_at_id_idx ON orders (created_at, id);   -- also read backward for DESC

-- page 1
SELECT id, created_at, total FROM orders ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC LIMIT 20;
-- next page: $1, $2 = created_at and id of the last row shown
SELECT id, created_at, total FROM orders
WHERE (created_at, id) < ($1, $2)
ORDER BY created_at DESC, id DESC LIMIT 20;


The id is there as a tie-breaker, because two orders can have the same created_at. The row comparison (a, b) < (x, y) compares left to right: a < x, or a = x AND b < y. The B-tree can use it as one Index Cond. The trade-off is that there is no "jump to page 400". Keyset gives next and previous, which is what infinite scroll and APIs with a cursor need.



 
Lab: OFFSET vs keyset on the same index

 
Each small square is one entry of the index on (created_at, id), newest first, 10 rows per page. Red is read and thrown away. Green is returned. Blue is the point where the index search starts.

 

 
OFFSET 

 

 
Keyset 

 

 

 





Rewrites that change the plan


A correlated subquery that runs per row (chapter 7). SELECT c.id, (SELECT max(o.created_at) FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id) FROM customers c runs the subquery 100,000 times, as a SubPlan. With an index each run is a lookup, so it may be fine. Without one it is 100,000 scans. A join to a grouped result does the work once: LEFT JOIN (SELECT customer_id, max(created_at) AS last_order FROM orders GROUP BY customer_id) l ON l.customer_id = c.id. For "top N per group", LATERAL with ORDER BY … LIMIT and an index on (customer_id, created_at) is the usual pattern.


OR across two tables. On one table, PostgreSQL can combine two indexes for a = 1 OR b = 2 with a BitmapOr. But WHERE c.email = $1 OR o.id = $2 after a join cannot use either index, because each side of the OR belongs to a different table. Split it into two queries that each use an index and combine them with UNION (which removes a row found by both). Use UNION ALL only if the two parts cannot overlap.


NOT IN and NOT EXISTS. They differ in meaning and in plan. If the subquery returns a NULL, x NOT IN (…) is never true, so the query returns no rows. NOT EXISTS has no such trap. The planner turns NOT EXISTS into an anti join (Hash Anti Join, or Hash Right Anti Join since PostgreSQL 16). It cannot do that for NOT IN. NOT IN runs as a SubPlan, which is hashed only if the planner expects the subquery result to fit in hash memory. The check in subselect.c counts each row as its width plus a 24-byte tuple header. 1,000,000 bigint customer ids × 32 bytes is 32MB, more than the default 8MB. So WHERE id NOT IN (SELECT customer_id FROM orders) becomes a plain SubPlan that rescans the materialized orders list for every customer. Write WHERE NOT EXISTS (SELECT 1 FROM orders o WHERE o.customer_id = c.id).


Large IN lists. WHERE id IN (1, 2, …, 5000) built as SQL text is a new statement each time. Each one costs a parse and a plan, and no prepared plan is reused. Send one array parameter instead: WHERE id = ANY($1::bigint[]). Since PostgreSQL 17 a B-tree scan finds a set of values more efficiently, and EXPLAIN ANALYZE in 18 shows how many index searches it needed. Since PostgreSQL 18, pg_stat_statements also groups IN lists of different lengths into one entry. For tens of thousands of values, join to unnest($1::bigint[]) or a temporary table instead.



Statistics, memory and big operations


Stale statistics. After a bulk load or a large delete, the statistics describe the old table. Autovacuum runs ANALYZE when the changed rows pass autovacuum_analyze_threshold (50) + autovacuum_analyze_scale_factor (0.1) × table rows, and it checks each database once per autovacuum_naptime (1 minute). A query that runs right after a load can be planned with the old numbers. The docs recommend running ANALYZE after large loads. Put it in the load job.


Correlated columns. The planner multiplies the selectivities of two conditions as if the columns were independent (chapter 21). If one column decides the other (a city decides its country), the estimate is far too low. Extended statistics fix that: CREATE STATISTICS customers_city_country (dependencies, mcv) ON city, country FROM customers; then ANALYZE customers; (this assumes the table also had a city column). For one column with a skewed distribution, a larger sample helps: ALTER TABLE orders ALTER COLUMN status SET STATISTICS 1000;.


A sort or hash that spills. Sort Method: external merge Disk or Batches: 8 means the operation did not fit in work_mem (chapters 23, 24). Raise it for that query only (SET LOCAL work_mem = '64MB' in its transaction) or for a role (ALTER ROLE reporting SET work_mem = '64MB'), or remove the sort with an index that gives the order.


count(*) on a big table must visit every visible tuple (chapter 24). Use pg_class.reltuples for an estimate, show "more than 1,000" with LIMIT 1001 on a subquery, or keep a counter.


Big updates and deletes: do them in batches. One UPDATE of 5,000,000 rows is one long transaction. It holds row locks until the end, creates 5,000,000 dead tuples that VACUUM cannot remove while it runs (chapter 13), writes a WAL spike that standbys must replay (chapter 30), and loses all the work if it fails at the end. Batches of a few thousand rows, each in its own transaction, keep locks short and let VACUUM work between them:


-- repeat until it updates 0 rows
UPDATE orders SET status = 'shipped'
WHERE id IN (SELECT id FROM orders
             WHERE status = 'paid' AND created_at < '2026-09-01'
             LIMIT 5000);


Bulk loads. The docs recommend COPY over many INSERTs ("almost always faster"), creating indexes after the load on a new table, dropping and recreating foreign keys for very large loads, raising maintenance_work_mem and max_wal_size temporarily, and running ANALYZE afterwards.



Slow, but not because of the plan


Lock waits. A query that is fast in EXPLAIN ANALYZE but slow in the app may be waiting for a lock that another transaction holds, for example an ALTER TABLE or a long transaction that updated the same rows. pg_stat_activity shows wait_event_type = 'Lock' for it. The fix is in the other transaction (chapter 28).


A bloated table. If a table has many dead tuples (chapter 13), a scan reads many pages for few rows. You see a high Buffers count for the rows returned. Fix VACUUM first, then the query.


Too many indexes. Every INSERT writes to every index. An UPDATE that changes an indexed column cannot be a HOT update and must insert into every index (chapter 17). Indexes also take space in shared buffers. pg_stat_user_indexes.idx_scan = 0 over a long period marks candidates to drop (check the standbys too, since their index use is counted there).



When the query is already good


Some queries are slow because they do a lot of real work, such as a dashboard that sums a year of orders on every page view. Then the fix is to do the work less often:


    

  • Denormalize: store a derived value, such as orders.item_count, kept up to date by the application or a trigger in the same transaction. Reads get cheap, writes get more expensive and there is a new way to get out of sync.
    • 

  • A materialized view: CREATE MATERIALIZED VIEW daily_sales AS SELECT … stores the result. REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY daily_sales recomputes it without blocking readers, but it needs a UNIQUE index on the view (plain columns, no WHERE clause). The data is as old as the last refresh.
    • 

  • A cache outside the database (for example Redis): the fastest reads, but you own invalidation and stale data.
    • 




What not to do


    

  • Do not set enable_seqscan = off (or any enable_* setting) in production. It does not forbid the node, it adds a big penalty, and it changes every plan in that session. It hides a bad estimate and breaks the queries where a Seq Scan was right. Use it in a test session to compare plans.
    • 

  • Do not add an index for every slow query. Each index slows every write and uses memory. Look for one index that serves several queries, and remove unused ones.
    • 

  • Do not trust a test on a tiny table. On 100 rows every plan is fast and the planner picks differently. Test with production-size data and realistic value distributions.
    • 

  • Do not change several things at once, and do not keep a change that did not measurably help.
    • 




Lab: the slow query clinic



 
Lab: pick a fix, see the new plan

 
Five slow queries on the shop database, each with its EXPLAIN ANALYZE excerpt (example output). Choose one fix. The lab shows the plan and time that fix gives, and why. Try the wrong fixes too: they show what does not change.

 

 

 

 

 











27. Transactions and isolation levels


Covers: BEGIN, COMMIT, ROLLBACK, autocommit, savepoints, ACID, the anomalies, the four levels as PostgreSQL implements them, lost update fixes, retries. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Introduction" to Concurrency Control (§13.1), "Transaction Isolation" (§13.2), "Explicit Locking" (§13.3), "Subtransactions" (§67.3), BEGIN, SAVEPOINT, SET TRANSACTION, INSERT (ON CONFLICT), "Index Uniqueness Checks" (§63.5), "PostgreSQL Error Codes" (Appendix A), "Client Connection Defaults" (§19.11); source tree src/backend/executor/README (EvalPlanQual) and src/backend/storage/lmgr/README-SSI. Checked 2026-09-30.


A transaction is a group of statements that the database treats as one unit. All of them take effect, or none of them do. The isolation level of a transaction sets how much it can see of other transactions that run at the same time.



A shop has many users at the same moment. Two customers buy the last desk lamp. An admin changes a price while a report adds up the prices. Without rules, the results depend on the exact order in which the statements of different sessions arrive. Transactions give two promises. A group of changes is never half done. And a transaction is protected, to a level that you choose, from the changes of other transactions.


This chapter uses two extra tables. The rows are example data.


ALTER TABLE products ADD COLUMN stock int NOT NULL DEFAULT 0 CHECK (stock >= 0);
CREATE TABLE staff (id int PRIMARY KEY, name text NOT NULL, on_call boolean NOT NULL);



BEGIN, COMMIT, ROLLBACK and autocommit


If you do not send BEGIN, PostgreSQL runs each statement in its own transaction and commits it at the end of the statement. This is autocommit. One UPDATE that changes 10,000 rows is still all or nothing. To group statements, send BEGIN, then the statements, then COMMIT (keep the changes) or ROLLBACK (discard them).


BEGIN;
INSERT INTO orders (customer_id, status, total) VALUES (1, 'new', 40.00) RETURNING id;   -- say 1000001
INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, qty, price) VALUES (1000001, 7, 1, 40.00);
UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 7 AND stock >= 1;
COMMIT;


If one statement in a transaction block fails, the whole transaction is marked as failed. Every later statement gets ERROR: current transaction is aborted, commands ignored until end of transaction block (SQLSTATE 25P02). A COMMIT of a failed transaction does a rollback and replies ROLLBACK. The app must check for this and must not think the data is saved.


Some things are not rolled back. A sequence (and so an identity column such as orders.id) gives each number only once, even if the transaction that took it rolls back. Gaps in orders.id are normal.



Savepoints


A savepoint is a mark inside a transaction. ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT s discards only the work after the mark. The transaction continues. RELEASE SAVEPOINT s removes the mark and keeps the work.


BEGIN;
INSERT INTO orders (customer_id, status, total) VALUES (2, 'new', 60.00);
SAVEPOINT try_coupon;
INSERT INTO order_items VALUES (1000002, 999999, 1, 0.00);   -- fails: no product 999999
ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT try_coupon;                            -- the order row stays
COMMIT;


Each savepoint is a subtransaction, and each subtransaction that writes gets its own XID. The docs say that a backend caches up to 64 open subtransaction XIDs in shared memory. After that, lookups go to pg_subtrans and the I/O cost grows a lot. Drivers or ORMs that make a savepoint around every statement can reach that limit. A PL/pgSQL EXCEPTION block also starts a subtransaction.



ACID, and what gives each letter




LetterPromiseHow PostgreSQL keeps it


AtomicityAll changes of a transaction take effect, or none do.Every tuple carries the XID that wrote it (chapter 12). The commit log (pg_xact) records per XID "committed" or "aborted". An aborted XID's tuples are never visible, so a rollback does not have to undo anything on the pages. VACUUM removes them later (chapter 13).


ConsistencyA transaction moves the data from one valid state to another.Constraints: NOT NULL, CHECK, UNIQUE, PRIMARY KEY, FOREIGN KEY (chapter 2). Rules that no constraint can express (such as "at least one person on call") are your job, and need the right isolation level or locks.


IsolationConcurrent transactions do not see each other's half-done work.MVCC snapshots (chapter 12) for reads. Row locks and table locks (chapter 28) for writers. Predicate locks at Serializable.


DurabilityAfter COMMIT returns, the change survives a crash.The commit record is written to the WAL and flushed to disk before COMMIT returns, with the default synchronous_commit = on (chapter 15). After a crash, WAL replay rebuilds the pages.





The anomalies


An anomaly is a result that concurrent transactions can produce but that no one-at-a-time order of the same transactions can produce. The SQL standard names four phenomena. Research on snapshot-based databases added more names. Each row below uses the shop.




AnomalyWhat happensShop example


Dirty readA transaction reads data that another transaction wrote but has not committed.An admin sets the lamp price to 30, then rolls back. A checkout already charged 30.


Non-repeatable readA transaction reads a row twice and gets different values, because another transaction changed it and committed in between.A checkout reads the price 40, shows it, and reads it again for the invoice. It gets 45.


Phantom readA transaction runs the same query with a WHERE clause twice, and the set of matching rows changes because another transaction inserted or deleted rows.A report counts new orders (2), then lists them (3 rows). The count and the list do not agree.


Lost updateTwo transactions read the same value, each computes a new value in the app, and each writes it back. The second write erases the first.Two checkouts read stock 5 and each write 4. Two lamps are sold, but the stock shows only one.


Read skewA transaction reads two related rows at different moments and sees a mix of old and new data.A report reads orders.total of order 1001 (25.00). Another transaction adds an item and updates the total to 37.00, then commits. The report now reads the items of 1001 and gets a sum of 37.00, which does not match the 25.00 it has.


Write skewTwo transactions read the same data, then each changes a different row based on what it read. Each is correct alone. Together they break a rule.The shop rule is that at least one staff member is on call. Eva and Finn both see 2 on call, and both go off call. Now 0 are on call.


Serialization anomalyThe general case: the committed result matches no serial order of the transactions. Write skew is one example.The docs example in §13.2.3. Each transaction sums one class of rows and inserts the sum into the other class.





The four levels, as PostgreSQL implements them


The SQL standard defines four levels by which phenomena each level must prevent. A database may prevent more. PostgreSQL does, and it has only three distinct levels inside. Table 13.1 of the docs, with the lost update, read skew and write skew added from the behaviour described in §13.2:




LevelDirty readNon-repeatable readPhantom readLost update (read, then write)Read skewWrite skew / serialization anomaly


Read Uncommittedstandard: allowed
PostgreSQL: not possible		possiblepossiblepossiblepossiblepossible


Read Committed (default)not possiblepossiblepossiblepossiblepossiblepossible


Repeatable Readnot possiblenot possiblestandard: allowed
PostgreSQL: not possible		not possible (error 40001)not possiblepossible


Serializablenot possiblenot possiblenot possiblenot possible (error 40001)not possiblenot possible (error 40001)




You pick the level per transaction:


BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL REPEATABLE READ;
-- or:  BEGIN;  SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE;   (before the first query)
SHOW default_transaction_isolation;   -- 'read committed'


The level cannot change after the first query or data-changing statement of the transaction. default_transaction_isolation sets the default for a session, a role or a database.



Read Uncommitted


You can ask for it, but PostgreSQL runs it as Read Committed. MVCC always keeps the old committed tuple next to the new uncommitted one, so there is no reason to show uncommitted data. No level in PostgreSQL gives dirty reads.



Read Committed: a new snapshot for each statement


Each statement takes a new snapshot when it starts (chapter 12 explains snapshots). It sees all data committed before that moment, plus the changes of its own transaction. Two SELECTs in one transaction can give different results, because a commit can happen between them. That is the non-repeatable read and the phantom.


Writes need more. An UPDATE, DELETE or SELECT ... FOR UPDATE finds its target rows with its snapshot. But by the time it reaches a row, another transaction can have changed or locked that row. PostgreSQL cannot write a new tuple on top of a tuple that is not the newest. So it does this:



 

  
B: DELETE FROM products WHERE stock = 10snapshot finds row 10 with stock = 10

  
↓ the tuple's xmax is XID 101, in progress

  
B waits for transaction 101 to endwait event: transactionid

  
↓ 101 commits

  
B follows the update chain to the newest tuplestock = 11

  
↓

  
Re-check the WHERE clause on the newest tuple11 = 10? no

  
↓

  
Skip the row, or apply the change to the newest tuple

 

 

  
The statement scans with its own snapshot. It only finds rows that were committed when the statement started.

  
The row it found was changed by transaction 101, which is still running. B does not know yet if that change will commit. B waits on 101's lock. It does not read, it does not guess.

  
If 101 rolls back, B continues with the row it found. If 101 deleted the row, B skips it. If 101 updated the row, B moves to the newest tuple of that row.

  
B evaluates its WHERE clause again, on the newest tuple only. The source code calls this the EvalPlanQual recheck. Other rows are not scanned again.

  
If the newest tuple still matches, B updates or deletes it, and an UPDATE computes SET from the newest values. So stock = stock - 1 counts down correctly from the value the other transaction wrote.

 

 




This is right for simple statements that target known rows. UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 7 always works from the newest stock. But the recheck looks at the new version of the rows it already found, and not at other rows. The statement can see a mix of two states. The docs give an example, which the lab below reproduces with products 9 and 10. Transaction A adds 1 to the stock of both rows (9 to 10, 10 to 11). At the same time, B runs DELETE FROM products WHERE stock = 10. B deletes nothing. Its snapshot saw row 9 with stock 9, so it skipped it. The recheck of row 10 saw 11. Before A and after A there is a row with stock 10, but B found none. For statements with complex conditions, use a stricter level.



Repeatable Read: one snapshot for the whole transaction


The snapshot is taken at the first query or data-changing statement of the transaction (not at BEGIN) and is kept to the end. All reads see the same data. There are no non-repeatable reads, no phantoms, no read skew. The research name for this is snapshot isolation.


Writes follow a stricter rule. If the target row was changed by a transaction that committed after our snapshot, there is no safe recheck. Our transaction would have to act on data that its snapshot cannot see. So it waits for the other transaction (if it is still running), and then:


    

  • if the other transaction rolled back, it continues;
    • 

  • if it committed and changed or deleted the row, our transaction fails with ERROR: could not serialize access due to concurrent update, SQLSTATE 40001 (serialization_failure). If the other transaction only locked the row, there is no error.
    • 



This stops the lost update. The app must roll back and run the whole transaction again. On the retry the new snapshot contains the other change. Read-only transactions never get this error.


Repeatable Read is still not serializable. Two transactions that read the same rows and then write different rows do not conflict on any row, so both commit. That is write skew. The docs also describe a read-only case. A report can see a batch marked as complete but miss one detail row of that batch.



Serializable: Serializable Snapshot Isolation


Serializable works like Repeatable Read, plus a monitor. The monitor looks for patterns of reads and writes that could give a result that matches no serial order. When it finds one, it rolls back one transaction with ERROR: could not serialize access due to read/write dependencies among transactions, also SQLSTATE 40001. The method is Serializable Snapshot Isolation (SSI), used since PostgreSQL 9.1.


    

  1. Every read takes a predicate lock (mode SIReadLock in pg_locks) on what it read (a tuple, a page, or the whole table after a sequential scan). These locks never block anyone and cannot cause a deadlock. They only record "this transaction read this".
    2. 

  2. When a transaction writes data that a concurrent transaction read (or reads data that a concurrent transaction wrote and it cannot see), PostgreSQL records a rw-conflict. It means that the reader must come before the writer in any serial order.
    3. 

  3. Every anomaly under snapshot isolation contains a dangerous structure, which is two rw-conflicts in a row, Tin → Tpivot → Tout. When one forms, and Tout committed first, PostgreSQL rolls back a transaction in it. In the write skew case, Eva's transaction read Finn's row and Finn's transaction read Eva's row. The arrows go both ways, so each is a pivot, and the second one to commit fails.
    4. 



The check can give false positives: a dangerous structure is not always part of a real cycle. The app sees an extra 40001 now and then. The cost is the tracking work plus the retries. The docs give this advice for Serializable:


    

  • declare transactions READ ONLY when you can (they can often skip predicate locks);
    • 

  • control the number of active connections (use a pool, chapter 29);
    • 

  • put no more into one transaction than integrity needs, and do not leave sessions "idle in transaction";
    • 

  • remove explicit locks and FOR UPDATE that Serializable makes unnecessary;
    • 

  • a sequential scan takes a predicate lock on the whole table, so more index scans can mean fewer failures; if the predicate lock table is short of memory, fine locks merge into coarse ones and failures increase (max_pred_locks_per_transaction, default 64).
    • 



For long reports, BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE READ ONLY DEFERRABLE waits until it can get a snapshot that is safe, then runs with no SSI cost and no risk of 40001. Data read by any other Serializable transaction is valid only after that transaction commits.




 
Lab: two sessions, one timeline

 
Pick a scenario and a level, then step through. Each result is computed by a small MVCC model. It has tuples with xmin and xmax, a snapshot per statement or per transaction, row locks that make a writer wait, the EvalPlanQual recheck and the 40001 checks. The Serializable check is a simplified form of SSI that checks at COMMIT. Real PostgreSQL can raise the error at an earlier statement.

 

  

 

 

 

 

 
Tuples in the table (committed data plus the versions the two sessions wrote). A tuple is visible to a snapshot if its xmin is committed in that snapshot (or is the session's own XID) and its xmax is not.

 




Things to try. In the lost update at Read Committed, B's UPDATE waits, then writes the stale 4. At Repeatable Read the same step fails with 40001. In the write skew at Repeatable Read, both commit and nobody is on call. At Serializable the second COMMIT fails. In the recheck scenario at Read Committed, B waits, the recheck fails, and the result is DELETE 0.



Patterns for a production app


Fixing the lost update


The problem is a read in one statement and a write in another, with app code between them. There are four common fixes.


-- 1. Atomic update: let the database do the arithmetic. Works at Read Committed.
UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 7 AND stock >= 1;   -- UPDATE 0 means sold out

-- 2. Pessimistic lock: lock the row when you read it. Others wait.
BEGIN;
SELECT stock FROM products WHERE id = 7 FOR UPDATE;
UPDATE products SET stock = 4 WHERE id = 7;     -- value computed in the app
COMMIT;

-- 3. Optimistic lock: a version column. Nobody waits long; a loser retries.
--    (needs: ALTER TABLE products ADD COLUMN version int NOT NULL DEFAULT 1)
SELECT stock, version FROM products WHERE id = 7;                                   -- 5, 1
UPDATE products SET stock = 4, version = 2 WHERE id = 7 AND version = 1;            -- UPDATE 0 means "someone else won, read again"

-- 4. Repeatable Read or Serializable, and retry on 40001.


The atomic update is the best choice when the new value can be written in SQL. It is one statement, it holds the row lock for the shortest time, and the Read Committed recheck makes stock >= 1 safe. FOR UPDATE fits when the app must decide something between the read and the write, but it holds the lock across a network round trip. The version column fits when the read and the write are far apart, for example a form that a user edits for a minute. A transaction must not stay open for that minute.




 
Lab: two checkouts buy the same lamp

 
Both sessions run the same app code, and the scheduler runs their statements in turns, so both read before either writes. Pick a fix and a start stock (example values) and watch who waits and who retries. XIDs are shown at BEGIN for clarity. PostgreSQL gives a transaction an XID at its first write.

 

  
  

 

 




With the start stock at 1 and "read, then write", both customers get a lamp that exists only once. The shop oversells. Every fix sells exactly one lamp. The atomic update and FOR UPDATE pay with a wait. The version column and Serializable pay with a retry.



"Check, then insert" needs a unique constraint


A signup handler that runs SELECT 1 FROM customers WHERE email = $1 and inserts if nothing came back is a race at every level below Serializable. Two requests both see nothing and both insert. The only reliable guard is a unique index, because the index insert checks the key against all tuples, including uncommitted ones (the second insert waits for the first transaction, then fails with 23505 unique_violation if the first committed). Let the constraint decide:


INSERT INTO customers (email, name, country) VALUES ('ana@acmeshop.com', 'Ana', 'DE')
ON CONFLICT (email) DO NOTHING
RETURNING id;          -- no row back means the email already exists

INSERT INTO order_items (order_id, product_id, qty, price) VALUES (1001, 7, 1, 40.00)
ON CONFLICT (order_id, product_id) DO UPDATE SET qty = order_items.qty + EXCLUDED.qty;


The docs say ON CONFLICT DO UPDATE guarantees an atomic insert-or-update outcome, even under high concurrency. At Read Committed it can update a row that was inserted by a concurrent transaction that its snapshot cannot see.



Retry logic


Two SQLSTATEs mean that the transaction was rolled back only because of other transactions, and that it can succeed if you run it again. They are 40001 serialization_failure and 40P01 deadlock_detected (chapter 28). Retry the whole transaction from BEGIN, with fresh reads, because the old reads are the reason it failed. Do not retry only the last statement.


for attempt in 1..5:
    try:
        BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL SERIALIZABLE
        ... all reads and writes ...
        COMMIT
        return ok
    except SQLSTATE in ('40001', '40P01'):
        ROLLBACK
        sleep(random(0, 2^attempt * 10 ms))    -- example backoff with jitter
raise "gave up"


Side effects outside the database (an email, a payment call) must not happen inside the retried block, or they happen twice. Also note that a 40001 can arrive on COMMIT, and that a lost connection during COMMIT leaves you not knowing if it committed. Make the operation safe to repeat, for example with an idempotency key under a unique constraint.



Keep transactions short


An open transaction holds its row locks and table locks to the end (chapter 28). Its snapshot also stops VACUUM from removing dead tuples that it could still see (chapter 13). So do no network calls, file uploads or user waits inside a transaction. Read what you need, call the payment service, and then open a short transaction to write. Two timeouts put a limit on mistakes. idle_in_transaction_session_timeout ends a session that sits idle inside a transaction, and transaction_timeout (since PostgreSQL 17) ends a transaction that runs too long. Both are off (0) by default.




NeedGood choice


Most web requests, single-row changesRead Committed with atomic updates and constraints


A report that must see one consistent stateRepeatable Read, or Serializable Read Only Deferrable


Business rules across rows that no constraint expressesSerializable plus retry, or explicit locks in one fixed order


"Create if not exists"Unique constraint plus INSERT ... ON CONFLICT











28. Locks, deadlocks and safe schema changes


Covers: the two layers (MVCC and locks), the eight table lock modes and their conflicts, the four row lock modes, NOWAIT and SKIP LOCKED, advisory locks, the lock queue, deadlocks, how to see locks, safe schema changes and backfills. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "Introduction" to Concurrency Control (§13.1), "Explicit Locking" (§13.3, tables 13.2 and 13.3), "Lock Management" (§19.12), "Client Connection Defaults" (§19.11), "What to Log" (§19.8), ALTER TABLE (description and notes), CREATE INDEX ("Building Indexes Concurrently"), SELECT ("The Locking Clause"), pg_locks (§53.12), "The Cumulative Statistics System" (wait events), "System Information Functions" (pg_blocking_pids), release notes 11, 12 and 18; source tree src/backend/storage/lmgr/README and src/backend/access/heap/README.tuplock. Checked 2026-09-30.


A lock is a mark kept by the database that says "this transaction uses this object in this way, and others that want a conflicting use must wait". PostgreSQL takes locks for you on tables and rows. Most of the time you do not see them. You see them when something waits.



Two layers: MVCC for reads, locks for the rest


MVCC (chapter 12) lets a reader see an old tuple while a writer makes a new one. So a plain SELECT never waits for an UPDATE, and an UPDATE never waits for a SELECT. The docs state it as "reading never blocks writing and writing never blocks reading".


MVCC does not solve two things. First, two writers of the same row. Only one can make the next tuple, so the second must wait (chapter 27 shows what it does after the wait). Second, changes to the table itself. A reader must not find that a column or the whole heap file was replaced while it reads. For those, PostgreSQL has table-level locks and row-level locks. Both are held until the end of the transaction. A ROLLBACK TO SAVEPOINT releases the locks taken after the savepoint.



Table-level locks


There are eight modes. The names are historical. All of them are table locks, even the ones with "ROW" in the name. The only real difference between the modes is which other modes they conflict with. Two transactions cannot hold conflicting modes on the same table at the same time. A transaction never conflicts with itself.




ModeTaken by (examples)


ACCESS SHARESELECT, and any query that only reads the table


ROW SHARESELECT ... FOR UPDATE / NO KEY UPDATE / SHARE / KEY SHARE


ROW EXCLUSIVEINSERT, UPDATE, DELETE, MERGE


SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVEVACUUM (not FULL), ANALYZE, CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, REINDEX CONCURRENTLY, CREATE STATISTICS, ALTER TABLE ... VALIDATE CONSTRAINT, some other ALTER TABLE forms


SHARECREATE INDEX (without CONCURRENTLY)


SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVECREATE TRIGGER, ALTER TABLE ... ADD FOREIGN KEY (on both tables)


EXCLUSIVEREFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW CONCURRENTLY


ACCESS EXCLUSIVEDROP TABLE, TRUNCATE, REINDEX, CLUSTER, VACUUM FULL, REFRESH MATERIALIZED VIEW, most ALTER TABLE forms, LOCK TABLE with no mode




Table 13.2 of the docs. X means the two modes conflict. The table is symmetric.




Requested \ HeldACCESS SHAREROW SHAREROW EXCL.SHARE UPDATE EXCL.SHARESHARE ROW EXCL.EXCL.ACCESS EXCL.


ACCESS SHAREX


ROW SHAREXX


ROW EXCL.XXXX


SHARE UPDATE EXCL.XXXXX


SHAREXXXXX


SHARE ROW EXCL.XXXXXX


EXCL.XXXXXXX


ACCESS EXCL.XXXXXXXX




Read the table by the purpose of each mode. SHARE (a normal CREATE INDEX) lets reads through but stops writes, because the build reads the heap once and must not miss new tuples. SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE conflicts with itself, so two VACUUMs or a VACUUM and a CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY cannot run on one table at the same time, but reads and writes continue. ACCESS EXCLUSIVE conflicts with everything. It is the only mode that blocks a plain SELECT.



Row-level locks


Row locks only block writers and lockers of the same row. They never block a plain read.




ModeTaken byBlocks


FOR UPDATESELECT ... FOR UPDATE, DELETE, and an UPDATE that changes a column with a unique index usable by a foreign keyevery other row lock


FOR NO KEY UPDATESELECT ... FOR NO KEY UPDATE, and every other UPDATEall except FOR KEY SHARE


FOR SHARESELECT ... FOR SHAREFOR NO KEY UPDATE, FOR UPDATE


FOR KEY SHARESELECT ... FOR KEY SHARE, and the foreign key check on the referenced rowFOR UPDATE only




Table 13.3 of the docs:




Requested \ HeldFOR KEY SHAREFOR SHAREFOR NO KEY UPDATEFOR UPDATE


FOR KEY SHAREX


FOR SHAREXX


FOR NO KEY UPDATEXXX


FOR UPDATEXXXX




The two weak modes exist for foreign keys. When you insert into order_items, PostgreSQL must be sure that product 7 is not deleted before the insert commits. It locks the products row FOR KEY SHARE. A concurrent UPDATE products SET price = 45 WHERE id = 7 takes FOR NO KEY UPDATE, which does not conflict. So the price change and the order insert do not wait for each other. Only a DELETE of product 7 or a change of its id would wait.


Row locks are not kept in memory. A row lock is written into the tuple header. The locker's XID goes into xmax, with flag bits that say "locked, not deleted" (see chapter 12 for xmax). When more than one transaction holds a share lock, the header points to a MultiXact, a small list of XIDs. So there is no limit on how many rows one transaction can lock. The cost is that SELECT ... FOR UPDATE writes to the page, which makes the page dirty and writes WAL. A transaction that waits for a row lock actually waits for the holder's transaction to end. In pg_stat_activity this shows as wait event transactionid (or tuple for the next waiters in line).



NOWAIT and SKIP LOCKED


By default, a locker waits. NOWAIT makes it fail at once with SQLSTATE 55P03 (lock_not_available) if a row is locked. SKIP LOCKED makes it skip the locked rows and return the rest. Skipped rows give an inconsistent view of the data, so use it only for queue-like tables. That is the job queue pattern. Several job runners (app processes) take jobs from one table, and no two take the same job:


CREATE TABLE jobs (
  id       bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,
  kind     text NOT NULL,          -- 'send_invoice', ...
  order_id bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES orders(id),
  done_at  timestamptz
);
CREATE INDEX ON jobs (id) WHERE done_at IS NULL;

-- each job runner, in a loop:
BEGIN;
SELECT id, kind, order_id FROM jobs
WHERE done_at IS NULL
ORDER BY id
LIMIT 1
FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED;
-- ... do the job ...
UPDATE jobs SET done_at = now() WHERE id = 42;   -- the id from the SELECT
COMMIT;


Runner 1 locks job 42. Runner 2 runs the same query, skips 42 without waiting, and locks 43. If a runner crashes, its transaction rolls back and the lock goes away, so job 42 comes back. Keep the job short, because the transaction stays open while it runs.



Advisory locks


An advisory lock locks a number that means something only to your app. PostgreSQL does not check that you use it. In this example, only one server may run the nightly invoice job.


SELECT pg_try_advisory_lock(1001);      -- true: you run the job. false: another session has it.
-- ... job ...
SELECT pg_advisory_unlock(1001);

BEGIN;
SELECT pg_advisory_xact_lock(7);        -- transaction level: released at COMMIT or ROLLBACK
...
COMMIT;


A session-level lock stays until you unlock it or the session ends, even if the transaction rolls back. A transaction-level lock goes away at the end of the transaction. Advisory locks live in the shared lock table, whose size comes from max_locks_per_transaction (default 64) and max_connections, so do not take millions of them. With a transaction-mode connection pool (chapter 29), a session-level lock stays on a server connection that another client may get next. Use the transaction-level form there.



The lock queue


Waiting lock requests on one table form a queue. The lock manager grants a new request at once only if it conflicts with no held lock and no request that is already waiting. Otherwise it joins the end of the queue. When a lock is released, waiters are woken in queue order, and a waiter is skipped if it conflicts with an earlier waiter that is still waiting. This order stops a steady stream of readers from starving a writer forever. It also causes the most common schema change outage.



 

  
S1: BEGIN; SELECT ... FROM orders; then idleholds ACCESS SHARE

  
↑ waits for S1

  
S2: ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN note text;wants ACCESS EXCLUSIVE, first in the queue

  
↑ wait behind S2

  
SELECTACCESS SHARE
UPDATEROW EXCLUSIVE
INSERTROW EXCLUSIVE
SELECT ...

  
↓

  
Connection pool full, request timeouts, the shop is down

  
SET lock_timeout = '2s'; S2 gives up with 55P03, the queue drains, retry later

 

 

  
A report or a forgotten session has an open transaction that read orders. It holds ACCESS SHARE until it ends. That alone blocks nothing, since everyone else also only needs modes that do not conflict with ACCESS SHARE.

  
A migration runs ADD COLUMN. The change itself takes milliseconds, but it needs ACCESS EXCLUSIVE, which conflicts with S1's lock. So it waits, for as long as S1 stays open.

  
Every new statement on orders, even a plain SELECT, conflicts with the waiting ACCESS EXCLUSIVE request. They queue behind it. pg_blocking_pids() reports them as blocked by S2 (a "soft" block), not by S1.

  
The app's connections all wait on orders. The pool runs out, and requests that do not touch orders also fail. A fast DDL statement has stopped the table because it waited.

  
The fix is to let the DDL give up. With lock_timeout set, the ALTER fails after 2 seconds (example value), the queued statements run at once, and the migration tool retries later. Before a migration, also look for long open transactions and end them.

 

 




SET lock_timeout = '2s';          -- per session, only for the migration
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN note text;
-- ERROR:  canceling statement due to lock timeout   -> sleep, then retry


lock_timeout counts each lock wait on its own. The default is 0 (no limit). The docs advise against setting it in postgresql.conf, because it would affect all sessions.




 
Lab: a lock manager for the orders table

 
Start statements in up to four sessions. The lab grants or queues each table lock by Table 13.2 and the queue rule above, then takes the row lock on order 1001 by Table 13.3. Statements marked autocommit end as soon as they get their lock. The others hold their locks until you click COMMIT / finish. Click "Load the pile-up" for the case in the diagram, then click COMMIT on S1, or turn on lock_timeout and advance time.

 

 

 

 

 




Things to try. Hold an UPDATE in S1 and run CREATE INDEX in S2. The index build waits for ROW EXCLUSIVE to go away. Then run a plain SELECT in S3. It passes the waiting build, because ACCESS SHARE does not conflict with SHARE. Replace the build with CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY. It gets its lock at once, then waits for the older transaction instead of blocking writers. Two UPDATEs of order 1001 both get ROW EXCLUSIVE, and the second waits on the row.



Deadlocks


A deadlock is a cycle of waits. Transaction A holds a lock that B wants, and B holds a lock that A wants. Neither can go on. It needs no explicit locks. Two checkouts that update the same two products in opposite order are enough.


-- A (cart: lamp, then shade)                     -- B (cart: shade, then lamp)
UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 7;
                                                  UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 9;
UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 9;   -- waits for B
                                                  UPDATE products SET stock = stock - 1 WHERE id = 7;   -- waits for A


PostgreSQL does not check for deadlocks on each wait, because the check is costly and most waits end soon. A waiting backend sets a timer for deadlock_timeout (default 1 s). When the timer fires and it still waits, it builds the wait-for graph (who waits for whom, from the lock table) and looks for a cycle through itself. If there is none, it goes back to waiting. If there is one, it breaks the cycle, usually by aborting its own transaction. The docs say that which transaction is aborted is hard to predict, so do not rely on it. The aborted transaction gets:


ERROR:  deadlock detected
DETAIL:  Process 4101 waits for ShareLock on transaction 102; blocked by process 4102.
Process 4102 waits for ShareLock on transaction 101; blocked by process 4101.
HINT:  See server log for query details.


That is SQLSTATE 40P01. The other transaction then gets its lock and continues. The app must retry the aborted one (chapter 27 has the retry loop). To prevent deadlocks, take locks in one fixed order everywhere. For a cart, sort the product ids before you update, or lock them all first with SELECT id FROM products WHERE id IN (7, 9) ORDER BY id FOR UPDATE. Also take the strongest lock you will need first, and keep transactions short.



 
Lab: two checkouts, two rows

 
Step through the events. Statements arrive 100 ms apart (example timing). Watch the wait-for graph. Change deadlock_timeout and see when the check runs. Then make both sessions use the same order.

 

  

 

 

 




A small deadlock_timeout finds real deadlocks sooner but runs more useless checks. The docs suggest a value above your typical transaction time. In the same order case, B waits for A once, the check finds no cycle, and both commit.



How to see locks


-- Who waits, and for whom:
SELECT pid, pg_blocking_pids(pid) AS blocked_by, wait_event_type, wait_event,
       state, now() - xact_start AS xact_age, left(query, 60) AS query
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE wait_event_type = 'Lock';

-- Every lock on orders:
SELECT l.pid, l.mode, l.granted, l.waitstart
FROM pg_locks l
WHERE l.relation = 'orders'::regclass
ORDER BY l.granted DESC, l.waitstart;

-- Long open transactions (the usual root cause):
SELECT pid, state, now() - xact_start AS age, left(query, 60)
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE xact_start IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY xact_start
LIMIT 5;


pg_locks shows one row per lock held or awaited, with granted = false for waiters. pg_blocking_pids(pid) lists both hard blocks (a holder of a conflicting lock) and soft blocks (a waiter ahead in the queue with a conflicting request). The docs warn that frequent calls cost something, because the function must briefly lock the lock manager's shared state. log_lock_waits = on (default off) writes a log line when a session waits longer than deadlock_timeout. PostgreSQL 18 added log_lock_failures, which logs lock failures such as SELECT ... NOWAIT. idle_in_transaction_session_timeout (chapter 27) ends forgotten sessions that hold locks.



Safe schema changes on a busy table


The question for each change has three parts: which lock it takes, for how long, and if it scans or rewrites the table. An ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock held for a few milliseconds is fine, if you use lock_timeout so that the wait cannot pile up. An ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock held during a rewrite of orders (1,000,000 rows, example) stops the shop for the whole rewrite.




ChangeLockCostSafe recipe


ADD COLUMN note textACCESS EXCLUSIVE, shortMetadata onlyRun with lock_timeout and retry.


ADD COLUMN gift boolean DEFAULT falseACCESS EXCLUSIVE, shortMetadata only since PostgreSQL 11. The default is stored in the catalog and returned for old rows.Same. NOT NULL with a constant default is also fast. NOT NULL with no default fails if the table has rows.


ADD COLUMN seen_at timestamptz DEFAULT clock_timestamp()ACCESS EXCLUSIVE, longA volatile default rewrites the table and its indexes. Same for stored generated and identity columns.Add the column with no default, set the default for new rows, backfill old rows in batches.


ALTER COLUMN ... SET NOT NULLACCESS EXCLUSIVEFull scan under the lockSee the recipe below. Since PostgreSQL 12 the scan is skipped if a valid CHECK proves no NULLs.


ADD FOREIGN KEYSHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE on both tablesScan to check every row, writes blockedADD CONSTRAINT ... NOT VALID, then VALIDATE CONSTRAINT (SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE, plus ROW SHARE on the referenced table).


ADD CHECK (...)ACCESS EXCLUSIVEScan under the lockNOT VALID, then VALIDATE CONSTRAINT.


CREATE INDEXSHAREWrites blocked for the whole buildCREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY (chapter 17). It cannot run inside a transaction block and can leave an invalid index on failure.


ADD UNIQUE (email) or ADD PRIMARY KEYACCESS EXCLUSIVEBuilds the index under the lockCREATE UNIQUE INDEX CONCURRENTLY, then ADD CONSTRAINT ... UNIQUE USING INDEX (fast).
