ALTER COLUMN ... TYPEACCESS EXCLUSIVE, longUsually rewrites the table and rebuilds its indexes (up to double the disk space). No rewrite if the old type is binary coercible, for example varchar to text.Expand and contract (below).


DROP COLUMNACCESS EXCLUSIVE, shortMetadata only. The data stays on disk until rows are rewritten.First deploy app code that no longer reads the column.


RENAME COLUMNACCESS EXCLUSIVE, shortMetadata onlyFast for the database, but every running app server that uses the old name fails. Use expand and contract, or a view.


VACUUM FULL, CLUSTERACCESS EXCLUSIVE, longRewrites the tableAvoid on a busy table. Chapter 13 covers bloat.




Adding a CHECK constraint with NOT VALID and validating it later works because new and changed rows are checked from the moment the constraint exists. The validation only has to scan the old rows, so it does not need to block writes.


Making a column NOT NULL


-- Up to PostgreSQL 17:
ALTER TABLE orders ADD CONSTRAINT orders_total_nn CHECK (total IS NOT NULL) NOT VALID;  -- short lock, no scan
ALTER TABLE orders VALIDATE CONSTRAINT orders_total_nn;          -- scans, SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE, writes go on
ALTER TABLE orders ALTER COLUMN total SET NOT NULL;              -- short lock, scan skipped (valid CHECK)
ALTER TABLE orders DROP CONSTRAINT orders_total_nn;

-- PostgreSQL 18: a not-null constraint can itself be NOT VALID
ALTER TABLE orders ADD CONSTRAINT orders_total_nn NOT NULL total NOT VALID;
ALTER TABLE orders VALIDATE CONSTRAINT orders_total_nn;



Expand and contract


To change orders.total from numeric(12,2) to whole cents in bigint, do not rewrite 1,000,000 rows under one lock. Split the change into steps that each take only a short lock, and change the app between steps:


    

  1. Expand. ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN total_cents bigint; (metadata only).
    2. 

  2. Deploy app code that writes both columns (or add a trigger that does it).
    3. 

  3. Backfill old rows in small batches (below).
    4. 

  4. Add NOT NULL by the safe route above.
    5. 

  5. Deploy app code that reads total_cents, then code that stops writing total.
    6. 

  6. Contract. ALTER TABLE orders DROP COLUMN total; (metadata only).
    7. 



A rename works the same way. Old and new app versions run side by side during a deploy, so each step must work with both.


Backfills in batches


-- Run in a loop, each batch in its own short transaction, until it reports UPDATE 0.
UPDATE orders SET total_cents = round(total * 100)::bigint
WHERE id IN (
  SELECT id FROM orders
  WHERE total_cents IS NULL
  ORDER BY id
  LIMIT 5000                      -- example batch size
);


One UPDATE of the whole table would hold row locks on all 1,000,000 rows until it ends, block every checkout that touches an order, make 1,000,000 dead tuples in one go, and write a large burst of WAL that standbys (read replicas) must replay (chapter 30). Small batches hold each lock for a moment, let autovacuum keep up, and can stop and restart at any time. The WHERE total_cents IS NULL makes a restart safe. Pause between batches if standbys fall behind.


One more edge case from the ALTER TABLE notes: the forms that rewrite the table are not MVCC-safe. A concurrent transaction with a snapshot older than the rewrite sees the table as empty.








29. Connections and connection pooling


Covers: the cost of a connection, max_connections, why app-side pools are not enough, PgBouncer and its pool modes, pool sizing, server-side timeouts, idle in transaction. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs §51.2 "How Connections Are Established", §19.3 "Connections and Authentication" (max_connections), §19.4 "Resource Consumption" (work_mem), §19.11 "Client Connection Defaults" (statement_timeout, lock_timeout, idle_in_transaction_session_timeout, idle_session_timeout, transaction_timeout), §19.12 "Lock Management" (max_locks_per_transaction), PostgreSQL 14 release notes (snapshot scalability); PgBouncer docs "Features" and "Configuration" (pgbouncer.org, PgBouncer 1.26), PgBouncer changelog 1.21.0; PostgreSQL wiki "Number Of Database Connections". Checked 2026-09-30.


A connection is one client session with the server. PostgreSQL serves each connection with its own backend process. A connection pool keeps a small number of connections open and lends them to many clients in turn, so the number of backends stays near what the hardware can run at once.



What one connection costs


PostgreSQL uses a "process per user" model (§51.2). The postmaster listens on the port. For each new connection it starts a new backend process, which serves that one client until it disconnects. Before the first query runs, these steps happen:


    

  1. A TCP handshake, one network round trip.
    2. 

  2. A TLS handshake if the connection is encrypted. This adds more round trips and CPU for the key exchange.
    3. 

  3. Authentication. The default password method is SCRAM-SHA-256, which adds round trips and CPU on both sides on purpose.
    4. 

  4. The postmaster forks the backend. The backend starts with empty private caches of the system catalogs (table, column and index definitions). It fills them as the session touches tables, so the first queries on a new connection are slower than later ones.
    5. 



A connection that stays open also costs memory. Each backend has private memory for its catalog caches, which grow with the number of tables and partitions it has touched. Each sort or hash in a running query can use up to work_mem (default 4 MB), and one query can have several of them at once (§19.4). There is no fixed number per backend. It depends on what the session did.



Why max_connections stays small


max_connections is typically 100 by default and can only change at server start. Three of those slots are reserved for superusers (superuser_reserved_connections). When the limit is reached, a new client gets FATAL: sorry, too many clients already. It is tempting to set it to 5,000. Here is what grows with it:


    

  • Shared memory. PostgreSQL sizes some shared structures directly from max_connections (§19.3). The shared lock table, for example, has room for max_locks_per_transaction (default 64) objects per server process (§19.12).
    • 

  • Snapshots. Each snapshot (chapter 12) must find which transactions are running, which means looking at the other backends. PostgreSQL 14 made this much cheaper with many sessions, but the work still grows with the number of sessions.
    • 

  • CPU contention. Only as many queries as there are cores can run at one moment. More active backends mean more context switches, more fights over internal locks, and CPU caches that are cleared by the next process (wiki "Number Of Database Connections").
    • 

  • Memory for queries. 1,000 active backends each doing two sorts can ask for 1,000 × 2 × 4 MB = 8 GB at the default work_mem. A hash can use twice that, because hash_mem_multiplier is 2.0 by default.
    • 



The wiki page describes the result. As you add active connections, throughput rises until the hardware is busy, then it stops rising, and past a "knee" it falls. The same 10,000 transactions often finish sooner when they run 10 or 20 at a time than 500 at a time. A transaction that waits a little in a queue for a free connection can finish sooner than one that starts at once on a crowded server.



Why the app's own pool is not enough


Most drivers and frameworks have a pool inside the app process (HikariCP, the pool in node-postgres, SQLAlchemy's pool). That removes the handshake from each request. It does not limit the total. Each app instance has its own pool and does not know about the others.



 

  
app pod 1
app pod 2
app pod 3
... pod 30

  
pool: 20in pod 1
pool: 20in pod 2
pool: 20in pod 3
pool: 20in pod 30

  
↓ 30 × 20 = 600 connections

  
600 backendsmax_connections is 100, so most are refused, or the limit is raised and the server slows down

  
PgBounceraccepts 600 client connections, opens at most 20 server connections

  
↓ 20 server connections

  
PostgreSQLpostmaster + 20 backends, about the number of cores

 

 

  
The shop runs 30 app instances behind a load balancer (example numbers). An autoscaler can add more at peak time.

  
Each instance has its own pool of 20 connections. Each pool is sized for its own peak, and it keeps idle connections open to be ready.

  
Together they want 600 connections. At any moment most of them are idle, but each one is still a backend process. Scale to 60 instances and the number doubles.

  
A pooler in front of the database takes all client connections. It is one place that knows the total and sets the limit.

  
The pooler hands a small set of server connections to clients in turn. The server sees 20 backends, whatever the number of app instances.

 

 





PgBouncer and its pool modes


PgBouncer is a small proxy that speaks the PostgreSQL protocol. The app connects to PgBouncer as if it were the server. PgBouncer needs about 2 kB per client connection by default, so it can hold thousands of idle clients cheaply. For each user and database pair it keeps a pool of server connections, default_pool_size (default 20). max_client_conn (default 100) limits the client side. The question is when a server connection goes back to the pool. That is the pool_mode:




ModeServer connection returns to the poolWhat breaks


session (default)when the client disconnectsNothing. All PostgreSQL features work. But a long-lived client holds a backend all the time, so it saves only the handshake, not backends.


transactionwhen the transaction endsAny state that lives in the session, because the next transaction can run on a different backend. SET and RESET, LISTEN, WITH HOLD cursors, SQL-level PREPARE and DEALLOCATE, temp tables with PRESERVE ROWS or DELETE ROWS, LOAD, and session-level advisory locks.


statementwhen each statement endsEverything that breaks in transaction mode, plus transactions with more than one statement, which are refused.




Transaction mode is the usual choice, because it is the one that lets 600 clients share 20 backends. To use it, change the app where it relies on session state:


    

  • Use SET LOCAL inside the transaction, or set values per role or database with ALTER ROLE shop_app SET statement_timeout = '5s'.
    • 

  • Use pg_advisory_xact_lock(), which is released at commit, instead of pg_advisory_lock().
    • 

  • Give LISTEN its own connection, direct or through a session-mode pool.
    • 

  • Use temp tables with ON COMMIT DROP, which work.
    • 



Prepared statements need care. SQL-level PREPARE is tied to one backend and breaks. Protocol-level named prepared statements, which most drivers use, are supported in transaction mode since PgBouncer 1.21 (2023). PgBouncer tracks each statement a client prepares and prepares it again on whichever server connection the client gets. This is controlled by max_prepared_statements. The current docs give its default as 200, and 0 turns it off. On older PgBouncer versions, turn off server-side prepared statements in the driver.



How big should the pool be?


The number of connections that do work at the same time should be near what the server can run at once, not near the number of clients. The PostgreSQL wiki gives a starting formula: (core_count × 2) + effective_spindle_count. Count physical cores, not hyperthreads. The spindle count is 0 when the active data is in memory. The wiki says itself that the formula is old and was not checked on SSDs. Treat it as a start point, then measure and adjust. For an 8-core server with data in memory that gives about 16. Set max_connections a little higher than the sum of the pools, so that an admin can still connect.


Little's law gives the other side. Connections in use = transactions per second × seconds each transaction holds a connection. At 2,000 transactions per second (example) that hold a connection for 5 ms each, 10 connections are busy on average. If the app holds the transaction open while it calls a payment API for 300 ms, the same load needs 600. Short transactions matter more than the pool size.




 
Lab: pool simulator

 
Each client runs transactions in a loop. It waits some time outside a transaction, then runs a transaction of several queries, with some app work between them. The server has a fixed number of cores. The simulation runs 5 seconds of time. Set a mode and watch the wait for a connection, the throughput and the number of backends. Then turn on the stuck client with a pool size of 1 or 2. The cost of an extra active backend (each one past the core count slows the others by 2%) is an example value, not a measured one.

 

  
  
  
  
 

 

  
  
  
  
 

 

  
  
  
 

 

 

 




What the lab shows. With no pooler and 200 clients, half are refused by max_connections, and the other half compete for 8 cores. In transaction mode, throughput holds with a small pool, and the wait moves to the pooler's queue, where it is cheap. In session mode, clients past the pool size never get a connection. Raise the app work inside the transaction and the same pool starves, because each connection is held while the app does nothing with it.



Timeouts that protect the server


All of these are 0 (off) by default. The docs advise against setting statement_timeout, lock_timeout and transaction_timeout in postgresql.conf, because that affects every session, including admin work. Set them per role or database, or per transaction.




SettingWhat it endsWhy


statement_timeouta statement that runs longer than the limitA bad query or a missing index cannot hold a backend and a pool slot for minutes.


lock_timeouta statement that waits longer than the limit for one lockA schema change that waits behind a long transaction blocks every query behind it (chapter 28). With SET lock_timeout = '2s' it gives up and you retry.


idle_in_transaction_session_timeoutthe whole session, if it sits inside an open transaction without sending a querySee below.


transaction_timeouta session whose transaction runs longer than the limit (since PostgreSQL 17)An upper limit on the whole transaction, busy or idle.


idle_session_timeouta session that is idle outside a transactionRarely needed. An idle session costs little. The docs warn against it behind a pooler, which may not expect the server to close its connections.




A session that is idle in transaction ran BEGIN and maybe some statements, and now waits for the app. Common causes are an app that does a slow HTTP call inside the transaction, or an exception path that forgets to roll back. While it waits it does three kinds of damage. It keeps all its locks, so rows it updated stay locked for other writers. Its snapshot holds back the xmin horizon, so VACUUM cannot remove dead tuples anywhere in the database that are newer than its start (chapter 13), and tables bloat. And behind a transaction-mode pooler it holds one of the few server connections. Find such sessions with:


SELECT pid, usename, now() - xact_start AS in_tx_for, left(query, 60) AS last_query
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE state = 'idle in transaction'
ORDER BY xact_start;


Then set idle_in_transaction_session_timeout for the app role, for example ALTER ROLE shop_app SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '30s'. The server ends such a session with FATAL: terminating connection due to idle-in-transaction timeout. The app sees an error, the locks go away, and VACUUM can move on.








30. Replication, backup and failover


Covers: streaming replication, hot standby, synchronous replication and the synchronous_commit levels, replication lag and read-your-writes, query conflicts, replication slots, logical replication, failover and split brain, pg_rewind, pg_dump, base backups, WAL archiving and point-in-time recovery, RPO and RTO. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs chapter 26 "High Availability, Load Balancing, and Replication" (§26.2 Log-Shipping Standby Servers, §26.2.5 Streaming Replication, §26.2.6 Replication Slots, §26.2.8 Synchronous Replication, §26.3 Failover, §26.4 Hot Standby), chapter 25 "Backup and Restore" (§25.1 SQL Dump, §25.3 Continuous Archiving and PITR), chapter 29 "Logical Replication" (§29.8 Restrictions), §19.5 and §19.6 settings (synchronous_commit, archive_timeout, max_slot_wal_keep_size, idle_replication_slot_timeout, max_standby_streaming_delay, hot_standby_feedback, recovery_min_apply_delay), pg_rewind, initdb, §18.6 "Upgrading a PostgreSQL Cluster". Checked 2026-09-30.


Replication keeps a copy of the database on other servers. In PostgreSQL's built-in physical replication, the primary sends its WAL (chapter 15) to one or more standby servers, and each standby replays it. A backup is a copy you can restore to return to an earlier state. Failover makes a standby the new primary when the primary fails.



Streaming replication


A standby is a server that is always in recovery. Crash recovery replays WAL once and stops. A standby replays WAL and never stops, because more keeps coming. You start a standby from a copy of the primary's data directory (a base backup, made with pg_basebackup) and set primary_conninfo to point at the primary. The standby then connects to the primary. A walsender process on the primary streams WAL records as they are written, without waiting for a segment file to fill. A walreceiver process on the standby writes them to its own pg_wal, and the startup process replays them (§26.2.5).


This copy is physical. The standby gets the same bytes in the same pages. So it copies the whole cluster (all databases), it must run the same major version, and you cannot write to it. With hot_standby = on (the default) you can run read-only queries on it. This is the common "read replica".



 

  
Primarytakes all writes, writes WAL to pg_wal

  
↓ walsender
WAL streamrecords as they are written, over one TCP connection per standby
↓ walreceiver

  

   
Standby Areplays WAL, serves read-only queries

   
Standby Bsynchronous. Commits wait for its reply

  

  
WAL archivearchive_command copies each finished segment
Base backupspg_basebackup, for example each night

 

 

  
The primary writes WAL for each change, as in chapter 15. Replication adds no second log. It reuses the WAL.

  
For each standby, a walsender process reads the WAL and streams it. The standby's walreceiver writes it to disk and reports back how far it has written, flushed and replayed.

  
Standby A replays the WAL and answers read-only queries. By default replication is asynchronous. The primary does not wait for any standby before it answers COMMIT.

  
If Standby B is named in synchronous_standby_names, each commit on the primary waits for B's reply. Section "Synchronous replication" below shows what the reply means.

  
Separate from replication, finished WAL segments are copied to an archive, and base backups are taken from time to time. Together they allow a restore to any point in time. A standby that falls too far behind can also fetch old WAL from the archive.

 

 





Synchronous replication


With asynchronous replication, the primary answers COMMIT when the WAL is on its own disk. If the primary is destroyed a moment later, the last commits may never have reached a standby. The loss is about the replication lag at that moment (§26.2.8). Synchronous replication closes that gap. You name standbys in synchronous_standby_names, and then synchronous_commit says what each commit waits for:




synchronous_commitCOMMIT returns afterA commit the app was told about is lost if


offnothing, not even the local flushthe primary crashes in the next 3 × wal_writer_delay (chapter 15)


locallocal WAL flush onlythe primary is lost before the standby got the WAL


remote_writelocal flush, and the standby has written the WAL to its operating system (not flushed)the primary is lost and the standby's OS crashes at the same time


on (default)local flush, and the standby has flushed the WAL to durable storageboth the primary and the standby lose their storage


remote_applyas on, and the standby has replayed it, so queries on the standby can see itas on. It also gives read-your-writes on that standby.




If synchronous_standby_names is empty, local, remote_write, remote_apply and on all mean "local flush". Each commit waits at least one network round trip to the standby. During the wait the transaction keeps its locks, so slow standbys also mean more lock contention (§26.2.8.3). Only commits that wrote something wait. Read-only transactions and rollbacks do not. You can set the level per transaction, for example SET LOCAL synchronous_commit = remote_apply for payments and local for page-view events.


The availability cost is the big one. If the only synchronous standby is down, every writing commit on the primary waits, possibly forever (§26.2.8.4). The primary is up, but writes hang. The fix is more candidates. synchronous_standby_names = 'FIRST 1 (s1, s2)' uses s1, and s2 takes over if s1 fails. 'ANY 1 (s1, s2, s3)' is a quorum. A commit waits for a reply from any one of the three. If you really cannot keep enough synchronous standbys, you must lower the number in the setting (or clear it) and reload, which gives up the guarantee for that time.


The guarantee is about what the app is told, not about what exists. The commit record is already in the primary's WAL before the wait starts. If the primary crashes during the wait, the transaction shows as committed when the primary comes back, even though the app never got an answer. The only promise is that the app never gets "COMMIT" before the synchronous standbys have the WAL.



Replication lag and read-your-writes


An asynchronous standby is behind the primary. The docs say the delay is typically under one second when the standby keeps up. Under load it can be much more. Now think of this flow in the shop. The customer places an order. The app writes it on the primary, gets COMMIT, and redirects to "My orders". That page reads from a standby, and the order is not there yet. The customer thinks the order failed and orders again.


The usual fixes are these:


    

  • Read from the primary for a short time after this user wrote something, or for all reads that follow a write in the same request.
    • 

  • Track the LSN. After the write, the app reads pg_current_wal_lsn() on the primary and keeps it (for example in the session). Before it reads from a standby, it checks pg_last_wal_replay_lsn() there. If the standby is behind that LSN, it reads from the primary or waits a little.
    • 

  • Commit that transaction with synchronous_commit = remote_apply. The commit then returns only when the synchronous standbys can show the change. This costs latency on every such commit and only covers the synchronous standbys.
    • 



Watch lag on the primary with pg_stat_replication. It has sent_lsn, write_lsn, flush_lsn and replay_lsn per standby, and the times write_lag, flush_lag and replay_lag.


SELECT application_name, state, sync_state,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), replay_lsn)) AS replay_behind,
       replay_lag
FROM pg_stat_replication;




 
Lab: replication, stale reads and failover

 
Time runs only when you press a button. Each "Commit" inserts one order on the primary. Choose the mode and the lag, commit a few orders, read from the standby, then kill the primary and promote the standby. The lag values are examples. A real standby's lag changes with load.

 

  
  
  
  
 

 

  
  
  
  
  
  
  
  
 

 

 

 




Things to try. In async mode with a large lag, commit three orders fast, kill the primary and promote. The app was told all three are done, and some are gone. In on mode, nothing the app was told about is lost, but "Commit, then read" still misses the order on the standby. Only remote_apply fixes that. Then tick "Standby is down" in a sync mode and commit. The commit never returns.



Query conflicts on a hot standby


The standby must apply every WAL record, and it cannot ask the primary to wait. Some records conflict with queries that are running on the standby (§26.4.2). The common case is VACUUM on the primary. It removes dead tuples that no transaction on the primary needs any more. But a long report on the standby can still need them. Other cases are ACCESS EXCLUSIVE locks from DDL on the primary, and DROP TABLE or DROP DATABASE.


The standby then pauses replay for up to max_standby_streaming_delay (default 30 s). After that it cancels the query with ERROR: canceling statement due to conflict with recovery. The 30 s is a total budget for replay to fall behind, not a limit per query. So the choice is between cancelled queries on the standby and a standby that falls behind. With hot_standby_feedback = on (default off), the standby tells the primary the oldest snapshot it still needs, and VACUUM on the primary keeps those tuples. The cost moves to the primary as bloat (chapter 13). A standby that runs 2-hour analytic queries with feedback on holds back VACUUM on the busy orders table for 2 hours.



Replication slots


The primary recycles old WAL after each checkpoint (chapter 15). If a standby is offline for a while, the WAL it needs may be gone, and it must be rebuilt from a new base backup. A replication slot prevents this. It is a named record on the primary of how far one consumer has got. The primary keeps all WAL that the slot still needs, and with hot_standby_feedback it also keeps the rows the consumer's queries need, even while it is disconnected (§26.2.6).


SELECT pg_create_physical_replication_slot('standby_a');   -- on the primary
-- on the standby: primary_slot_name = 'standby_a'

SELECT slot_name, slot_type, active,
       pg_size_pretty(pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), restart_lsn)) AS wal_kept
FROM pg_replication_slots;


The same guarantee is the hazard. A slot whose consumer is gone, for example a deleted standby or a stopped change-data tool, keeps WAL without limit. pg_wal grows until the disk is full, and then the primary stops. max_slot_wal_keep_size (default -1, no limit) puts a cap on it. Past the cap the slot loses its WAL and its consumer must be rebuilt, but the primary lives. PostgreSQL 18 adds idle_replication_slot_timeout (default 0, off), which invalidates slots that were inactive for longer than the limit. Alert on the wal_kept column above.



Logical replication


Logical replication sends row changes, not pages. With wal_level = logical, the primary decodes its WAL into "insert this row", "update the row with this key", "delete the row with this key". It sends them to subscribers, which apply them with normal SQL-level operations. The row is found by its replica identity, usually the primary key (§29.1).


-- on the publisher
CREATE PUBLICATION shop_pub FOR TABLE orders, order_items;
-- on the subscriber (the tables must already exist there)
CREATE SUBSCRIPTION shop_sub
  CONNECTION 'host=192.0.2.10 dbname=acmeshop user=repl'
  PUBLICATION shop_pub;


The subscription first copies the current data from a snapshot, then streams the changes in commit order. The subscriber is a normal server. It can have other tables and other indexes and can take writes. That makes logical replication useful for things physical replication cannot do:


    

  • Major version upgrades with little downtime. Replicate from a PostgreSQL 17 server to an 18 server, then switch the app over (§18.6).
    • 

  • Moving or sharing some tables, for example only orders to a reporting database.
    • 

  • Change data capture, where an outside tool reads the decoded changes through a logical slot and sends them to a queue or a search index.
    • 



Logical replication in PostgreSQL 18 has limits (§29.8). The schema and DDL are not replicated, so you run ALTER TABLE on both sides yourself. Sequence values are not replicated, so before a switchover you must set each sequence on the new side past the highest used value. Large objects are not replicated. Views, materialized views and foreign tables cannot be published. If someone writes to the same rows on the subscriber, conflicts stop the apply until you fix them.



Failover


PostgreSQL gives you the parts for failover but not the decision. The docs say directly that it "does not provide the system software required to identify a failure on the primary and notify the standby" (§26.3). To make a standby the new primary you promote it with pg_ctl promote or SELECT pg_promote(). It replays the WAL it already has, stops recovery, starts a new timeline (a new branch of WAL history) and accepts writes. The app must then connect to the new address, which is often done by moving a virtual IP or a DNS name.


The dangerous case is split brain. The old primary was not dead, only cut off from the network. It comes back and still thinks it is the primary. Some clients write to one server and some to the other, and the two histories cannot be merged. So the old primary must be stopped for sure before or during promotion. This is called fencing, or STONITH ("shoot the other node in the head"). Tools such as Patroni automate the detection, the fencing and the promotion. With asynchronous replication, every failover can lose the commits that had not reached the standby.


After a failover the old primary has WAL that the new primary never saw. It cannot simply follow the new primary. pg_rewind finds the point where the timelines split and copies back only the pages that changed since then, which is much faster than a new base backup. It needs wal_log_hints = on or data checksums. Since PostgreSQL 18, initdb turns data checksums on by default. The changes that only the old primary had are thrown away.



Backup and point-in-time recovery


Replication is not a backup. A standby copies mistakes as fast as it copies good data. DROP TABLE orders or a DELETE without a WHERE reaches every standby within the lag. A delayed standby (recovery_min_apply_delay = '1h') gives you a window to react, but it is still not a backup.




pg_dump (logical)pg_basebackup + WAL archive (physical)


What it copiesSQL or an archive of one database, with schema and rowsthe whole cluster's files, plus every WAL segment after them


Consistencyone snapshot taken when the dump starts. It does not block normal reads and writes, only commands that need an exclusive lock.the copy is not consistent by itself. Replaying WAL from the backup's start makes it consistent.


Restore tothe moment the dump started, onlyany moment after the base backup that the archive covers


Across versionsyes, the usual way to move between major versions or platformsno, same major version


Speed on a large databaseslow to restore, because all indexes are built againfile copy plus WAL replay




For point-in-time recovery (PITR), you turn on archiving (archive_mode = on and an archive_command or archive_library that copies each finished segment to safe storage), and you take base backups regularly. To recover to 14:02, just before someone ran the bad DELETE at 14:03, you restore the latest base backup from before that time, set restore_command and recovery_target_time = '2026-09-30 14:02:00+00', create recovery.signal and start the server. It replays archived WAL up to that time and stops (§25.3). Only finished segments are archived. On a quiet server a segment can take a long time to fill, so archive_timeout forces a switch to a new segment after a set time. Test restores regularly. A backup that was never restored is only a hope.


Two numbers describe any plan. RPO (recovery point objective) is how much recent data you can accept to lose, measured in time. RTO (recovery time objective) is how long you can accept to be down. An asynchronous standby gives an RPO of about the replication lag and an RTO of the time to detect the failure and promote. Synchronous replication gives an RPO of zero for commits the app was told about, at the price of commit latency and the availability risk above. PITR from an archive gives an RPO of the WAL that was not archived yet and an RTO of the time to copy the base backup and replay the WAL, which for a large database can be hours.








31. Partitioning and scaling out


Covers: declarative partitioning (range, list, hash), the partition key, tuple routing, unique constraints on partitioned tables, partition pruning at plan time and at execution time, benefits and costs, when to partition, partition maintenance (DETACH CONCURRENTLY, default partition), partitionwise join and aggregate, the order of scaling steps, sharding, hard limits. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs §5.12 "Table Partitioning" (§5.12.1 Overview, §5.12.2 Declarative Partitioning, §5.12.4 Partition Pruning, §5.12.6 Best Practices), CREATE TABLE (PARTITION OF, DEFAULT), ALTER TABLE (DETACH PARTITION ... CONCURRENTLY), §19.7 "Query Planning" (enable_partitionwise_join, enable_partitionwise_aggregate), Appendix K "PostgreSQL Limits", PostgreSQL 12 and 17 release notes. Checked 2026-09-30.


Partitioning splits one large table into smaller tables, called partitions, by the value of one or more columns, called the partition key. Queries still use the one table name. PostgreSQL sends each row to the right partition and skips partitions that cannot hold the rows a query asks for. Scaling out means to spread data or load over more than one server.



Declarative partitioning


In the example shop, orders grows by about 83,000 rows a month (example number). Most queries ask for recent orders, and the shop keeps 3 years. This is the classic case for range partitioning by time:


CREATE TABLE orders (
  id           bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY,
  customer_id  bigint NOT NULL REFERENCES customers(id),
  status       text NOT NULL,
  total        numeric(12,2) NOT NULL,
  created_at   timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),
  PRIMARY KEY (id, created_at)            -- must include the partition key
) PARTITION BY RANGE (created_at);

CREATE TABLE orders_2026_09 PARTITION OF orders
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-09-01') TO ('2026-10-01');
CREATE TABLE orders_2026_10 PARTITION OF orders
  FOR VALUES FROM ('2026-10-01') TO ('2026-11-01');
CREATE TABLE orders_default PARTITION OF orders DEFAULT;

CREATE INDEX ON orders (customer_id);     -- creates one index per partition


Some facts about this (§5.12.2):


    

  • The partitioned table orders has no storage. Each partition is a normal table with its own heap and its own indexes. An index created on the parent is created on every partition, now and later.
    • 

  • A range includes its lower bound and excludes its upper bound. So 2026-10-01 00:00 goes to orders_2026_10, not orders_2026_09.
    • 

  • An INSERT into orders is routed to the partition whose bounds hold the key. If no partition fits and there is no default partition, the insert fails. An UPDATE that changes created_at to another month moves the row to the other partition.
    • 

  • There are three methods. Range (time, id ranges), list (for example PARTITION BY LIST (country) with FOR VALUES IN ('DE', 'AT')), and hash (for example PARTITION BY HASH (customer_id) with FOR VALUES WITH (MODULUS 8, REMAINDER 0)). Hash spreads rows evenly when there is no natural range.
    • 

  • You cannot turn an existing table into a partitioned table. You create a new one and move the data, or attach the old table as one partition.
    • 

  • Identity columns on partitioned tables work since PostgreSQL 17. Foreign keys that reference a partitioned table work since PostgreSQL 12.
    • 



The primary key is (id, created_at), not (id). A unique constraint on a partitioned table must include all partition key columns. The reason is that there is no global index. Each partition has its own B-tree, and each B-tree can only check uniqueness inside its own partition. If the key is part of the constraint, two equal values must be in the same partition, so the local check is enough (§5.12.2.3). This spreads to other tables. order_items must now store order_created_at too, so that its foreign key can reference orders (id, created_at).




 

  
AppINSERT INTO orders ... / SELECT ... FROM orders WHERE created_at >= ...

  
↓

  
orders (partitioned table)no storage, only the rules

  
orders_2026_07Jul
orders_2026_08Aug
orders_2026_09Sep, takes all new rows

  
orders_2026_07_customer_id_idx
orders_2026_08_customer_id_idx
orders_2026_09_customer_id_idx

  
orders_defaultrows that fit no other partition

  
orders_2026_10created before it is needed
orders_2023_09detached, dumped, dropped

 

 

  
The app only knows orders. The partitioned table holds the partition key and the bounds of each partition, not rows.

  
One partition per month. New orders all go to the current month, so its pages are the hot ones in shared buffers. Old months are rarely read.

  
Each partition has its own indexes. They are small, so the upper levels of each B-tree stay in memory. The docs say partitioning in effect replaces the upper levels of the index (§5.12.1).

  
A default partition catches rows that fit no bounds, for example an order with a wrong date far in the future. Without it such an insert fails. It has costs, see "Maintenance" below.

  
A job creates next month's partition ahead of time and removes the month that is older than 3 years. Removing a month is a metadata change, not a big DELETE.

 

 





Partition pruning


Partition pruning is how PostgreSQL skips partitions. The planner compares the WHERE clause with the bounds of each partition. If it can prove a partition holds no matching rows, that partition is not in the plan (§5.12.4). Pruning uses only the partition bounds, not indexes. It can happen at two times:


    

  • At plan time, when the values are constants: created_at >= '2026-09-01' AND created_at < '2026-10-01'. Pruned partitions do not appear in EXPLAIN at all.
    • 

  • At execution time, when the value is only known when the query runs. Examples are parameters of a prepared statement with a generic plan, now(), a value from a subquery, and the parameter of a nested loop join. Partitions removed when the executor starts are shown as Subplans Removed: N. Partitions skipped during the run show as (never executed) in EXPLAIN ANALYZE. Partitions removed at executor start are still locked.
    • 



Pruning needs a condition on the partition key column itself. WHERE customer_id = 42 has no condition on created_at, so every partition is searched, one index lookup per partition. WHERE date_trunc('month', created_at) = '2026-03-01' puts a function around the key, so the planner cannot compare it with the bounds. Write the condition as a range on the bare column instead. The same is true for WHERE id = 42. The primary key index is per partition, so a lookup by id alone probes every partition's index. With 36 monthly partitions that is 36 index lookups instead of one.




 
Lab: which partitions does this query read?

 
orders has one partition per month of 2026 and a default partition. "Now" is 2026-09-30 12:00 UTC. Change the WHERE clause and read the plan. The plan is shown in the shape of EXPLAIN (COSTS OFF). Row counts per partition are example numbers.

 

  
 

 

  
  
  
  
 

 

 

 

 

 





What partitioning gives and what it costs




GivesCosts


Old data leaves by DROP TABLE or DETACH PARTITION of one partition. No big DELETE, no dead tuples, no VACUUM afterwards (§5.12.1).Unique constraints and primary keys must include the partition key. There is no global uniqueness on id alone.


Smaller indexes per partition. The hot partition's indexes fit in memory.A query without the partition key reads every partition.


A query for one month can use a Seq Scan of that partition instead of an index scan over the whole table.Planning takes longer and uses more memory when many partitions remain after pruning. The docs say a few thousand partitions are handled "fairly well" when pruning leaves only a few (§5.12.6).


VACUUM and ANALYZE work per partition. Old, unchanged partitions need little work. Very old months can go to cheaper storage.Each session that touches a partition loads its metadata into its own memory. Many sessions times many partitions uses a lot of memory.




When should you partition? The docs give a rule of thumb. Partition when the table would be larger than the server's physical memory (§5.12.1). A second strong reason is a retention rule, such as "delete orders older than 3 years", that would otherwise need huge DELETEs. Partitioning is not a way to make a lookup by an indexed column faster. A B-tree lookup on 1,000,000 or 100,000,000 rows costs only one or two more page reads (chapter 16). If the table is 10 GB on a server with 64 GB of RAM and old data is never removed, a good index is enough. Choose the partition key from the column that most queries filter on and that data is removed by. Do not make too many partitions. One partition per customer can become 100,000 partitions if the business grows. Hash partitioning with a fixed number is then the better choice (§5.12.6).



Maintenance


Create partitions ahead of time, from a scheduled job. If next month's partition is missing and there is no default partition, all inserts fail at midnight on the 1st.


To remove a month, DROP TABLE orders_2023_09 is instant, but it needs an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock on the parent orders. It must wait for running queries on orders, and new queries wait behind it (chapter 28). Use SET lock_timeout and retry. A gentler way is this:


ALTER TABLE orders DETACH PARTITION orders_2023_09 CONCURRENTLY;
-- now a normal table: dump it to cold storage, then
DROP TABLE orders_2023_09;


DETACH ... CONCURRENTLY takes only a SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE lock on the parent. It uses two transactions and waits for every transaction that uses the table in between. It cannot run inside a transaction block, and it is not allowed if the table has a default partition. If it is interrupted, ALTER TABLE ... DETACH PARTITION ... FINALIZE completes it.


The default partition has a second cost. When you add a new partition, PostgreSQL must scan the default partition to check that it holds no rows for the new range. It does this while it holds an ACCESS EXCLUSIVE lock on the default partition. Keep the default partition empty, and treat any row in it as an alarm.


To add a partition with less locking, create a normal table, load and check it, add a CHECK constraint that matches the bounds, and then run ALTER TABLE orders ATTACH PARTITION ... FOR VALUES .... ATTACH needs only SHARE UPDATE EXCLUSIVE on the parent, and the matching CHECK lets it skip the scan of the new table. CREATE INDEX on a partitioned table cannot use CONCURRENTLY. To index a large partitioned table without a long lock, run CREATE INDEX ... ON ONLY orders (it stays invalid), then CREATE INDEX CONCURRENTLY on each partition, then ALTER INDEX ... ATTACH PARTITION for each. The parent index becomes valid when all partitions are attached.


Partitionwise join joins matching partitions of two tables one pair at a time, when both tables are partitioned the same way and the join includes the partition key. Partitionwise aggregate groups each partition on its own. Both are off by default (enable_partitionwise_join, enable_partitionwise_aggregate). They make planning slower, and each partition's join or aggregate can use its own work_mem, so memory use can grow with the number of partitions.



Scaling beyond one server


Before you add servers, use up the cheaper steps, roughly in this order:


    

  1. Fix queries and indexes (chapters 19 and 26). One missing index can cost more than a second server gives.
    2. 

  2. Scale up. A bigger machine, more RAM so the working set stays in memory, faster disks for the WAL. This changes nothing in the app.
    3. 

  3. Connection pooling (chapter 29), so the server does not waste its CPU on thousands of backends.
    4. 

  4. Read standbys (chapter 30) for read-heavy load. The app must handle replication lag.
    5. 

  5. Caching of hot, rarely changed results in the app or a cache server. The cost is stale data and the work to invalidate it.
    6. 

  6. Partitioning on the same server, for very large tables and data retention.
    7. 

  7. Sharding. Several primaries, each with part of the data.
    8. 



Sharding splits rows over servers by a shard key, for example customer_id. The app can do it itself, with a map from customer to server. Or an extension can do it, such as Citus, which distributes tables over several PostgreSQL nodes and routes queries. Every step up to here keeps one primary. Sharding gives that up, and that has costs:


    

  • Cross-shard queries. A join or report over customers on different shards must collect data from many servers. Pick a shard key so that most queries touch one shard. For a shop, customer_id keeps a customer's orders and items together.
    • 

  • Cross-shard transactions. A transaction on one server is atomic. A change on two servers needs two-phase commit or a design that accepts partial states. It is no longer simple ACID.
    • 

  • Global rules. A unique email across all shards, a sequence of order numbers, a foreign key to a row on another shard. None of these is enforced by one server any more.
    • 

  • Operations. Backups, upgrades, schema changes and failover now happen N times, and moving data between shards when one grows is its own project.
    • 




Hard limits


These are the hard limits from the docs (Appendix K). Practical limits, such as disk space and performance, usually come first.




ItemUpper limitComment


database sizeunlimited


number of databases4,294,950,911


relations per database1,431,650,303


relation (table) size32 TBwith the default page size of 8,192 bytes. A partitioned table has no size of its own, so each partition can grow to this limit.


rows per tableas many tuples as fit on 4,294,967,295 pages


columns per table1,600fewer if the tuple does not fit on one page


columns in a result set1,664


field size1 GB


columns per index32can be increased by recompiling


partition keys32can be increased by recompiling


identifier length63 bytescan be increased by recompiling


query parameters65,535




One more limit matters for very large tables. Each table can hold at most 232 values stored out of line in TOAST (chapter 11), because each one needs a 32-bit OID. The docs name partitioning as a possible workaround, because each partition has its own TOAST table.









32. Monitoring and security


Covers: pg_stat_activity, cancel and terminate, timeouts, pg_stat_statements, slow query logs and auto_explain, table and index statistics, pg_locks and pg_blocking_pids, pg_stat_database, pg_stat_io, alerts; SQL injection, roles and privileges, default privileges, the public schema change, row-level security, pg_hba.conf and authentication, OAuth, TLS and sslmode, SECURITY DEFINER and search_path. Sources: PostgreSQL 18 docs "The Cumulative Statistics System" (§27.2), "Routine Vacuuming" (§24.1), "System Administration Functions" (§9.28), "System Information Functions" (§9.27), pg_stat_statements (§F.32), auto_explain (§F.3), "Error Reporting and Logging" (§19.8), "Client Connection Defaults" (§19.11), "Connections and Authentication" (§19.3), "Privileges" (§5.8), "Row Security Policies" (§5.9), "Schemas" (§5.10), CREATE POLICY, CREATE FUNCTION, "Database Roles" (§21.1), "Role Attributes" (§21.2), "The pg_hba.conf File" (§20.1), "Password Authentication" (§20.5), "OAuth Authorization/Authentication" (§20.15), libpq "SSL Support" (§32.19), release notes for PostgreSQL 14, 15, 16, 17 and 18. Checked 2026-09-30.


Monitoring means reading the statistics that PostgreSQL collects about sessions, queries, tables and I/O, so that you find a problem before the users do. Security means control of who can connect, what each role can read and change, which rows it can see, and that neither the network nor the SQL text can be used against you.



Monitoring: what to look at and why


The cumulative statistics system keeps counters in shared memory. Each backend sends its counts just before it goes idle, at most once per second. So the numbers lag a little behind, and a query that still runs is not in the totals. The counters grow from the last reset. To get a rate, take two samples and subtract. The current-query columns of pg_stat_activity are always up to date.



pg_stat_activity: what runs now


One row per server process. The important columns are state, wait_event_type and wait_event (what the backend waits for, for example Lock / transactionid or IO / DataFileRead), xact_start, query_start, backend_xmin and query.


-- the longest open transactions, and what they did last
SELECT pid, usename, state, wait_event_type, wait_event,
       now() - xact_start AS xact_age, left(query, 60) AS last_query
FROM pg_stat_activity
WHERE xact_start IS NOT NULL
ORDER BY xact_start
LIMIT 10;


state is active (runs a statement), idle (waits for the client, no transaction), idle in transaction (in a transaction, waits for the client) or idle in transaction (aborted). idle in transaction is the one to watch. The application opened a transaction and did not commit, often because it makes an HTTP call inside the transaction or has a bug in the error path. The session holds its locks, and its snapshot stops VACUUM from removing dead tuples that are newer than it (chapter 13). One forgotten session can make every table grow.


Two functions stop a backend. pg_cancel_backend(pid) sends SIGINT and cancels the current query. The session stays, and a transaction block goes to the aborted state. pg_terminate_backend(pid) sends SIGTERM and ends the session, which rolls back its transaction and releases its locks. Cancel first. Terminate when the session is idle in transaction (there is no query to cancel) or does not react. Set limits so that you rarely need to do either by hand: statement_timeout, idle_in_transaction_session_timeout, and transaction_timeout (since PostgreSQL 17). All three are off (0) by default. Set them for the application role, for example ALTER ROLE app SET idle_in_transaction_session_timeout = '60s'.



pg_stat_statements: which queries cost the most


An extension in contrib. Add it to shared_preload_libraries (needs a restart), then run CREATE EXTENSION pg_stat_statements in the database. It groups statements by a hash of their parse tree after parse analysis, the queryid. Constants are replaced by $1, $2 in the query text, so WHERE id = 7 and WHERE id = 8 are one row. It keeps up to pg_stat_statements.max statements (default 5000) and drops the least-executed ones when full.


SELECT queryid, calls, round(total_exec_time) AS total_ms,
       round(mean_exec_time::numeric, 2) AS mean_ms, rows,
       shared_blks_hit, shared_blks_read, temp_blks_written,
       left(query, 70) AS query
FROM pg_stat_statements
ORDER BY total_exec_time DESC
LIMIT 10;


Sort by total time to find what uses the server most. A 2 ms query that runs 5 million times a day costs more than a 4 second report that runs once. Sort by mean time to find what makes single users wait. Sort by calls to find N+1 patterns (one query per row from an application loop). shared_blks_read shows pages not found in shared buffers. temp_blks_written shows sorts and hashes that did not fit in work_mem (chapter 24). Reset with pg_stat_statements_reset() after a deploy to measure the new code.



 
Lab: read a pg_stat_statements sample

 
Example numbers from one day of the shop. Sort by each column and see how the first row changes. The share column is each query's part of the total time of all rows.

 

 

 





Logs: slow statements and their plans


log_min_duration_statement = '250ms' logs each statement that runs 250 ms or longer, with its duration (the default -1 turns it off). The log shows the real values, which pg_stat_statements hides. The auto_explain module also logs the plan. Load it, set auto_explain.log_min_duration (default -1, off), and optionally auto_explain.log_analyze = on. Be careful with log_analyze. It times every plan node of every statement, logged or not, and the docs warn that this can hurt performance badly. Turn auto_explain.log_timing off to reduce the cost. log_lock_waits = on logs each wait for a lock that lasts longer than deadlock_timeout (1 s by default).



Tables, indexes and locks


-- tables read mostly by Seq Scan, and dead tuples waiting for VACUUM
SELECT relname, seq_scan, idx_scan, n_live_tup, n_dead_tup, last_autovacuum, last_autoanalyze
FROM pg_stat_user_tables ORDER BY n_dead_tup DESC LIMIT 10;

-- indexes never used since the last reset (keep the ones behind constraints)
SELECT s.relname, s.indexrelname, pg_size_pretty(pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid)) AS size
FROM pg_stat_user_indexes s JOIN pg_index i ON i.indexrelid = s.indexrelid
WHERE s.idx_scan = 0 AND NOT i.indisunique
ORDER BY pg_relation_size(s.indexrelid) DESC;

-- who blocks whom
SELECT pid, pg_blocking_pids(pid) AS blocked_by, wait_event_type, left(query, 60)
FROM pg_stat_activity WHERE cardinality(pg_blocking_pids(pid)) > 0;


A large table with a high seq_scan and a low idx_scan points to a missing index (chapter 19). A high n_dead_tup and an old last_autovacuum point to autovacuum that cannot keep up or is blocked by an old snapshot (chapter 13). Index usage counters are per server, so check the standbys before you drop an index that the read replicas use. pg_blocking_pids() reads the lock table (pg_locks) and returns the processes that hold, or wait in front for, a conflicting lock (chapter 28). It needs short exclusive access to the lock manager, so do not call it many times per second.


pg_stat_database has one row per database: xact_commit, xact_rollback, deadlocks, temp_files and temp_bytes (all temporary files, from any sort or hash), blks_read and blks_hit. pg_stat_io (since PostgreSQL 16) splits I/O by backend type, object and context: reads, writes, extends, fsyncs, and in PostgreSQL 18 also bytes and WAL I/O. It shows, for example, that ordinary backends write dirty pages themselves because the background writer does not keep up (chapter 14). For replication, pg_stat_replication on the primary shows write_lag, flush_lag and replay_lag per standby (chapter 30).



The numbers worth an alert




SignalWhereWhy it matters


Connections near max_connections (default typically 100)count(*) FROM pg_stat_activityAt the limit new connections fail. Use a pooler (chapter 29).


Age of the oldest transaction, and idle in transaction sessionsnow() - min(xact_start), backend_xminHolds locks and stops VACUUM, so tables and indexes bloat.


XID age toward wraparoundSELECT datname, age(datfrozenxid) FROM pg_databaseAutovacuum forces a freeze at autovacuum_freeze_max_age (default 200 million). Warnings start 40 million XIDs before the wraparound point, and near it the server stops giving new XIDs (chapter 13). Alert far earlier.


Replication lagpg_stat_replication.replay_lagStale reads on standbys, data loss on failover, WAL kept on the primary's disk.


Disk spaceThe OS, pg_database_size()A full WAL disk stops the server.


Cache hit ratioblks_hit / (blks_hit + blks_read)A sudden drop means the working set no longer fits. But blks_read counts reads that the OS page cache served, so the ratio understates real hits. And a high ratio does not prove the queries are good. Watch the trend, not a fixed target.


Deadlocks, temp files, rollbackspg_stat_database (rates)Lock order bugs, too little work_mem, application errors.





Security: the layers a connection passes



 

  
Applicationconnects as role app to database acmeshop

  
↓ TLS handshake, checked by sslmode

  
Encrypted channel

  
↓ first matching line of pg_hba.conf

  
Authenticationscram-sha-256, cert, oauth, ...

  
↓ every statement

  
PrivilegesGRANT on schema, table, column, function

  
Row-level securitypolicies add conditions to each row

  
The SQL text itselfinjection bypasses the layers above

 

 

  
The application opens a connection with a role name, a database name and a secret.

  
With TLS, the client can check that it talks to the right server and nobody can read the traffic. Only sslmode=verify-full checks both.

  
The server picks the first line in pg_hba.conf that matches the connection type, database, user and client address. That line names the authentication method. If it fails, no later line is tried.

  
Each statement is checked against the role's privileges. The app role should have only the privileges it needs.

  
Row-level security policies then decide which rows the role may see and write, for example only the rows of the current tenant.

  
If the application builds SQL by joining strings, an attacker can change the query. It then runs with every privilege the app role has. The layers above do not see a problem.

 

 





SQL injection


The server parses the SQL text into a parse tree (chapter 10). What is a value and what is code is decided by the text alone. When the application joins user input into the text, the input can close the string literal and add its own code. The parse tree changes. The server cannot see the difference, because the text is all it gets.


// vulnerable: input is part of the SQL text
db.query("SELECT id, status, total FROM orders WHERE customer_id = 1 AND status = '" + input + "'");
// safe: the text has a placeholder, the value travels on its own
db.query("SELECT id, status, total FROM orders WHERE customer_id = 1 AND status = $1", [input]);


With a bind parameter, the driver uses the extended query protocol. The Parse message carries the text with $1. The Bind message carries the value as data, separately. The value never passes through the parser, so no input can change the parse tree. Quotes in it are just characters. The extended protocol also allows only one statement per Parse message, so stacked statements such as ; DROP TABLE cannot ride along. Escaping by hand is a weaker fix. It is easy to forget one place, and it depends on the encoding and on standard_conforming_strings.


Two limits. Identifiers (table and column names, ORDER BY columns, ASC or DESC) cannot be parameters. Pick them from a fixed allowlist in the application. Inside PL/pgSQL, dynamic SQL with EXECUTE has the same risk. Use EXECUTE format('SELECT ... FROM %I WHERE status = $1', tbl) USING input: %I quotes an identifier, and USING passes values as parameters.



 
Lab: SQL injection, and the parameterized form

 
The page shows customer 1 (Ana) her orders with a status filter. Type into the filter or pick an attack. The left side joins strings, the right side sends the input as $1. The lab parses and runs the WHERE clause on the sample rows. It supports AND, OR, NOT, parentheses, =, <>, <, >, -- comments and UNION SELECT from customers.

Roles and privileges


PostgreSQL has one concept for users and groups, the role. A role with the LOGIN attribute can connect (CREATE USER is CREATE ROLE with LOGIN). A role can be a member of other roles and gets their privileges. The owner of an object can do everything with it, including DROP and ALTER. Other roles get only what is granted.


-- owner of the schema objects, used only by migrations
CREATE ROLE shop_owner NOLOGIN;
-- the application: least privilege
CREATE ROLE app LOGIN PASSWORD '...';
GRANT CONNECT ON DATABASE acmeshop TO app;
GRANT USAGE ON SCHEMA public TO app;
GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE ON orders, order_items TO app;
GRANT SELECT ON products, customers TO app;
-- tables that shop_owner creates later get the same grants
ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES FOR ROLE shop_owner IN SCHEMA public
  GRANT SELECT, INSERT, UPDATE ON TABLES TO app;


Least privilege for the app role means: not a superuser, not the owner of the tables, no DELETE if the app never deletes, no CREATE on schemas. Then an injection or a stolen password cannot drop tables or read the tables it has no grant for. ALTER DEFAULT PRIVILEGES applies only to objects created later by the named role, so run migrations as that role. PUBLIC is the group of all roles. New tables, schemas and sequences give PUBLIC nothing. Functions give PUBLIC EXECUTE by default.


The public schema change in PostgreSQL 15. Before 15, every role could CREATE objects in the public schema. An untrusted role could create a function or operator there with the same name as one another role calls and so run code as that role (CVE-2018-1058). Since 15, PUBLIC has no CREATE on public, and the schema is owned by pg_database_owner. This applies to new clusters and new databases. A database upgraded or restored from an older version keeps its old permissions, so run REVOKE CREATE ON SCHEMA public FROM PUBLIC there yourself.



Row-level security


Privileges work per table or column. Row-level security (RLS) works per row. When it is on for a table, every normal query gets the policy expression added as a condition. If RLS is on and no policy exists, the default is deny. No rows are visible and no row can be written.


-- example: acmeshop hosts several shops (tenants)
ALTER TABLE orders ADD COLUMN tenant_id int NOT NULL DEFAULT 1;
ALTER TABLE orders ENABLE ROW LEVEL SECURITY;
CREATE POLICY tenant_isolation ON orders
  USING      (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id', true)::int)
  WITH CHECK (tenant_id = current_setting('app.tenant_id', true)::int);

-- the application, in each transaction
BEGIN;
SET LOCAL app.tenant_id = '2';
SELECT * FROM orders WHERE status = 'new';   -- sees only tenant 2
COMMIT;


    

  • USING filters existing rows. It decides what SELECT returns and which rows UPDATE and DELETE can reach. A row that fails is hidden with no error.
    • 

  • WITH CHECK tests new rows from INSERT and UPDATE. A row that fails (false or NULL) makes the whole statement fail with an error. If a policy for ALL or UPDATE has no WITH CHECK, the USING expression is used for both.
    • 

  • Who bypasses it. Superusers and roles with BYPASSRLS always do. The table owner does too, unless you run ALTER TABLE orders FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY. So the app must not connect as the owner.
    • 

  • Several policies are OR-ed (permissive, the default). AS RESTRICTIVE policies are AND-ed on top.
    • 

  • The setting. current_setting('app.tenant_id', true) returns NULL when the setting is missing, so the comparison is NULL and no row passes. That fails closed. Use SET LOCAL inside the transaction. With a transaction-mode pooler (chapter 29), a plain SET stays on the server connection and the next client could get another tenant's setting.
    • 

  • Performance. The policy is a condition on every query, so tenant_id must be in the indexes, usually as the first column. The policy is checked before the query's own conditions. Only functions and operators marked LEAKPROOF may run before it. A query condition with a non-leakproof function cannot be pushed under the policy, which can block a good plan.
    • 




 
Lab: row-level security, USING and WITH CHECK

 
Pick who connects, the tenant setting and the policy. Then run a SELECT, or an INSERT for a tenant. Each row shows if it passes USING. The INSERT shows if the new row passes WITH CHECK.

 

  

 

 

 

 

 





Authentication: pg_hba.conf


Each line of pg_hba.conf has a connection type (local, host, hostssl, hostnossl), a database, a user, a client address (for host lines) and a method. The server uses the first line that matches. There is no fall-through. If that method fails, the connection fails.


# TYPE   DATABASE  USER        ADDRESS          METHOD
local    all       postgres                     peer
hostssl  acmeshop  app         198.51.100.0/24  scram-sha-256
hostssl  acmeshop  +analysts   198.51.100.0/24  oauth issuer="https://login.example.com" scope="openid"
host     all       all         0.0.0.0/0        reject


    

  • scram-sha-256 is a challenge-response method. The password never crosses the network, and the server stores only a salted verifier. password_encryption has defaulted to scram-sha-256 since PostgreSQL 14 (before it was md5). PostgreSQL 18 deprecates MD5 passwords. Setting one gives a warning, and support will be removed in a future major release.
    • 

  • peer (local socket, OS user name) and cert (client TLS certificate) need no password. trust lets anyone in with no check. Never use it on a host line.
    • 

  • oauth is new in PostgreSQL 18. The client gets a bearer token from an OAuth 2.0 authorization server, and the server checks it with a validator module named in oauth_validator_libraries. PostgreSQL does not include an authorization server. libpq needs to be built with --with-libcurl for the client flow.
    • 

  • hostssl lines accept only TLS connections, so a client cannot fall back to plain text.
    • 




TLS: what sslmode checks




sslmodeEncryptedServer identity checkedNotes


disableNoNo


allow, preferMaybeNoprefer is the libpq default, kept for backward compatibility. An attacker in the path can just refuse TLS.


requireYesNoEncrypted, but to whoever answers. A man in the middle can present any certificate. (If a root CA file exists, libpq acts as verify-ca.)


verify-caYesThe certificate is signed by a trusted CAWith a public CA, anybody with a certificate from that CA passes.


verify-fullYesCA signature and the host name matches the certificateThe only mode that proves you reached the server you named.




require stops a passive listener, but not an active attacker who sits between the app and the database. That attacker could accept the connection, read the password exchange for a weak method or the query data, and pass everything on. Use verify-full with sslrootcert pointing to your CA. (SCRAM with channel binding, channel_binding=require, also detects a man in the middle, because the proof includes the server's certificate.)



SECURITY DEFINER functions and search_path


A normal function runs with the privileges of the caller. A SECURITY DEFINER function runs with the privileges of its owner. That is useful. An app role with no access to customers.email can call a function that returns a masked email. It is also a way up for an attacker. Unqualified names in the function are resolved with the caller's search_path. If the caller can create objects in a schema that comes earlier in the path, the caller can create a table or function there with the same name, and the owner's function uses it. Every role can create temporary tables, and the temporary schema pg_temp is searched first for tables unless the path lists it somewhere else.


CREATE FUNCTION masked_email(cid bigint) RETURNS text
LANGUAGE sql STABLE SECURITY DEFINER
SET search_path = public, pg_temp          -- trusted schemas, then pg_temp last
AS $$ SELECT left(email, 2) || '***' FROM public.customers WHERE id = cid $$;
REVOKE ALL ON FUNCTION masked_email(bigint) FROM PUBLIC;
GRANT EXECUTE ON FUNCTION masked_email(bigint) TO app;


Always set search_path on the function, with pg_temp last, and qualify names with the schema. Revoke the default EXECUTE from PUBLIC in the same transaction that creates the function.
